## "Sacrament Book I" Sac. Bk. I

Jonathan Edwards"Sacrament Book 1"

"Sacrament Book 1"

The only extant notebook from a series Edwards kept in preparing *An Humble Inquiry*, on the communion controversy, this was originally Bk. II, re-assembled and re-numbered as Bk. I. At one point in this manuscript, Edwards refers to "Bk. IV." With the exception of a few scattered entries, the entire book is struck through with use lines. This notebook provides a good example of Edwards’ process of working towards a finished treatise.

*****

The manuscript (BRBL, f. 1247) is eighteen leaves, with the first and last leaves glued to insides of the front and back covers, which is constructed from a ream wrapper and inscribed "Sac. B. II I." Stitch marks show that the notebook was disbound at some point by Edwards and reconstructed with sheets from other books or with blank sheets tipped in, during which he renumbered several of the existing pages: pp. 2-3 were originally numbered pp. 20-21, pp. 3-4 were originally pp. 68-69, and the last page was originally p. 70. The leaves that would have comprised pp. 8-9, 14-19, 26-27, and 36-37 are missing, most likely removed by Edwards.

Sacrament Book I

(** Bk. I, p. 5, col. 2.)[^001-note7] That in Rom. 11, of the natural branch’s being broken off from the olive tree and the gentile being grafted in, indeed proves that the gentiles were admitted to be the people of God, as the Jews. But it don’t prove that the gentiles were the people of God in such a sense as to imply more in it than the sense in which the Jews were the people of God. The gentiles being taken in their stead proves that they had in substance the same privileges, but not [that] they had no more privileges. The Jews might be rejected and the gentiles admitted in their stead in a great many respects, and yet that will not prove that they were not more than merely admitted in their stead, and that the conditions and requisites of their being the people of God outwardly might be of an higher nature, as well as the privileges they have by being the people of God of an higher nature, and their nearness to God greater, etc.

A state of religious privileges--hearing the word, and of living in the enjoyment of a religious education and the ministry of the word, is sometimes called a being in the temple or house of God, and kingdom of God, etc., and these are said to be called.

Ps. 26:4-5, "I have not sat with vain persons, nor will I go with dissemblers. I have hated the congregation of evildoers; nor will I sit with the wicked." Our churches, if the major part of them are such as are not visibly gracious persons, will be congregations of evildoers such as the Psalmist hated and would not sit with. A very great part of them will inevitably be such as live in known sin. INCONVENIENCES.

[. . .]

INFANT BAPTISM. God trusting in his professing people, accepting their infants to his church, confiding in it that they will command their children and their household after them, and that if they do their duty this way their children will also fear and serve him: but if they don’t so much as pretend to love God more than other things, there is no reason for such a trust. See what Mr. Stoddard says about natural men’s education of their Children.[^001-note8] see Bk. IV, p. 83.[^001-note9]

The design of a public PROFESSION of Christ is to declare of which side one is, whether on Christ’s side or on the side of his rivals--the world, men’s own righteousness, etc.--to declare which they choose, which they prefer, which side their hearts are [on]. If this ben’t done [in] a public profession, nothing is done worth the doing; no honor is put upon Christ, Christ is not professed, his religion is not professed, men do not profess to be his disciples and followers.

There is no other being of his side but being so in heart, for Christ’s kingdom is not of this world.

The PRIESTS are expressly forbidden to admit any into God’s sanctuary that are UNCIRCUMCISED in heart. Ezek. 44:6-7, "And thou shalt say unto the rebellious, even to the house of Israel, O ye house of Israel, let it suffice you of all your abominations, in that ye have brought into my sanctuary strangers, uncircumcised in heart, and uncircumcised in flesh, to be in my sanctuary, to pollute it, even my house, when ye offer my bread." V. 9, "Thus saith the Lord God; No stranger, uncircumcised in heart, nor uncircumcised in flesh, shall enter into my sanctuary, of any stranger that is among the children of Israel." The bread there spoken of represents the same things as in the Christian sacrament.

Then they might sit down together with union of heart to one another, as brethren in Christ and of the same family, which can’t be without [a profession]. The nature and design of the ordinance requires this. ’Tis a visible testimony of this union, and not only of such a benevoluence as we have to mankind in general.

"If thou shalt CONFESS with thy mouth the Lord Jesus, and believe with thine heart, . . . thou shalt be saved" [Rom. 10:9].

’Tis very much like that, "He that believeth and is baptized shall be saved" [Mark 16:16].

Texts where the phrase "ALL-HEART" are used: see Concordance.

Texts where it most evidently signifies a gracious sincerity: I Sam. 12:20, 24; Deut. 13:3, Deut. 30:6; Matt. 22:37; Deut. 6:5 and 10:12; Mark 12:30; Luke 10:27; II Chron. 31:21; Joel 2:12-13; I Kgs. 8:48; II Kgs. 23:25; I Kgs. 2:4, I Kgs. 8:23, ch. 14:8; II Kgs. 10:31, II Kgs. 23:3; II Chron. 22:9; Ps. 86:12; Prov. 3:5, "Trust in the Lord with all thine heart," equivalent to "believe with all thine heart."

So the parallel phrases of "whole heart" and "perfect heart."

It was not only an assent of the understanding they were wont to look at in order to baptism, but the consent of the will. Acts 2:41, "Then they that gladly recieved the word were baptized," i.e. there was an appearance of it in all. There was an appearance of the same thing as was in Zaccheus, that is expressed by his receiving him joyfully and there was an appearance of "singleness of heart," v. 46. (* p. 28, col. 1.)[^001-note10]

JOHN THE BAPTIST, his driving the Pharisees and Saducees away with that, that they should bring forth fruits "meet for repentance" [Matt. 3:8], is a proof that they came with a profession of repentance, and without doubt it was such a repentance as he had preached, viz. repentance for the remission [of sins]; and therefore his baptism is called a baptism of such a repentance [Mark 1:4], which I know not how we shall understand otherwise than that it was a seal of such a professed repentance.

Further, it may be observed that the ancient, dark, typical dispensation was continued under John the Baptist, and some things in his baptism were regulated by its laws, as baptizing when there was much water; and therefore ’tis not so clear and sure arguing from hence, as was observed concerning the Passover. (** Bk. III, p. 4.)[^001-note11]

ADVANTAGES. The manner all over the country is that when they[^001-note12] are married and want to have children baptized, then to come and own the covenant.

[That] HOLINESS was required in partaking of the PASSOVER as well as sacrifices, is confirmed by Ezra 6:21 and, more plainly, II Chron. 30:19, 22.

Before I speak of the ADVANTAGES, declare briefly what I think is the right way of managing in churches. METHOD.

The FOOLISH VIRGINS were, to all appearance, the children of the bride-chamber, and they are saints. They were such, as to appearance, had accepted of the invitation to the wedding, which represents the invitation of the gospel, wherein the Bridegroom and bride say, "Come." The foolish virgins made the very same profession with the wise. Their lamps were alike. They had the same hope of interest in the Bridegroom, and of meeting him, and entering in with him to the marriage. How wild and ungrounded would the assertion be, if any should assert that the foolish virgins never (Next p.)[^001-note13] [. . .]

PASSOVER. If we can’t reconcile the institutions of the New Testament dispensation, and find much difficulty to reconcile them, or give all the reason of the difference no argument from hence, is at all to the purpose with regard to the constitution of the new dispensation that is plainly and clearly revealed in the New Testament, which is the special rule of the new dispensation, and is the law that is the very foundation of it. [. . .] may be raised about the grounds of this impossibility, and the difference between that and the impossibility which arises from a defect in man’s nature. Yet ’tis impossible, as impossible as ’tis for an "Ethiopian to change his skin, or the leopard his spots" [Jer. 13:23]. Instance in Judas, who was covetous, a thief, proud and malicious.

*[^001-note15] If gracious sincerity might as properly be required as moral sincerity, then those things that are or ought to be connected with it may as properly be required. And if the former may as properly be required instantly as the latter, then may its concomitants or consequents be as properly be instantly required.

COVENANT. Sealing an instrument is to confirm it as our own act and deed. To seal a covenant among men is to declare and confirm our consent and part in the covenant .

COVENANT. There are two parties in the covenant; both professed[ly] enter into covenant. Christ on his part, in his profession, professes his heart, the sincerity of his heart, in a full consent to his part; and so doubtless does the other party. The bride in covenanting does as much profess her heart as that Bridegroom; when God avouched, he professed the sincerity of his heart, the full Consent of his heart.

COVENANT. What reason can be assigned why an adult person’s coming from among the heathen and offering himself to baptism, is not as significant and sufficient a PROFESSION of Christianity, without speaking any words, as one’s coming out of infancy, his coming and offering himself as a communicant at the Lord’s Table, without speaking any words?

COVENANT, PROFESSION. ’Tis reasonable that there should be something to make a person’s Christianity visible and to exhibit to the eye of the church first, before he be received amongst God’s people to communion with them in their special privilege: for ’tis visibility that is the ground of such reception, and reception precedes actual joint participation. If a family receives a new member to a joint participation of family on the visibility of certain qualifications, a visibility of those qualifications in order of nature precedes that participation, because ’tis the very ground of the society’s acting in and receiving them to it. Therefore, profession should not only be made at the Lord’s Table, but should precede admission to the Lord’s Table.

ISRAEL’S COVENANTING. When Moses said, "the Lord hath not given you [. . .] eyes to see" [Deut. 29:4], he treated the people as Christ did his disciples when he said, "O fools, and slow of heart to believe," Luke 24:25; "If ye had faith as a grain of mustard seed" [Luke 17:6]; "Thou savourest not the things that be of God, but the things that be of men" [Matt. 16:23]; "O ye of little faith."[^001-note16] "Have I been so long with you, and hast thou not known me?" [John 14:9]. Mark. 4:40, "How is it that ye have no faith?"

COVENANTING, MORAL SINCERITY. That which has a direct tendency to do men hurt, and greatly to harden men in sin, it is not but for ministers to lead them to: but public covenanting and solemn vowing to give themselves up to God, sell all, etc., when they pretend not to have such a heart, no sincere design to do it, no root in them, no oil in their vessels, to love the world better than God, to choose it before God, to be disposed to serve it more, and to make themselves and the world their highest end, has a most direct tendency to do men hurt, desperately to harden their hearts; for no other is to be expected than living in the violation: yea, they do at the very same instant violate it and contradict it, and that willfully. They wholly violate it, act entirely contrary to it and practically disannul all that they say. They are guilty of perjury. When they promise that they will give up themselves and their whole lives, that implies [. . .][^001-note17]

[. . .] doings of Christ to introduce ’em than the doings of the devil, it being the proper work of the kind master to take in raw[^001-note18] children into his school, and the proper business of the compassionate physician and healer of men to take in sick people into the infirmary, and not the doings of the cruel enemy and grand destroyer of man.

CERTAIN RULE to judge who are converted. The nature of the case does not require [it]. All that men have to do is to treat ’em as saints, admit ’em to their communion as such; the rest is left to their own conscience. But a judgment of charity only is sufficient in this case and thousands of other cases, to oblige us to treat ’em[^001-note19] as worthy of our Respect, our kindness, our trust, and all privileges we are concerned in bestowing or admitting men to.

ARGUMENT. What common sense teaches all mankind to expect [or] require in admission of members of all societies, especially societies formed and established for very important [reasons]: viz. to admit none but such as they have some reason to think are hearty friends of the society, and the design and ends of it. Now ungodly men are not hearty friends to the interests and designs of this society, but enemies thereto. They are enemies to the head of the society: they are enemies to his government, to his dominion, his glory; enemies to his commands, enemies to [his] holiness, to his mediatorial kingdom and glory, to the great designs of his redemption, to his main benefits, to the things revealed in his main and most essential doctrines.

This is a reasonable account of Christ’s instituting a church in the world: that[^001-note20] he came into this fallen world, that Satan had gained over to his interest, to gather out of a number from the world, to redeem a number from the world from amongst men; that he should save them from their sins, from the wickedness of the world, and to make them wise and good, to redeem to himself a peculiar people zealous of good works; that he should appoint that these wise and good persons that come over to his side should be a society, by them united to promote their common interests in the world. This doubtless [is] the nature and design of the church of Christ. Would it have been prudent, in the forming of those societies or associations that were formed in England in the time of the late rebellion, in defense of their rightful sovereign, to have admitted any but such as professed and appeared to be loyal subjects? And if the kingdom of Scotland or Ireland had rebelled, joined the Pretender in opposition to their King George, and he had had gone thither in person and called together out of this rebel kingdom a company to join him and unite to him and one to another in a firm covenant, in order to overthrow the power and interest of the Pretender, and restore his interest there, would it have been prudence to admit such as had no visibility of being his true friends, or otherwise than his enemies, and friends to the Pretender?

OBJECTION. "Of Zion shall it be said, This and that man were born in her" [Ps. 87:5].

*Ans*. In the original it is, "Unto Zion it shall be said, This and [that] man is born in her." The prefix *lamed* signifies "to," or the dative case, but it never signifies *de*, or "concerning."

"To Zion it shall be said," agreeable to the words, v. 4, "I will make mention of Rahab and Babylon to them that know me."

And if it be interpreted, "Of Zion it shall be said, This and that man was born in her," the meaning can be no other than that they were born into her, of parents that belonged to her, and by means [not] used in hell, not that those that were born were inhabitants of Zion before they were born. Their Father is in Zion; Zion is their mother. Ministers, that are their spiritual parents, are in Zion; the means used are in Zion, and the [new] birth brings ’em into Zion, to be inhabitants or citizens of that city. There they first appear when they are brought into the new world by the new birth; and therefore when Zion is figuratively represented as a city, they are fitly spoken of as born in Zion.

Zion has them as it were in her womb before they are born, and travails in birth with them. Ministers and godly friends travail in birth with them who are inhabitants of Zion.

ARGUMENT. I Cor. 10: 17, compared with ch. 12:12 to the end and Rom. 12:5, and other parallel places.

ANSWER to an objection.] No argument can be good that is as much against the scheme it would defend and support, as that which it would overthrow.

TARES AND WHEAT. If Christ has in great compassion to us created a hospital for curing the sick, and in compassion to the sick commanded ’em to be brought in order to be healed, shall it be said when any come and see this, "Lord, how comes thy hospital to have sick people in [it]?" And shall Christ make answer, "An enemy hath done this?"

* Last p. but one] PRIESTHOOD BY GENERATION. All that is evident by it is these two things:

(1) That generation, or a descent from priests, was one requisite qualification. No one pretends that there was no other; no one pretends that there were no qualifications of a moral and religious nature requisite beside that natural qualification of natural generation,[^001-note26] and that notwithstanding the law of Moses requiring all of that family {to be priests}. Yet some that had their natural qualification, had no right for want of the other moral and religious qualification; and if this be allowed, the argument is given up, unless the question be begged. For if there be any argument in the case, the argument is this: You bring in more qualification as requisite than the law of Moses required, for the law of Moses required only natural generation; or thus: Holiness can’t be required, because that was more than was required by the law of Moses. Inasmuch as natural generation was all the qualification which the law of Moses required, either the argument must stand thus, or it must stand thus: Holiness can’t be required, because natural generation was one of the qualifications; everybody sees that there is no consequence.

Here[^001-note27] I would inquire what the argument is. Is it this: Holiness could not be required in order to a right, because natural generation was all the qualification; or is it this: Holiness can’t be required, because natural generation was one of the qualificaions. If there be any argument around it, it must be one of these two.

Let it be consider[ed] what is the assertion or position that is made the foundation of this argument. If it be declared precisely what it [is], the argument will at once vanish. It must be one of these two.

Here is something supposed concerning the priests of old which this argument is founded on, and let it be considered precisely what it is. It must be one of these two things.

They allow this is not the only qualification required. They hold that something is to be added. And what is the addition that is to be made? They say moral sincerity. And what right have they to add this? What better than I to answer, True holiness of heart? The precept that prescribes generation don’t make any more of moral sincerity than grace. ’Tis no more expressed in the rule, nor any more implied in the rule, nor any more room left for it by the rule; and therefore the rule affords more objection against the one than the other, for it meddles with one no more than the other.

The Argument must be founded on one of these two positions that it was all the Qualification or that it was one Qualification If the former tis fully as strong against their scheme as mine If the latter every body can easily see that there is no Consequence

(2) Another thing that[^001-note28] is acknowledged is that it was indeed the duty of all of that pedigree to attend; that is not denied. But there is no consequence for: for so it was the duty of all of them to love God. And so it is allowed to be the duty of all under the gospel to come to the Lord’s Supper.

1.[^001-note29] The argument is just the same as that from the Passover. A right to the priesthood came to the posterity of Aaron by generation, just in the same manner as a right to the Passover came to the posterity of Jacob by generation; and therefore, just the same answer serves equally for both.

2. The priesthood was only the administration the ordinances of the old testament; and if the same qualifications were not requisite in order to attend them as the new, then the argument falls to the ground. If the same were requisite as in the ordinances of the new, and so the same qualifications in the priests as in the new testament ministers, then it will follow that, however natural Generation was one qualification, yet much more was requisite.

3. ’Tis apparent by the 50th psalm, they had no right if they were wicked men. "What hast thou to do to declare my statutes?" [Ps. 50:6].

4. God tells the people he did not require wicked men to offer sacrifices and attend the sacraments of Jewish worship, and not only so, but forbids ’em, Is. 1; then tells ’em what was requisite in order to acceptance, viz. repentance, and such repentance as was the condition of full and perfect[^001-note30] pardon of sin.

5. It don’t follow, because every son of Aaron was required to officiate, that he might officiate without guilt, any more than that he might officiate without the priest’s garments. It would have been horrible presumption in ’em to have approached the altar without first washing themselves, and being anointed, and putting on the linen garments. And,

These priest’s garments were types of grace. And therefore, when it is prophecied in Ezek. 44:17, etc., that in the times of the new testament they should have on those garments, it must be understood of the antitype of them, i.e. the graces of the Spirit.

It is a great argument that the whole that is argued[^001-note31] from the old testament against the necessity of holiness in order to come to Christian sacrament is of no force, that by the same way of arguing, with equal force, you may prove holiness is not requisite to the work of the gospel ministry.

ARGUMENT. Their notion of moral sincerity is a nonen[t]ity.

Their notion of moral sincerity is something that falls short of true godliness, and yet is a truly having those things in the heart in what is professed. What they suppose should be professed is belief of such doctrine and keeping all God’s commands as long as they live, i.e. a willingness now to abstain from all external sins for the present, and not only so, but renounce ’em for ever. But they have no willingness for this, with no sincerity at all; they are deceived in thinking so. See Mr. Mayhew’s explication of moral sincerity.[^001-note32]

’Tis true, a natural man may have some sincerity of some sort in some things, but he has no sincerity or reality of any sort in all that they suppose should be professed. They deceive themselves if they think so. They have no universal, sincere or real willingness at all as to all external duty, in the whole compass and duration of it, and attended with the trials that are to be expected.

If the argument from PLAIN FACT in the New Testament, the precepts of Christ and the practice of the Apostle, and the plain fact in all the churches in the world in the apostolic age ben’t answered,[^001-note33] it signifies nothing, let what will be argued about the constitution of the Jewish people, their officers and ordinances. Whatever perplexing difficulties may be raised, will only serve to confirm what we are all taught: that divine things were then exhibited in a dark mystery, mysterious, dark prophecies, dark ordinances, a dark constitution, etc. It was mysterious in very many respects. The design of its ordinances are not so well [understood], even those of them that were symbolical. Those that communicated in ’em, yea, and the priests that administered them, did not understand many of the symbols they administred, the symbolical actions they performed.

OWN THE COVENANT. To own is to pretend or profess something, & ’tis to pretend to do something contrary to renouncing. And therefore ’tis to pretend to do something that they cannot do without speaking either dishonestly or unwarily, who do renounce it. How can they own it, that pretend to do no other than renounce it? To own is at least to pretend not to renounce.

BAPTISM. Positive charity alone operates to persons’ being admitted to any new positive privileges; but the baptizing of a child is a new positive privilege as much as the admission of a person to the Lord’s Supper. When an adult person that has heretofore been one of the church is received in by baptism, he is received to a new positive privilege, and positive charity is requisite. And therefore, when a child is born, in order to our doing anything whereby we positively and solemnly testify that we positively receive that child as a saint, one regenerated and whose sins are washed away in the blood of Christ, we must have positive charity for the child. But a positive charity for the child is founded on a positive charity for the parent; the parent was one whom God had a favor for as one of his beloved people, that gave up himself and [his] offspring to God, and that therefore He will bless his offspring with saving blessings for his sake.

A negative charity is sufficient for negative privileges. Not positively censuring and condemning a person is sufficient for not inflicting any positive punishment on a person as wicked, and therefore may be sufficient to suffer persons that are already in the church without admonition or excommunication.

Verbal PROFESSIONS. If persons that are baptized in infancy are admitted to the Lord’s Table without any verbal profession of godliness, or something positive exhibited to afford ground of positive charity, it utterly defeats the design which required ground of positive charity in admission of adult persons into the Christian church by baptism. The design aimed at that makes this requisite, is that ordinarily those who are united communicants in the church should have ground of positive charity one for another; but this design is as effectually defeated as if no ground of positive charity were required at first.

Use frequent RECAPITULATIONS.

APOSTLES’ Epistles. The question is whether it was an accidental thing. or a thing belonging to the nature and character of the society.

OBJECTION. We read of none that professed to believe in Christ with a common faith that was ever rejected and not received into the church.

*Ans*. 1. Nothing else in those days was ordinarily called faith but a saving faith, or an appearance of it.

[ *Ans*.] 2. It being known that they were bound to profess no other but a saving faith, and that it was the custom to accept none but on such a profession; hence none offered to make any other but what they pretended to be this, and none offered to be received into the church but on a pretense of this. In that, there was seldom occasion to reject any.

[ *Ans*.] 3. We have instance in the history of some that had a common faith and moral seriousness, and conviction of conscience as the fruit of it, that yet were not admitted to baptism, but something further waited for. Acts 2; compare Acts 13:48 and 52.

The words "believers" and "disciples" are evidently used to signify the same thing as in the beginning of the next chapter, where it signifies that faith that those that joined themselves to the Company [of] Christians had, or seemed to have; and vv. 22-23 of that chapter.

The people that did not join themselves to them had a common faith, even[^001-note34] zeal and admiration and religious affection. Acts 4:21, Acts 5:13, Acts 5:26.

4. Not being admitted among Christians as of their company, to join with them in their religious priviledges, or to bear their name, together with other circumstances, viz. the sufferings they exposed to, tended to prevent men’s long continuing to follow ’em, to hear the word preached, or to have anything to do with them.

EPISTLES. *Objection*. It might so happen through the peculiar state of the church.

*Ans*. It was not so universally. There were times when they were high in reputation, and were in peace. They were in great reputation with the multitude, when persecuted by the rulers, Acts 4:21, Acts 5:13, Acts 5:26. They had times of great rest and freedom from persecution, Acts 9:31. These were the Hebrews whom the Apostle writes to.

WEDDING GARMENT. Infidels might come into Christian congregations to hear the word preached, as appears by what the Apostle says, and by many places in the Acts. They might come to hear the word preached. But will it ever be said to them, "How camest thou in hither, in a state of sin?"

METHOD. See "Moral Agency," Bk. IV, p. 90.[^001-note35]

IX. OBJECTION. The strength of every good argument to prove any proposition not self-evident, and so needs proof, lies in some more evident proposition. And this argument, if it has any strength, lies in this proposition, that men must have have a known right to the Lord’s Supper in order to their coming regularly to the Lord’s Supper.

One objection against my opinion, is that it would occasion a very USTEADY attendance on the Lord’s Supper, because that as often as persons have prevailing doubts, they must stay away. But according to their doctrine of fitness for the Lord’s Supper, persons must not come when not fit. With their qualification, it would cause a vastly greater unsteadiness; their communicating must be as unsteady and uncertain as the moral sincerity of natural men, which is like a lamp without oil, etc.

(* P. 37.)[^001-note36] How unreasonable is it to take persons into a state for life on qualifications that, from their nature, they have reason to look upon as not being for life and but for a very short time, under a notion of a fitness for what they are admitted to. What they are admitted to is a durable privilege, and therefore nothing can be a fitness for it but a durable qualification. To suppose otherwise implies a contradiction: it supposes it a fitness far more than it is a fitness. How unreasonable for a man that is going a journey in the night to take a lamp to enlighten him through the journey that he knows is nothing but a flaming wick without oil.

It can’t be a fitness in any respect for that which he is admitted, for he is admitted to a long privilege. ’Tis a long qualification only that fits a suit, a long privilege. A short qualification can no more [. . .] (** Bk. II, p. 116.)[^001-note37]

[. . .] the king and parliament should be pleased to introduce a new constitution of things in one of the plantations by a new charter, or a new body of instituions and statutes. In those things wherein their new charter is abundantly clear and full, that is the rule, though it may be hard for us to account for some other time, or find out the reasons that might influence the wisdom of the king and parliament. And though we can’t tell how to reconcile the difference that appears to wisdom and justice, that is none of our business in order to determine what our present rights and obligations are by the charter we are under. Not that the Old Testament is out of date, but I say that the Old Testament *dispensation*[^001-note38] is out of date, and consequently the constitution that established it.

Last p., col. 2][^001-note39] [. . .] pretended to be the same sort of persons with the foolish virgins, and never were received by the wise under any such notion.

To say that persons may PROFESS Christianity, and not profess *real*[^001-note40] Christianity but only profess *visible* Christianity, is just the same absurdity as if we should say that they profess that they are professors; and if it be inquired what they profess, that they do profess, or that they make visibility visible.

## "The Result of a Council of Nine Churches Met at Northampton, June 22, 1750" Result of the Council 6-22-50

Chester WilliamsResult of the Council at Northampton, June 22, 1750

We have in manuscript and printed form the report of the council that met at Northampton on June 22, 1750, and voted Edwards’ dismissal, accompanied by the Protest of those who voted against dismission. The manuscript copy passed through Edwards’s hands, or perhaps was his personal copy of record, since he made two emendations in the Protest. He may have used this document to write the letter to Joseph Bellamy dated June 25, 1750 (Letter A116a), which includes the full text of the Result and Protest. It is interesting to note that, on the Protest, the name of the last signator, Samuel Bancroft, was rendered as "Samuel Bankrupt." Was this an unfortunate slip by the amanuensis of the protesters, or, if copied out by someone from the other side, a final satirical jab? In any case, Edwards drew a line through the mistake and re-wrote Bancroft’s name himself below.

The text was published in Boston soon after as *The Result of a Council of Nine Churches Met at Northampton, June 22, 1750. With a Protest Against the Same*. There followed a short pamphlet exchange between Robert Breck and William Hobby, with Breck issuing *An Account of the Conduct of the Council Which Dismissed the Rev. Mr. Edwards* (1750), followed by Hobby’s *A Vindication of the Protest Against the Result of the Northampton-Council* (1751), and ending with Breck’s *A Letter to the Rev. Mr. Hobby* (1751).

*****

Two folio leaves (BRBL, f. 1350). Report in hand of Chester Williams; Protest in unidentified hand, with two changes by Edwards. There are only minor differences between the MS copy and the imprint.

[^002-note1]

At a Councill of Nine Churches, viz

The Chh in EnfieldRevd: Mr: Peter Reynolds Pastr Mr: Edd. Collings Dele

SheffieldJona. Hubberd Pastr: Mr: Danl: Kellogg Delege

SuttonDavid Hall Pastr: Mr: Jona: Hale Delege

ReadingWillm: Hobby Past: Mr: Samll: Bancroft Del:

First Chh in SpringfieldRobt: Breck Past: Mr: Thos: Stebbins Del:

SunderlandJoseph Ashley Past Mr Samll Montague Del.

HatfieldTimo: Woodbridge Pastr: Olivr: Partridge Esqr Del:

First Chh in HadleyChester Williams Pastr: Enos Nash Del:

PelhamRobt: Abercrombie Pastr Mr: Math: Gray Del:

Conven’d at ye Call of ye First Chh in Northampton together with ye Elder of

of ye Chh in Cold Spring added by ye Consent of both ye Pastor & Chh of

Northampton, in Order to advise to a Remedy from ye Calamitys Arising

from ye Unsettled broken State of ye first Chh in Northampton by

Reason of a Controversy subsisting about ye Qualifications for full

Communion in ye Chh:

The Revd: Mr: Hubbard was chosen Moderator

and ye Revd: Mr: Williams Scribe

The Councill after Seeking ye divine Presence & Direction had ye Matter

in Controversy laid before them, & finding ye Sentiments of ye Pastor & Chh:

concerning ye Qualifications Necessary for full Co`munion to be diametrically Opposite

to each Other, ye Pastor Insisting upon it as Necessary to ye. Admision of Members

to full Co`munion that they should make a Profession of Sanctifying Grace, Whereas

The Brethren are of Opinion that ye. Lords Supper is a converting Ordinance, and

consequently, that Persons if they have a Competency of Knoledge & are of a blameless

Life, may be admitted to ye. Lords Table, althô they make no Such Profession – And

also finding that by Reason of this Diversity of Sentiments, ye Doors of ye Chh

have been shut for some Years so that there has been no Admissions; and not

being Able to find out any Method wherin ye Pastor & Brethren can Unite

Consistent with their Own Sentiments in Admitting Members to full Co`munion

The Councill did then according to ye Desire of ye Chh express’d in their Letters [^002-note2]

Missive proceed to consider of ye Expediency of dissolving ye Relation between

Pastor & People. & After Hearing ye: Chh: upon it & Mature Deliberation

of ye Case, ye Questions were put to ye Members of ye Councill severally ---

1. Whether it be ye Opinion of this Councill that ye Revd: Mr: Edwards persisting in his

Principles, & ye Chh: in theirs, in Opposition to his, & Insisting on a Separation, it is

Necessary that ye. Relation between Pastor & People be dissolved

Resolved in ye. Affirmative ---

2. Whether it be expedient that this Relation be immediately dissolved.

pass’d in ye Affirmative -------------

However we take Notice that Notwithstanding ye Unhappy Dispute which has Arisen

& so long Subsisted between ye. Pastor & Chh: of Northampton upon ye Point

beforemention’d that we have had no Other Objection against him but What

Relates to his Sentiments upon ye. Point Aforesd: laid before us; and Althô we have

heard of some Stories Spread Abroad Reflecting upon Mr: Edwards’s Sincerity with

Regard to ye: Change of his Sentiments abt: ye. Qualifications for full Co`munion,

Yet we have Recd. full Satisfaction that they are false & groundless. And

althô we dont all of us agree with Mr. Edwards in Our Sentiments upon ye

Point, Yet have abundant Reason to believe yt: he took much pains to get Light

in that Matter, & yt: he is uprightly following ye Dictates of his Own Conscience, and

with great Pleasure Reflect upon ye Christian Spirit & Temper he has discovered in

the unhappy Controversy subsisting among them, & think Our Selves bound to

testify Our full Charity towards him & Reco`mend him to any Chh: or People Agreeing

with him in Sentiments as a Person Eminently Qualifyd for ye Work of the

Gospell Ministry. -- ----- And we wou’d Reco`mend it to ye. Revd: Mr: Edwards

and ye first Chh in Northampton to take proper Notice of ye. heavy Frown

of divine Providence in Suffering them to be Reduc’d to such a State, as to

Render a Separation Necessary after they have lived so long & Amicably together

and been Mutual Blessings & Comforts to each Other. & now Reco`mending ye

Revd Mr. Edwards & ye Chh in Northampton to ye. Grace of God

We Subscribe

Northampton June 22d: 1750 Jona: Hubbard Moderator

in ye Name of ye. Council ----

[^002-note3]

We cannot agree to a Dismission of the Revd Mr Edwards at least

for the present for the following reasons, previous to which we Observe

That tho we presume not to infringe the right of others Consciences Yet

We beg leave to enjoy our own and being Sought to to ffor Advice

and Counsel at Northampton are constrained to Say to this Church

1: That we disapprove of the Separation of the Revd. Mr Edwards from his

People because that in the Nature of the Thing there is no Just Cause

therefor his Sentiments being as we Apprehend perfectly harmoni=

ous with the Mind of the Lord Jesus Christ and Strictly conformable

to the Practice of the Apostles and that of the Reformed Churches in

general.

2: Upon Supposition that Mr Edwards were in the wrong in the present

Controversy yet there is as we Apprehend no Proportion between

the Importance of the Controversy and that of his Dismission

3: It Appears to us no proper Essays in the way of fair reasoning

with or before the Parties to convince either of them of the Truth

or falseness of their Principles which Love to the Truth itself

and their Souls requires.

4. Because the Church or at least its Committee while they offer to us

reasons for Separating them from their Pastor yet will not

Suffer us so to enter into the Grounds of those reasons as to

offer to them that Light which the word of God Affords which we

esteem an Imposition on our Consciences and does but tend to keep

them in the Dark.

These Brethren are Some of the reasons for which we can by no

means approve a Separation at least at Present but if Such

Separation shou’d eventually come on We bear a free and chearfull

Testimony in favour of our dearly beloved Brother and your

once dearly beloved Pastor tho now esteemed your Enemy because

as we apprehend he has told you the Truth. He needs not indeed

any recommendation of ours which is more properly a Comendation

of Ourselves than of him nor need we Say much to others for that

his Praise is in most of our Churches thrô the Land yet we are

constrained to Say to the world that God has furnished him with

those ministerial Gifts and Graces by which he has hitherto

shone as a burning and shining Light and tho’ his People in

general cease to rejoice in his Light yet we hope and trust

others may rejoice in it for a long Season so wishing that the

Dear People of God in this place may take the Point in [^002-note4]

Controversy into a meek, Calm Serious and Prayerfull

Consideration and that So truth with Holiness may greatly

prevail in this place We Subscribe

Yours in the Bonds of the Gospel

Northampton June 22d 1750

David Hall

William Hobby

Edward Billing

Robert Abercrombie

Jonathan Hale

Mathew Gray

Samuel Bankrupt [^002-note5]

Samuel Bancroft [^002-note6]

[^002-note7]

The RESULT

Of a Council of Nine Churches

Met at Northampton, *June* 22. 1750.

With

A Protest against the same,

By a Number of the said Council.

aaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa[^002-note8]

At a Council of nine Churches, *viz*.

The CHURCH in

*Enfield*, Rev. Mr. *Peter Reynolds*, Past.

Mr. *Edward Collins*, Delegate.

*Sheffield*,--- *Jonathan Hubbard* Past.

Mr. *Daniel Kellogg*, Delegate.

Mr. *Jonathan Hale*, Delegate.

Mr. *Samuel Bancroft*, Delegate.

The first Church in

*Springfield*,---Robert Breck, Pastor.

Mr. *Thomas Stebbins*, Delegate.

*Sunderland*,--- *Joseph Ashley*, Pastor.

Mr. *Samuel Montague*, Delegate.

*Hatfield*, --- *Timothy Woodbridge*, Pastor.

*Oliver Partridge*, Esq; Delegate.

The first Church in

*Hadley*, --- *Chester Williams*, Pastor.

Mr. *Enos Nash*, Delegate.

Mr. *Matthew Gray*, Delegate.

Convened at the Call of the first Church in

*Northampton*, together with the Elder of

the Church in *Cold-Spring* +[^002-note9] added by the

Consent of both the Pastor and Church of [^002-note10]

*Northampton*, in Order to advise to a Remedy

from the Calamities arising from the unsettled

broken State of the first Church in *Northamp-*

*ton*, by Reason of a Controversy subsisting a-

bout the Qualifications for full Communion in

the Church.

The Rev. Mr. *Hubbard* was chosen

Moderator.

And

The Rev. Mr. *Williams*, Scribe.

The Council, after seeking the divine Presence

and Direction, had the Matter in Controversy laid

before them, and finding the Sentiments of the

Pastor and Church, concerning the Qualifica-

tions necessary for full Communion, to be dia-

metrically opposite to each other, the Pastor

insisting upon it as necessary to the Admission

of Members to full Communion, that they

should make a Profession of sanctifying Grace;

whereas the Brethren are of Opinion, that the

Lord’s Supper is a converting Ordinance, and

consequently that Persons if they have a Com-

petency of Knowledge, and are of a blameless

Life, may be admitted to the Lord’s Table,

altho’ they make no such Profession : And

also finding that by Reason of this Diversity of

Sentiments, the Doors of the Church have been

shut for some Years, so that there has been no

Admissions : And not being able to find out [^002-note11]

any Method wherein the Pastor and Brethren

can unite, consistent with their own Senti-

ments, in admitting Members to full Commu-

nion : The Council did then, according to

the Desire of the Church, express’d in their

Letters missive, proceed to consider of the

Expediency of dissolving the Relation between

Pastor and People ; and after hearing the

Church upon it, and mature Deliberation of

the Case, the Questions were put to the Mem-

bers of the Council severally ;

1. *Whether it be the Opinion of this Council,*

*that the Reverend Mr*. Edwards *persist-*

*ing in his Principles, and the Church in*

*theirs in Opposition to his, and insisting on*

*a Separation, it is necessary that the Re-*

*lation between Pastor and People be dis-*

*solved?* Resolved in the Affirmative.

2. *Whether it be expedient that this Rela-*

*tion be immediately dissolved?*

Pass’d in the Affirmative.

However, we take Notice, that notwith-

standing the unhappy Dispute which has ari-

sen and so long subsisted between the Pastor

and Church of *Northampton*, upon the Point

before-mentioned, that we have had no other

Objection against him, but what relates to his

Sentiments upon the Point aforesaid, laid be-[^002-note12]

fore us : And altho’ we have heard of some

Stories spread Abroad, Reflecting upon Mr.

*Edwards’*s Sincerity, with Regard to the

Change of his Sentiments about the Qualifica-

tions for full Comuunion; yet we have re-

ceived full Satisfaction that they are false and

groundless: And altho’ we don’t all of us agree

with Mr. *Edwards* in our Sentiments upon the

Point, yet have abundant Reason to believe,

that he took much Pains to get Light in that

Matter, and that he is uprightly following

the Dictates of his own Conscience ; and with

great Pleasure reflect upon the Christian Spi-

rit and Temper he has discovered in the un-

happy Controversy subsisting among them ;

and think our selves bound to testify our full

Charity towards him, and recommend him to

any Church or People agreeing with him in

Sentiments, as a Person eminently qualified for

the Work of the Gospel-Ministry.

And we would recommend it to the. Rev.

Mr. *Edwards* and the first Church in *North-*

*ampton* to take proper Notice of the heavy

Frown of divine Providence, in suffering them

to be reduced to such a State as to render a

Separation necessary, after they have lived so

long and amicably together, and been mutual

Blessings and Comforts to each other.

And now recommending the Reverend Mr. [^002-note13]

*Edwards*, and the Church in *Northampton*, to

the Grace of GOD,

We Subscribe,

*Jonathan Hubbard*, Moderator,

In the Name of the Council.

*Northampton, June* 22d. 1750.

A true Copy examin’d by

*Chester Williams,* Scribe.

The Protest.

WE cannot agree to a Dismission of the

Reverend Mr. *Edwards*, at least for the

present, for the following Reasons; previous

to which we observe, that tho’ we presume

not to infringe the Right of others Consci-

ences, yet we beg Leave to enjoy our own :

And being sought to for Advice in[^002-note14] Council

at *Northampton*, are constrain’d to say to the[^002-note15]

Church ; That

1. We disapprove of the Separation of the

Reverend Mr. *Edwards* from his People, be-

cause that in the Nature of the Thing there

is no just Cause therefor, his Sentiments be-

ing, as we Apprehend; perfectly harmonious

with the Mind of the Lord Jesus Christ,

and strictly conformable to the Practice of the

Apostles, and that of the Reformed Churches

in general through the World.[^002-note16]

2. On the[^002-note17] Supposition that Mr. *Edwards*

were in the wrong in the present Controversy, yet

there is, as we apprehend, no Proportion be-

tween the Importance of the Controversy, and

that of his Dismission.

3. That[^002-note18] it appears to us, that there have

been no proper Essays, in the Way of fair Rea-

soning with or before the Parties, to convince

either of them of the Truth or Falseness of

their Principles ; which Love to the Truth it

self and their Souls requires.

4. Because the Church, or at least it’s

Committee, while they offer to us Reasons for

separating them from their Pastor, yet will

not suffer us so to enter into the Grounds of

those Reasons, as to offer to them that Light

which the Word of God Affords : Which we

esteem an Imposition on our Consciences,

and doth[^002-note19] but tend to keep them in the dark.

These, Brethren, are some of the Reasons

for which we can by no Means approve a Se-

paration, at least at present: But if such Sepa-

ration should eventually come on, we bear a

free and chearful Testimony in Favour of our

dearly beloved Brother, and your once dearly be-

loved Pastor; tho’ now esteemed your Enemy,

because, as we apprehend, he has told you the

Truth. He needs not indeed any Recom-

mendation of ours ; which is more properly

a Commendation of our Selves, than of him. [^002-note20]

Nor need we say much to others, for that his

Praise is in most of our Churches thro’ the

Land : Yet we are constrained to say to the

World, that GOD has furnish’d him with

those ministerial Gifts and Graces, by which

he has hitherto shone as a burning & shining

Light : And tho’ his People in general cease

to rejoyce in his Light ; yet we hope and

trust, others may rejoyce in it for a long Sea-

son. So wishing that the dear People of God

in this Place may take the Point in Contro-

versy into a meek calm serious and prayerful

Consideration, and that so Peace with[^002-note21] Truth

and[^002-note22] Holiness, may greatly prevail in this Place,

We Subscribe,

Your’s in the Bonds of the Gospel,

*Jonathan Hale, David Hall,*

*Matthew Gray, William Hobby,*

*Samuel Bancroft. Edward Billing,*

*Robert Abercrombie*

Northampton, *June*

22*d*. 1750

*N. B.*This Copy, tho’ not attested by the Scribe, who is

at an Hundred Miles distance, is yet by a careful

comparing of it with the Original, which is now in

my Hands, attested as true, by me

Wm. Hobby.

## "Request for a Council from the Northampton Minority," [c. 1750-51] Request for Council (1751)

Timothy DwightRequest for a Council from the Minority

Undated, but doubtless dating from late 1750 or early 1751, this petition was sent by Edwards’ adherents in Northampton to the Revs. Thomas Prince and Thomas Foxcroft of Boston, Peter Clark of Salem Village, William Hobby of Reading, and David Hall of Sutton, two of which, Hobby and Hall, had been members of the June 22 Council and had voted against Edwards’ dismission. Here the topics are broached of Edwards being re-settled over the first church in Northampton, or settled over a second church, or even over an "academy" in an unspecified location.

In lieu of knowing who else the minority appealed to besides these five ministers, it may be that subsequent documents from the Northampton church, such as "Reflections on the Remonstrance," are addressed to one of them, possibly Prince. Edwards drew a map of western New England that is in Thomas Prince’s papers at the Massachusetts Historical Society and has on the verso Prince’s draft notes from the May 1751 council, which ultimately decided the issue of a second Northampton church and gave Edwards permission to pursue the post at Stockbridge. Prince may have needed the map to find his way to Northampton for a meeting even before the May 16 council.

*********

One folio leaf (BRBL, f. 1353). In hand of Timothy Dwight. Countersigned on verso, perpendicular to text along the edge, "Church Papers." Damaged along right side. Silked because MS broken into several parts along folds.

It is desired by those people of the Town of Northampton that Adhered

to the Revrd Mr Jonathan Edwards, late Pastor of Sd Town & that Still Entertain hopes of his being install’d in the work of the Gospell Ministry

over them that the Revrd: Messrs Thomas Prince Thomas Foxcroft

Ministrs of the Gospell in Boston Peter Clark of Salem Village Wm

Hobby of Reading & David Hall of Sutton would be pleased to give direction to the Sd Adherents under their Unhappy & Lamentable Cir

cumstances what method is best for them to take under their pre

sent difficulties

1 It is thôt A very difficult thing to Obtain from among the Young

candidates for the Ministry one that is not Corrupt in principles

& not tainted with Some of the Errors that So Abound in ye Land

2dly Tis feared that if Mr Edwards leaves us the Cause he has Espoused

will greatly Suffer if not intirely Sink amongst us

3dly Tis desired it may be considered whether there may not Consistent

with the Gospell Rule be an Accommodation & Mr Edwards & those

that hold to the practice of Mr Stoddard Subsist together as Pastor &

Church

4thly If this failes Tis desired it may be considered what the lowest num

ber is (That are able to Satisfie Mr Edwards as to an Honble

Support) that will render it A warrantable & Suitable things that

Mr Edwards Should be Install’d over them or among them

as their Minister

5thly For Encouragement & Inducemt to allow of A Small number to be

Sufficient at first it may be Considered that A Considerable number

in the Neighbour Towns have declared themselves to be of Mr Edwds

Oppinion & Several have declared they will Join with his Church

if A Church be gathered & there will be no Opportunity for Such

to Communicate if this failes

It may Also further be Considered that there are many of the

people of the Town that are in doubt About Mr Edwards’s P[^003-note1][^003-note2]ciples that nevertheless resolve to Attend his Ministry if he Ab[^003-note3]

mong us that now because of the great resistance are Cautious [^003-note4]

Appearing for him but undoubtedly will be reconciled to him [if]

he Abides Among us

& Lastly please to Consider what we that Adhere to him can do If [Mr]

Edwards leaves us we must think our Selves without remedy what r[^003-note5]lief can we have must we be Obliged to Attend the holy ordinances [of]

the Lords Supper with those that dont profess to know any thing about him to keep A feast of Love to Christ & one Another with those y[t]

are Enemies to him & perfectly at Enmity with him we earnestly desire that these things may be Considered & we directed [^003-note6]

what Course to take

& if All Above failes Enquire of the Aforesd Gentlemen

& Messrs Rogers’s[^003-note7] & Others About an Accadamy & put them

All in mind for Encouragement that Mr Edwards dwell

in A part of the Country where provisions are plenty &

Cheap & Avocations & diversions rare & unusual ------

[^003-note8]

It is desired by those people of the town of Northampton that adhered to the Rev. Mr. Jonathan Edwards, late pastor of said town, and that still entertain hopes of his being installed in the work of the gospel ministry over them, that the Rev. Messrs. Thomas Prince [and] Thomas Foxcroft, ministers of the gospel in Boston, Peter Clark of Salem Village, William Hobby of Reading, and David Hall of Sutton, would be pleased to give direction to the said adherents under their unhappy and lamentable circumstances, what method is best for them to take under their present difficulties.

First. It is thought a very difficult thing to obtain from among the young candidates for the ministry, one that is not corrupt in principles and not tainted with some of the errors that so abound in the land.

Secondly. ’Tis feared that if Mr. Edwards leaves us, the cause he has espoused will greatly suffer, if not entirely sink amongst us.

Thirdly. ’Tis desired it may be considered whether there may not, consistent with the gospel rule, be an accommodation, and Mr. Edwards and those that hold to the practice of Mr. Stoddard subsist together as pastor and church.

Fourthly. If this fails, ’tis desired it may be considered what the lowest number is (that are able to satisfy Mr. Edwards as to an honorable support) that will render it a warrantable and suitable thing, that Mr. Edwards should be installed over them or among them as their minister.

Fifthly. For encouragement and inducement, to allow of a small number to be sufficient at first, it may be considered that a considerable number in the neighbor towns have declared themselves to be of Mr. Edwards’ opinion, and several have declared they will join with his church if a church be gathered, and there will be no opportunity for such to communicate. If this fails, it may also further be considered that there are many of the people of the town that are in doubt about Mr. Edwards’ p[rin]ciples, that nevertheless resolve to attend his ministry if he ab[ide a]mong us, that now, because of the great resistance, are cautious [of] appearing for him, but undoubtedly will be convinced and reconciled to him [if] he abides among us.

And lastly. Please to consider what we that adhere to him can do if [Mr.] Edwards leaves us. We must think ourselves without remedy. What r[e]lief can we have? Must we be obliged to attend the holy ordinances [of] the Lord’s Supper with those that don’t profess to know anything about Him, to keep a feast of love to Christ and one another with those tha[t] are enemies to him and perfectly at enmity with him? We earnestly desire that these things may be considered and we directed what course to take; and if all above fails, inquire of the aforesaid gentlemen, and Messrs. Rogers[^003-note9] and others, about an academy, and put them all in mind for encouragement that Mr. Edwards dwell in a part of the country where provisions are plenty and cheap, and avocations and diversions rare and unusual.
