## "Reflections on the Remonstrance Sent to Mr. Clark and Mr. Edwards" Reflections on Remonstrances

Ebenezer Pomeroy, Jr."Reflections on the Remonstrance Sent to Mr. Clark and Mr. Edwards"

This is a copy of a document, drawn up for Edwards sometime in late 1751 (certainly after the council of May 15) by Ebenezer Pomeroy, Jr., clerk of the Northampton church. It contains retractions by the Northampton Church Committee from statements made in its original "Remonstrance" against Edwards and his followers upon the effort to found a second church in town. The address apparently was made to an individual (probably Rev. Thomas Prince of Boston, moderator of the council) or a council; the "Reflections" refers to the head negotiator as "Sir" and one acquainted with "those breathren by whose procuremt. we Suppose you are here," that is, Edwards’ adherents or, more specifically, Edwards and Col. Timothy Dwight, leader of the dissidents. While we do not have the original Remonstrance, we can deduce its content from this and other documents (e.g. the Petition of the Minority and the Northampton Church Committee’s Report on it). Among other things, the committee charged Dwight with threatening to block efforts to settle a new pastor of the first church if Edwards was not allowed to be installed over a second church, and it charged Edwards with complicity. The Church Committee does moderate some of its statements here, but they do not back down on the essential points of their opposition and criticism of Edwards and Dwight.

*********

The manuscript (BRBL, f. 1351) is three leaves, octavo, with the last page blank, except for a notation by Edwards.

[1. Transcript.]

The First Church in Northampton for a Considerable time time past having had & still having many things in ye situation And Circumstances wh a wise Sovereigne Holy and Just God has Seen fit to bring them into Which ought to Excite not only each Particular member thereof to a Most Solemn Serious & impartial examination of his past and Present temper and Conduct and to Sincere and deep repentance and Humiliation of & for what he Shall upon Such Examination find amiss & Sinful in Either But also the whole Chh as a Body impartially to Canvass yr publick acts & Procedings relatieing to the malancholly affr of the Di[s]mission of their late revd and worthy Pastor Mr Jona Edwards & other matters wh have Ensued and ben Consequent thereon which have ben attended which[^004-note1] much Temptation and Exceedingly Exposed, as well as Perticular persons to do that wh might be offencive to God and a Transgression of the perfect & holy Laws of Christianity And if on Such Examination they Should find any of their publick acts truely unjustifiable and Such as they might reasonable Judge provoking to God; To make Such retractions & reflections as the rules of the Gospel require; The Sd Church Under Such apprehensions having taken the letter exhibited to the Revd Mr Peter Clark[^004-note2] a member of the Eclisiastick Counsel that Sat in N H in may Last Commonly Called the Churches remonstrance Under perticular & Deliberate examination think it their duty to make the following reflections And remarks thereon; Perticularly in the first pages of Sd remonstrance the Chh used these words viz. (and Some things Unacountable in the Conduct of Mr Edwards Where they apprehend they ought not to have Used So Strong an Expression but ought rather to have expressed themselves in these or the like words ( and Some things very deficuilt for us to account for in the Conduct of Mr Edwards -------- In the third page these words are Used viz ( if we had had mediating Counsils here from generation [^004-note3] to generation they never Could have altered the Case or found out a way &c / Altho we beleive the thing to be true as exp ressed yet we apprehend we ought instead of these terms to have Used terms more mild and yt the words used manifested too great an earnestness and engagedness of Spirit ---- and a little onward in the Same page we Said ( There was no more Prospect of the latter then of the moons falling meaning by the word latter what immediately preceded viz Mr Es relinqushing Some of his before mentioned Sentiments; We own yt ye form of Speech was to ludicrous and merry and did not Savour enough of Solemnity and gravity of mind and apprehend instead thereof we ought to have Said (And there was no prospect of the latter; In the beginning of the Sixt page is a paragraph in thes words viz ( And here Sr We Cant but Observe to you Some thing we take to be very unjustifiable in the Conduct of those breathren by whose procuremt. we Suppose you are here As well as Some other things In Mr Eds his Conduct to us unacountable both Savouring very much of the reverse of that Christian meekness gentleness Sincerity and Charity wh they make So high Pretentions to. Perticularly ye Violent Efforts they & Especially the Primum Mobile[^004-note4] ( tho it is Somewhat dificuilt to Say who is the briskest Spring have made Against our obtaining Any other Minister then Mr Ed, And the Indefaticable pains Used to resetle Mr E. After a fair dism ission per fas & ne fas / We freely Acknowledge yt Severall Expressions in this parragraft are to harsh Severe & Sarcastical and Do not Comport with that gentleness Mildness & meekness wh the rules of the Gospel require; more perticularly the phrase per fas & ne fas, for although we Apprehend Some of the methods Used to resettle Mr E. were not Justifiable yet we are Sensiable yt ye terms per fas & ne fas are to strong and Carry to high a [^004-note5] Charge in them ---- And Whereas the Sd Paragraph A Charge upon the Agreived Brethren Collectively of Violent Efforts made to prevent our obtaining any other Minister yn Mr E. We acknowledge yt we have not Suffi[ci]ent reason to Conclude yt any others of them besides ye Gent. Intended by the Words primum Mobile did directly use any means to prevent our Obtaining a Minister other yn Mr E. tho we Apprehend yr Indefatigable pains to resetle him was a great Impediment in the way of our obtaining another yn Mr E. but we Confess we have not Sufficient reason to Conclud that they had that in direct View --- We therefore freely ask yr pardon of that Charge made on them without Sufficient grounds And Inasmuch as in the before mentioned 6th page the Chh Said ( How little does it Savour of Christian moderation meekness and Charity for a perticular man to Say yt we viz The first Chh & Parish in N H. Should not have any Minister til they had Settled Mr E. --- yt it Signified Nothing for us to try &c We confess yt we Apprehend we were guilty of Some Small Mistake in reciting ye words Spoken by a Certain Gent, tho we dont apprehend yt we mi[s]represented the Same. We rather think yt that part of the paragraph to wh ye above recited words belong ought to have ben as follows viz. How little does it Saviour of Christian moderation meekness & humility for a perticular man to Say ( if you (meaning ye first Chh & Parish of N H) will be willing yt we ( meaing Mr Es, adherents) Should have Mr E. Ill do what I Can to help you get another Minister as good as you Can I can get you a Minister or I Can keep you from having one – you wont have a Minister till Mr E. is Settled &c as in the remonstrance --- And Whereas onward in the sixt page after Complaining of very Wrong representations made of Some things by a Certain Gentm and Some others And giving an Instance thereof We Said yt Such representations were no Other yn down right falshoods and we Should be glad they were not if we had not reason to Judge they were not wilfully Such – we freely acknowledge that the Terms ( downright falshoods ) were too harsh & Saviored to much of Unchristian heat Severity and bitterness of Spirit And yt we ought not to have gone into any Stronger Expressions in that Peticular yn yt Such representations were very wrong -----

And whereas the Above recited Sentence Implies yt we [^004-note6] Apprehended that we had not Sufficient reason to Judge yt ye Sd Misrepresentation Complained of by us was not a wilfull falshood, We Confess yt ye Abo[v]esd Expression Manifested yt we had too great Jealousy of that perticular Brother & Some others and were too ready to Construe yr Conduct Uncharitably and yt if we had then had a proper Excercise of Charity towards them we Should have then hoped And we declare yt we do now hope yt Sd misrepresentations proceded from a Mistake in Judgement onely ------ we therefore freely ask their forgivness thereof. And wherein the Same page page we Said in these words, and there are Some things in Mr Eds Conduct So Unacountable that had Such things ben predicted of him before his Dismission nothing Could have ben more Shocking to us ------ We declare yt we are Sensible yt the language is to Strong and naturally tended to raise an Idea in the minds of the more formidable yn ye perticulars after enumerated would warrant for which we freely ask Mr Edwards his pardon, and think that the Sentence ought to have run thus ---- And there are Some perticulars in Mr E’s Conduct that are to us So Unacountable that if Such things had ben predicted of him before his dismission they would have ben Surprizing to us &c And whereas there is a little further on the 7th page an Express Averment yt When Mr E, Set out on a Journey with a Certain Gent to procure A Council he knew yt we were abt. to Send for a Candidate. We acknowledge yt that part of the Charge is in Stronger terms yn ye Evidence would warrant. We having no Other ground to Conclude that he knew it then yt as near as we Can remember it was a thing Commonly known and talked of at time, but inasmuch as we are Credibly Info rmed that Mr Edwards peremtorily declares he did not know it we are bound in Charity to beleive him And therefore declare that that Strong Expression ought not to have ben Inserted And Whereas there is an Implied Averment yt Mr Edwards end in Sending for and being active in procuring Sd Council was yt they might install him here We own that we had not Sufficient Evidence to Determine that yt was direct aim therein we therefore Humbly Ask his pardon in the above Sd perticulars for makeing Sd Charges beyound what ye ground or Evidence we had would well Support ------ And Whereas [^004-note7] in the Eight page the form of Spech We made Use of plainly declares yt Mr E, had a great Inclination to Continue at N H as a Minister Altho as we Apprehended he had reason to Judge it would be at the Expeence of the p peace & Prosperity of the greatest part of the Town Altho we Apprehend the terms we Used Cant be Interpreted Into A Charge of being So inclined to Continue at N, H as a Minister as he desired it at the Same time beleiving and being Convinced in himself yt It would be at the Expence of the peace & prosperity of ye greatest part of the Town ( as it is Construed by Some ) yet we freely own yt ye Expressions were more perremtory yn ye Evidence would warrant And that we discovered a Censoriousness that was inconsistent with ye proper excercise of that Evangellical Charity wh beleiveth all And hopeth all things --- And Since we have heard that he Labored Abundantly before the Counsel which was here Abt yt time to Exhibit to them the great deficuilties & Objections there was in the way of his resetling here We Cant but hope yt Some things Which Induceed us to beleive yt he was much Inclined to resettle here proceded from a Solicitude & great desire to know his duty ---- We therefore freely & humbly ask Mr E, to forgive our Censoriousness And rashness in this Perticular ---- ---- ---- ---- ----

And finally we Humbly & Earnestly Pray God of his great Grace & mercy in Christ Jesus to Pardon what has ben Provoking & offencive to him in the foregoing Instances wherein we have made Reflections on our Selves and make us fully Sensible of in every thing wherein we have provoked & offended him and give us true repentance Thereof ----

By a vote desired Mr Edwards prayers for this Chh And by a Vote that a Coppy of the reflections on the remonstrance be Sent to Mr Clark & Mr Edwards

A True Coppy Atest Ebenr Pomroy Junr Church Clerk [^004-note8]

Retractations of Northampton People [^004-note9]

[^004-note10]

The First Church in Northampton for a considerable time past having had and still having many things in the situation and Circumstances which a sovereign, holy and just God has seen fit to bring them into, which ought to excite not only each particular member thereof to a most solemn, serious and impartial examination of his past and present temper and conduct, and to sincere and deep repentance and humiliation of and for what he shall, upon such examination, find amiss and sinful in either; but also the whole church as a body impartially to canvass their public acts and proceedings relating to the melancholy affair of the di[s]mission of their late, reverend and worthy pastor Mr. Jonathan Edwards, and other matters which have ensued and been consequent thereon, which have been attended with[^004-note11] much temptation and exceedingly exposed the church, as well as particular persons, to do that which might be offensive to God and a transgression of the perfect and holy laws of Christianity; and if, on such examination, they should find any of their public acts truly unjustifiable and such as they might reasonably judge provoking to God, to make such retractions and reflections as the rules of the Gospel require; the said church, under such apprehensions, having taken the letter exhibited to the Rev. Mr. Peter Clark,[^004-note12] a member of the ecclesiastic council which sat in Northampton in May last, commonly Called "The Church’s Remonstrance," under particular and deliberate examination think it their duty to make the following reflections and remarks thereon:

Particularly in the first pages of said Remonstrance, the church used these words, viz. "and some things unaccountable in the conduct of Mr. Edwards"; where they apprehend they ought not to have used so strong an expression, but ought rather to have expressed themselves in these or the like words: "and some things very difficult for us to account for in the conduct of Mr. Edwards . . ."

In the third page these words are used, viz. "if we had had mediating councils here from generation to generation, they never could have altered the case or found out a way," etc. Although we believe the thing to be true as expressed, yet we apprehend we ought instead of these terms to have used terms more mild, and that the words used manifested too great an earnestness and engagedness of spirit. And a little onward in the same page we said, "There was no more prospect of the latter than of the moon’s falling," meaning by the word "latter" what immediately preceded, viz. Mr. Edwards’ relinquishing some of his before-mentioned sentiments. We own that the form of speech was too ludicrous and merry and did not savor enough of solemnity and gravity of mind, and apprehend instead thereof we ought to have said, "And there was no prospect of the latter."

In the beginning of the sixth page is a paragraph in these words, viz.

And here, sir, we can’t but observe to you something we take to be very unjustifiable in the conduct of those brethren by whose procurement we suppose you are here, as well as some other things in Mr. Edwards his conduct to us unaccountable, both savoring very much of the reverse of that Christian meekness, gentleness, sincerity and Charity which they make so high pretensions to; particularly the violent efforts they, and especially the *primum mobile* of them[^004-note13] (though it is somewhat difficult to say who is the briskest spring[)], have made against our obtaining any other minister than Mr. Edwards, and the indefatigable pains used to resettle Mr. Edwards after a fair dismission, *per fas et ne fas*.

We freely acknowledge that several expressions in this paragraph are too harsh, severe and sarcastical and do not comport with that gentleness, mildness and meekness which the rules of the gospel require, more particularly the phrase *per fas et ne fas*; for although we apprehend some of the methods used to resettle Mr. Edwards were not justifiable, yet we are sensible that the terms *per fas et ne fas* are too strong and carry too high a charge in them. And whereas the said paragraph contains a charge upon the aggrieved brethren collectively of violent efforts made to prevent our obtaining any other minister than Mr. Edwards, we acknowledge that we have not suffi[ci]ent reason to conclude that any others of them, besides the gentleman intended by the words *primum mobile*, did directly use any means to prevent our obtaining a minister other than Mr. Edwards. Though we apprehend their indefatigable pains to resettle him was a great impediment in the way of our obtaining another, but we confess we have not sufficient reason to conclude that they had that in direct view. We therefore freely ask their pardon of that charge made on them without sufficient grounds.

And inasmuch as in the before-mentioned sixth page the church said, "How little does it savor of Christian moderation, meekness and charity for a particular man to say that we--viz. the First Church and Parish in Northampton--should not have any minister till they had settled Mr. Edwards, . . . that it signified nothing for us to try," etc.: we confess that we apprehend we were guilty of some small mistake in reciting the words spoken by a certain gentleman, though we don’t apprehend that we mi[s]represented the same. We rather think that that part of the paragraph to which the above-recited words belong ought to have been as follows, viz. "How little does it savor of Christian moderation, meekness and humility for a particular man to say, ’If you’ (meaning the First Church and Parish of Northampton) ’will be willing that we’ (meaning Mr. Edwards’ adherents) ’should have Mr. Edwards, I’ll do what I can to help you get another minister as good as you can; I can get you a minister or I can keep you from having one--you won’t have a minister till Mr. Edwards is settled,’" etc., as in the Remonstrance.

And whereas onward in the sixth page, after complaining of very wrong representations made of some things by a certain gentleman and some others, and giving an instance thereof, we said that such representations were no other than downright falsehoods, and we should be glad if we had reason to judge they were not willfully such: we freely acknowledge that the terms "downright falsehoods" were too harsh and savored too much of unchristian heat, severity and bitterness of spirit, and that we ought not to have gone into any stronger expressions in that particular than that such representations were very wrong.

And whereas the above-recited sentence implies that we apprehended that we had not sufficient reason to judge that the said misrepresentation complained of by us was not a willful falsehood, we confess that the abo[v]esaid expression manifested that we had too great jealousy of that particular brother and some others, and were too ready to construe their conduct uncharitably; and that if we had then had a proper exercise of charity towards them, we should have then hoped--and we declare that we do now hope--that said misrepresentations proceeded from a mistake in judgment only. We therefore freely ask[^004-note14] their forgiveness thereof.

And where in the same page we said in these words, "and there are some things in Mr. Edwards’ conduct so unaccountable that, had such things been predicted of him before his dismission, nothing could have been more shocking to us": we declare that we are sensible that the language is too strong and naturally tended to raise an idea in the minds of the hearers more formidable than the particulars after-enumerated would warrant; for which we freely ask Mr. Edwards his pardon, and think that the sentence ought to have run thus: " . . . and there are some particulars in Mr. Edwards’ conduct that are to us so unaccountable that, if such things had been predicted of him before his dismission, they would have been surprizing to us," etc.

And whereas there is a little further on in the seventh page an express averment that, when Mr. Edwards set out on a journey with a certain gentleman to procure a council, he knew that we were about to send for a candidate: we acknowledge that that part of the charge is made in stronger terms than the evidence would warrant, we having no other ground to conclude that he knew it than that, as near as we can remember, it was a thing commonly known and talked of at [that] time; but inasmuch as we are credibly informed that Mr. Edwards peremptorily declares he did not know it, we are bound in charity to believe him: and therefore declare that that strong expression ought not to have been inserted.

And whereas there is an implied averment that Mr. Edwards’ end in sending for and being active in procuring said council was that they might install him here, we own that we had not sufficient evidence to determine that that was his direct aim therein. We therefore humbly ask his pardon in the abovesaid particulars, for making said charges beyond what the ground or evidence we had would well support.

And whereas in the eighth page the form of speech we made use of plainly declares that Mr. Edwards had a great inclination to continue at Northampton as a minister, although, as we apprehended he had reason to judge it would be at the expense of the peace and prosperity of the greatest part of the town: although we apprehend the terms we used can’t be interpreted into a charge of being so inclined to continue at Northampton as a minister as that he desired it at the same time, believing and being convinced in himself that it would be at the expense of the peace and prosperity of the greatest part of the town (as it is construed by some), yet we freely own that the expressions were more peremptory than the evidence we had would warrant, and that we discovered a censoriousness that was inconsistent with the proper exercise of that true evangelical charity which believeth all things, and hopeth all things. And since we have heard that he labored abundantly before the council which was here about that time to exhibit to them the great difficulties and objections there was in the way of his resettling here, we can’t but hope that some things which induced us to believe that he was much inclined to resettle here proceeded from a solicitude and great desire to know his duty. We therefore freely and humbly ask Mr. Edwards to forgive our censoriousness and rashness in this particular.

And finally, we humbly and earnestly pray God, of his great grace and mercy in Christ Jesus, to pardon what has been provoking and offensive to him in the foregoing instances, wherein we have made reflections on ourselves, and make us fully sensible of in everything wherein we have provoked and offended him, and give us true repentance thereof.

By a vote desired Mr. Edwards’ prayers for this church, and that a copy of the reflections on the Remonstrance be sent to Mr. Clark and Mr. Edwards.

A true copy. Attest: Ebenezer Pomeroy, Jr., Church Clerk

## "Notes for the Petition of the Northampton Minority, [c. 1751] TDwight Notes for 1751 Petition

Timothy DwightNotes for the Petition of the Northampton Minority, c. 1751

This is a leaf of notes (ANTS f. ND1.8) made by Timothy Dwight apparently during a church meeting, possibly of a disciplinary nature. Dwight would eventually use these notes to help him draft the Petition of the Northampton Minority complaining about the treatment that Edwards’ adherents received at the hands of the rest of the church following the dismissal. The notes consist of a short series of testimonies from Northampton residents about statements by Dwight regarding his determination to have Edwards settled over a second church in town.

[^005-note1]

Capt Wright About my Violent Efforts against Settling

any other Minister but Mr Edwards. Mr Burr had ma

=ny Letters & he could be Glad of an Accomodation on Mr E[^005-note2]

got all from the Coppies of Mr Clarks & Mr Princes letters wch were

for that Purpose no other Evidence heard

Ens Clark: found out by my talk yt I was fix’d for Mr Edwds

& that if the opposrs of Mr Edwds I would do wt I could to get one

& Yet [-----] none till Mr Edwds is Settled Mr Clark testifies

that it Signified nothing for em to try by he would not Swear

In this but was confidt of the truth of it

no more Evide to this perticulr

Mr Noah Clark Testifies that let NH Sink or Swim or words

fully Tantamount Mr Edwds Should be Settled in NH but

now he Cant remembr ye words nor does he know whether

it was over all or part of ye Town

Mr Nathll Clark to Sergt Jn wright that Noah told Nathll in

time of it that my words were let what will come of it

my brethren all Say yy never heard me use the Expression

At all that was in Such way that I was in Earnest

Nathll Clark Says I told Mr Pomroy the Same & that I Said then

I would not Quarrell with em but help ’em to as good a one

as I Could

[^005-note3]

Capt. Wright[^005-note4] testifies about my violent efforts against settling any other minister but Mr. Edwards. Mr. Burr[^005-note5] had many letters, and he could be glad of an accommodation on Mr. E.[^005-note6] Got all from the copies of Mr. Clark’s and Mr. Prince’s[^005-note7] letters, which were for that purpose. No other evidence heard.

Ens. Clark:[^005-note8] Found out by my talk that I was fixed for Mr. Edwards, and that if the opposers of Mr. Edwards would not have him, I would do what I could to get one, a good one, and yet none till Mr. Edwards is settled.[^005-note9] Mr. Clark testifies that I said it signified nothing for ’em to try by; he would not swear in this, but was confident of the truth of it. No more evidence to this particular.

Mr. Noah Clark[^005-note10] testifies that [I said], "Let Northampton sink or swim," or words fully tantamount, "Mr. Edwards should be settled in Northampton"; but now he can’t remember the words, nor does he know whether it was over all or part of the town.

Mr. Nathaniel Clark to Serg. John Wright: [^005-note11] That Noah told Nathaniel in [the] time of it that my words were, "Let what will come of it." My brethren all say they never heard me use the expression at all, that was in such way that I was in earnest.

Nathaniel Clark says I told Mr. Pomeroy[^005-note12] the same, and that I said then I would not quarrel with ’em, but help ’em to as good a one as I could.

[^005-note1]: **marginal.** [1. Transcription]

[^005-note2]: The phrase "on Mr E" is conjectural.

[^005-note3]: **marginal.** [2. Edited version]

[^005-note4]: Possibly Noah Wright (1699-1775).

[^005-note5]: Rev. Aaron Burr, Sr. of Elizabethtown, N.J.

[^005-note6]: The phrase "on Mr E." is conjectural.

[^005-note7]: Rev. Peter Clark of Danvers and Rev. Thomas Prince of Boston.

[^005-note8]: Possibly either Josiah Clark, Sr. (1698-1789) or Jr. (1721-1808).

[^005-note9]: That is, Dwight would help the First Church of Northampton settle a good minister, but not till after they had allowed Edwards to be settled over a second church.

[^005-note10]: 1694-1776; deacon.

[^005-note11]: Either Nathaniel Clark, Sr. (1680-1767) or Jr. (1706-1773); John Wright (1713-1795).

## Petition of the Northampton Minority, [c. 1751] 1751 N'ton Minority Petition

Timothy DwightPetition of the Northampton Minority, c. 1751

This undated document, written by Timothy Dwight sometime after May 1751 on behalf of the Northampton minority that supported Edwards, was composed to justify their absenting themselves from the Lord's Supper due to the many injurious statements made by leaders of the church. Apparently, this vindication would have been addressed to the church or one of its committees.

*****

The manuscript (ANTS f. 1750.9) was originally two folio leaves, but the bottom half of leaf two is now missing.

[1. Manuscript transcript.] We the subscribers members of the first Church in Northampton Offer what follows for our vindication in Absenting from the Co`munion at the Lords Table first The practise which this church is Obstinately & resolutely fixed in with regard to its own con :stitution & the Admission of its members --- we dont suppose that every corruption in the Constitution of A Church will Justifie A withdrawing from its Communion, Yea we Allow that A very great Corruption in this respect in many cases may & ought to be born with for A long time, rather than make an Open break & all means to be used for A reformation of the Church in Union & Communion with it but when it is the Establish'd principle of A Church that such ought to be Admitted its members & may be reciev'd as its Officers even as its pastors as make no pretence to any part of the distin guishing Essence of the religion of Jesus Christ or any true friendship to him & when they continue Ireclaimable in this practise & A declared resolution of Continueing in it when all possible means have been Used to Convince & reclaim them by Such as are most proper to Exhibit [true?] light to 'em & their reformation is quite hopeless & they have proceeded to utmost Ex[trem?] ities in their Opposition to means of conviction even to A great degree of persecution & in an publick renunciation of their Pastor for a Contrary Opinion & not only refuseing convicttion but constantly refuseing in any Just Manner to hear & consider reasons Offered. When this is the Case with a Church we think it will Justifie Such as have been members of Such a Church in withdrawing communion from them, because if they continue their communion they can have no prospect of any Other than of Always seeing the Sacred ordinances of Christ greatly prostituted & prophaned Every time they Attend them & this done by the Chh they Join with & also can have no prospect of any Other but being Obliged Always to participate of holy ordinances with Such as are not visibly their brethren, & friends to Christ. Yea without any means Used or pretended to tending to prevent its comeing to that that the greatest number & Governing part of the Church shall be such as those, naturally Exposeing the church to its own utter ruin as to those thing that are the main Ends & designs of the Christian Church. We say we think that when A Church is so infected & Corrupted in its very constitution with so fatal & incurable a disease considered considered as a body its vital & governing parts it will Justify any of Zions friends & Such as have a proper regard for the intrest of Christs Kingdom in withdrawing from Such a Church

We hereby signifie that we dont Offer what is Above in expectation of Satisfaction from the Church but as A vindication of our selves & Justification of our Opinion & that light may be Offered to the Church's consideration But 2dly: A great difficulty we have in Joining with this Church is what is contained in the Remonstrance Sent to the Revnd Mr Clark in May last which we Apprehend contains in it a great degree of unchristian Bitterness, censoriousness, Revileing, & Slandering Against Mr Edwards & those that Adhered to & particularly Against Col Dwight; therein the Chh by a publick & deliberate Act of theirs in a most Open manner reviled & Slandered Mr Edwds in Accuseing him with a Conduct to the highest degree unaccountable & Shocking particularly in Saying he contradicted his farewell Sermon & that he notwithstanding his declaration of dismission pretended to be not Only de jure but de facto pastor of the Chh from which he was dismissd & that he Set out on a Journey to procure a Council [to] Install him here, about so long before, as he himself knew the people were About to send for a Candidate, as that the news would probably spread to the Total preventing their Success therein, & in refuseing so important a place as Stockbridge where he might in All probability Settle in peace, for the Sake of Settling here in the fire of Contention with a hand full, & that he shew a great inclination Yea an Exceeding engagedness of Spirit to Settle at Northampton at the Expence of the Peace & prosperity of the greater part of the Town. These things are All denied by Mr Edwards & Several of them we know to be false Accusations

And the Church go on & Charge those of that Adhered to Mr Edwards with Schism which they themselves say is the most grievous & heavy Sin, & we Apprehend there was no Sufficient foundation at All for Such a Charge

And then with a flout & Sneer deride us & Say that in Some of our Moods & to Serve partic particular purposes, we & our Abbettors pretended there was but a little difference be[2]tween Mr Edwards's, & the Church's Opinion, & that Great & Good men had differed in the same point & Yet nevertheless had Always held Communion together. But we dont remember we ever heard of this, till it came out of Messrs Woodbridges & Williams's letter of Advice to the Church of Coldspring, & so it happens come out of the Mouths of the Church's Abbettors & not of ours. & how well this Advice Agreed with that, the Same Gentn gave this Church, in A Similar case is well known. & then mention that Some of us condemd our Selve, by partaking of the Lords Table the Sabbath immediately before the last Councill Sat here, which we don't think was so great an inconsistency, for we thôt it reasona ble, when we Expected A Council in A few days to Advise us Under our difficulties, that we should wait for their Advise, & not make A determination for our selves Just at that Juncture.

And then in page Sixth besides What has been taken notice of before as Said Against Mr Edwds there is a very large & unwarrantable liberty taken in revilings & bitter reflections Against those that Adhered to Mr Edwards, Charging of with A Conduct unaccountable, that Savoured of the reverse of a Spirit of Meekness Gentleness Sincerity & Charity which we made Such high pretensions to, & particularly, the violent Efforts we made against their Obtaining Another Minister, & Especially the Primum Mobile, in the Same breath confessing they could which was the was the first most violent mover. & our violent Efforts to hinder the getting Another Minister, Pr fas aut ne fas, & that let Northampton Sink or Swim, Mr Edwards should be settled over them Again, & of the rong representations made by Col Dwight, (as we Suppose is meant by Singleing out one in particular) in Say ing that the greatest part of the Church & parish could be content, & really chose to have Mr Edwards for their pastor, his late principle notwithstanding, & that the Church & Parish were Nosed, & forced from their true inclination, by a few particular rash & hot headed men. & then declare that they Acted on principle but lamented the necessity of it, & Still lamented it, And lookd upon it as A frown of Heaven upon 'em. & then declare that Such representations are no Other than down right falsehoods, & the Church would be glad they had reason to think they were not wilfully such and then in page 8th; there a long Harrangue Against Mr Edwards Again, for manifesting an unea siness at his being dismiss'd, & that he Shew A Strong inclination to be Settled here again in the work of the Ministry, After he had manifested such A willingness to be dismissd in his letter to Deacon Cook & otherwise, as thô he had been guilty of A great deal of Con Deceit in it. All which may be Justly & Easily Accounted, the Abominable Abuse & Injury Mr Edwards Suffered respecting his part of the Council in the pains taken to prevent yr Coming, & finally his having one in number less than the Church had, without which the Council never could have got A vote for his dismission, & now Mr Edwards see the Spirit & temper the Church & people, he had So long & so faithfully Served were of, & how resolute to get rid of him & that &c here the Pr fas et ne fas might have been mentioned with the greatest propriety thô it was wholly lost Just now

Defamation thô only of A private brother, by A private person, in private conversation, Yea in Saying things not Absolutely false, is look'd upon to be A thing that disqualifies A person for Communion, till repentance is Manifested, but we think it is A thing inexpressibly more Aggravated, for A Church as A body, in A deliberate & publick Act & vote, & in A Writeing drawn up, (which is A proper matter of record to remain to future Generations) not only thus to defame, but Slander A Minister of the Gospell & one that had long ben their pastor, & so much their Spiritual father, before Some of the most Eminent Ministers & the Elders & messengers of Some of the principal Churchs in the Land, which is further Exceedingly Aggravated, with the most peremptory, repeat ed, Obstinate, refusall of any Opportunity, for Mr Edwards's & our having any Opportunity vindicating our Character, before them to whom we were Accused, our Accusers be [3]ing face to face with us

We cant think our selves Obliged, to Join in the Ordinance of the Lords Supper, with a Church, unless there be some reason, or at least room to think that Christ will be with that Church in that Ordination that ordinance, Afording his presence & owning & blessing them in their Administration, but we think there is no room to Expect this with this Church under its present Circumstances, for we think the Church has to a very high degree disqualified themselves, by Acting as A body in the vote Above mentioned.

We have Objections Also to make against Sundry particular members of the Church, The word of God forbids Christian Churches to Tolerate Railers in their Communion & one of the Chief of the brethren of this Church did on Novr. 20th: 1749 in the Open Church Meeting, revile Mr Edwards,then our Pastor, by saying it was Apparent he regarded his own Temporal intrest, more than the good of the Church, & althô this was Openly done in the presence of great numbers, no notice was taken of it. The same Gentn: did Openly before the Church & Council that Sat here in June 1750 Abuse & vilifie the witnesses, whose evidences Mr Edwards produced to vindicate his Character. Saying there was not A word of truth in All that they had Said, Thô some of those witnesses were ministers, & persons of Eminent Character Yet the Church have never taken any notice of this

Another of the brethren of this Church viz Lt John Miller, Uttered openly & in the hearing of Several, Such revileing Expressions concerning Mr Edwards our late Pastor as these viz It would be well if his head was Seven feet under ground but he thôt Six would do his turn & Concerning Col Dwight What follows (viz) that men [. . .] they Suppose unqualified, not thrô any gross neglect or maladministration of the Chh, but only A Supposed Error in their Judgment, but we think the case to be farr Otherwise, when the Church as A body in their publick Acts, have so greatly disqualified themselves by such A manifestation of A Spirit so Contrary to that Christian love, requisite to A proper Attendance of this holy feast of love.

We are not Obliged to Attend the Lords supper under such circumstances as render it impossible to use it for the purposes for which it was instituted now tis very Evident that this holy feast was intended as A Mutual Christian Charity & friendship & so as A Testimony of the Communicants recieving & Accepting one Another as the Objects of that Charity which is due to Such. But it is impossible to give that Testimony towards those that we think live obstinately in Offences that are A Just Barr in the way of persons being recd as members in good standing.

As this is A feast of Christian friendship tis the design of the Ordinance that the communicants should not Only give Testimony of their Christian friendship to their fellow Communicants but Also that they in partaking with them should signifie their Mutually Accepting the Testimony which their fellow communicants give of their Christian friendship to them but tis impossible Solemnly to signifie or Shew A Cordial Acceptance of A pretended Testimony of Christian friendship from them that we know are at the Same time Openly Acting A part directly contrary to friendship that live in Open Alienation & bitterness manifested by reproaches revileings &c

If it be said that our withdrawing from this Church is contrary to our Covenant with this Church wherein we promised to be subject to the Government of this Church during our Abode here --- we answer that it is very unreasonable to understand that in Such an unlimited Sense that we wil continue in Communion with [. . .]

[2. Edited version.]

We the subscribers, members of the First Church in Northampton, offer what follows for our vindication in absenting from the communion at the Lord's table.

First, the practice which this church is obstinately and resolutely fixed in with regard to its own constitution and the admission of its members. We don't suppose that every corruption in the constitution of a church will justify a withdrawing from its communion; yea, we allow that a very great corruption in this respect in many cases may and ought to be borne with for a long time, rather than make an open break, and all means to be used for a reformation of the church in union and communion with it. But when it is the established principle of a church that such ought to be admitted its members, and may be received as its officers, even as its pastors, as make no pretense to any part of the distinguishing essence of the religion of Jesus Christ, or any true friendship to him; and when they continue irreclaimable in this practice and a declared resolution of continuing in it, when all possible means have been used to convince and reclaim them by such as are most proper to exhibit [true] light to 'em, and their reformation is quite hopeless, and they have proceeded to utmost ex[trem]ities in their opposition to means of conviction, even to a great degree of persecution and in a public renunciation of their pastor for a contrary opinion; and not only refusing conviction, but constantly refusing in any calm or just manner to hear and consider reasons offered: when this is the case with a church, we think it will justify such as have been members of such a church in withdrawing communion from them, because if they continue in their communion they can have no prospect of any other than of always seeing the sacred ordinances of Christ greatly prostituted and profaned every time they attend them, and this done by the church they join with; and also can have no prospect of any other but being obliged always to participate of holy ordinances with such as are not visibly their brethren, and friends to Christ--yea, without any means used or pretended to, tending to prevent its coming to that, that the greatest number and governing part of the church shall be such as those, naturally exposing the church to its own utter ruin as to those great things that are the main ends and designs of the Christian church. We say we think that when a church is so infected and corrupted in its very constitution with so fatal and incurable a disease considered [in] its vital and governing parts, it will justify any of Zion's friends and such as have a proper regard for the interest of Christ's kingdom in withdrawing from such a church.

We hereby signify that we don't offer what is above in expectation of satisfaction from the church but as a vindication of ourselves and justification of our opinion, and that light may be offered to the church's consideration. But

Secondly, a great difficulty we have in joining with this church is what is contained in the Remonstrance sent to the Rev. Mr. Clark in May last, which we apprehend contains in it a great degree of unchristian bitterness, censoriousness, reviling, and slandering against Mr. Edwards and those that adhered to him, and particularly against Col. Dwight: therein the church, by a public and deliberate act of theirs, in a most open manner reviled and slandered Mr. Edwards in accusing him with a conduct to the highest degree unaccountable and shocking, particularly in saying he contradicted his farewell sermon and that he, notwithstanding his declaration of a willingness for a dismission, pretended to be not only *de jure* but *de facto* pastor of the church from which he was dismissed; and that he set out on a journey to procure a council [to] install him here, about so long before, as he himself knew the people were about to send for a candidate, as that the news would probably spread to the total preventing their success therein, and in refusing so important a place as Stockbridge, where he might in all probability settle in peace, for the sake of settling here in the fire of contention with a handful; and that he shows a great inclination, yea, an exceeding engagedness of spirit to settle at Northampton at the expense of the peace and prosperity of the greater part of the town. These things are all denied by Mr. Edwards, and several of them we know to be false accusations.

And the church go on and charge those of [them] that adhered to Mr. Edwards with schism, which they themselves say is the most grievous and heavy sin, and we apprehend there was no sufficient foundation at all for such a charge.

And then [they] with a flout and sneer deride us, and say that in some of our moods, and to serve particular purposes, we and our abettors pretended there was but a little difference between Mr. Edwards' and the church's opinion, and that great and good men had differed in the same point and yet nevertheless had always held communion together. But we don't remember we ever heard of this, till it came out of Messrs. Woodbridge's & Williams' letter of advice to the church of Cold Spring, and so it happens to come out of the mouths of the church's abettors and not of ours. And how well this advice agreed with that, the same gentlemen gave this church, in a similar case, is well known. And then [they] mention that some of us condemned ourselves, by partaking of the Lord's table the sabbath immediately before the last council sat here; which we don't think was so great an inconsistency, for we thought it reasonable, when we expected a council in a few days to advise us under our difficulties, that we should wait for their advice, and not make a determination for ourselves just at that juncture.

And then in page [the] sixth, besides what has been taken notice of before as said against Mr. Edwards, there is a very large and unwarrantable liberty taken in revilings and bitter reflections against those that adhered to Mr. Edwards, charging of them with a conduct unaccountable, that savored of the reverse of a spirit of meekness, gentleness, sincerity and charity, which we made such high pretensions to; and particularly, the violent efforts we made against their obtaining another minister, and especially the *primum mobile*, in the same breath confessing they could not tell which was the most violent mover; and our violent efforts to hinder the getting another minister, *per fas aut ne fas*, and that let Northampton sink or swim, Mr. Edwards should be settled over them again; and of the wrong representations made by Col. Dwight (as we suppose is meant by singling out one in particular), in saying that the greatest part of the church and parish could be content, and really chose to have Mr. Edwards for their pastor, his late principle notwithstanding; and that the church and parish were nosed, and forced from their true inclination, by a few particular, rash and hot-headed men. And then [they] declare that they acted on principle, but lamented the necessity of it, and still lamented it, and looked upon it as a frown of heaven upon 'em; and then declare that such representations are no other than downright falsehoods, and the church would be glad they had reason to think they were not willfully such. And then in page [the] eighth, there [is] a long harangue against Mr. Edwards again, for manifesting an uneasiness at his being dismissed, and that he show[ed] a strong inclination to be settled here again in the work of the ministry, after he had manifested such a willingness to be dismissed in his letter to Deac. Cook and otherwise, as though he had been guilty of a great deal of deceit in it. All which may be justly and easily accounted for: the abominable abuse and injury Mr. Edwards suffered respecting his part of the council, in the pains taken to prevent their coming, and finally his having one in number less than the church had, without which the council never could have got a vote for his dismission; and now Mr. Edwards see[s] the violent spirit and temper the church and people, he had so long and so faithfully served were of, and how resolute to get rid of him, and that, etc.: here the *per fas aut ne fas* might have been mentioned with the greatest propriety, though it was wholly lost just now.

Defamation, though only of a private brother, by a private person, in private conversation, yea, in saying things not absolutely false, is looked upon to be a thing that disqualifies a person for communion, till repentance is manifested; but we think it is a thing inexpressibly more aggravated, for a church as a body, in a deliberate and public act and vote, and in a writing drawn up (which is a proper matter of record to remain to future generations), not only thus to defame, but slander a minister of the gospel, and one that had long been their own pastor, and so much their spiritual father, before some of the most eminent ministers and the elders and messengers of some of the principal churches in the land; which is further exceedingly aggravated, with the most peremptory, repeated, obstinate refusal of any opportunity, for Mr. Edwards' and our having any opportunity vindicating our character, before them to whom we were accused, our Accusers being face to face with us.

We can't think ourselves obliged, to join in the ordinance of the Lord's Supper, with a church, unless there be some reason, or at least room to think that Christ will be with that church in that ordinance, affording his presence and owning and blessing them in their administration; but we think there is no room to expect this with this church under its present circumstances, for we think the church has to a very high degree disqualified themselves, by acting as a body in the vote above-mentioned.

We have objections also to make against sundry particular members of the church. The Word of God forbids Christian churches to tolerate railers in their communion, and one of the chief of the brethren of this church, viz. Moderator [Ebenezer] Pomeroy, Senior, did on Nov. 20th, 1749, in the open church meeting, revile Mr. Edwards, then our pastor, by saying it was apparent he regarded his own temporal interest, more than the good of the church; and although this was openly done in the presence of great numbers, no notice was taken of it.

The same gentleman did openly before the church and council that sat here in June 1750 abuse and vilify the witnesses, whose evidences Mr. Edwards produced to vindicate his character, saying there was not a word of truth in all that they had said, though some of those witnesses were ministers, and persons of eminent Character: yet the church have never taken any notice of this.

Another of the brethren of this church, viz. Lt. John Miller, uttered openly and in the hearing of several, such reviling expressions concerning Mr. Edwards our late pastor as these, viz. "It would be well if his head was seven feet underground, but he thought six would do his turn," and "Mr. Edwards was just like his old cow lowing after a good mess"; and concerning Col. Dwight what follows (viz.), that men [. . .]

[. . .] they suppose unqualified, not through any gross neglect or maladministration of the church; but only a supposed error in their judgment, but we think the case to be far otherwise, when the church as a body in their public acts, have so greatly disqualified themselves by such a manifestation of a spirit so contrary to that Christian love, requisite to a proper attendance of this holy feast of love.

We are not obliged to attend the Lord's Supper under such circumstances as render it impossible to use it for the purposes for which it was instituted. Now 'tis very evident that this holy feast was intended as a mutual Christian charity and friendship, and so as a testimony of the communicants' receiving and accepting one another as the objects of that charity which is due to such. But it is impossible to give that testimony towards those that we think live obstinately in offenses that are a just bar in the way of persons being received as members in good standing.

As this is a feast of Christian friendship, 'tis the design of the ordinance that the communicants should not only give testimony of their Christian friendship to their fellow communicants, but also that they in partaking with them should signify their mutually accepting the testimony which their fellow communicants give of their Christian friendship to them; but 'tis impossible solemnly to signify or show a cordial acceptance of a pretended testimony of Christian friendship from them that we know are at the same time openly acting a part directly contrary to friendship, that live in open alienation and bitterness manifested by reproaches, revilings, etc.

If it be said that our withdrawing from this church is contrary to our covenant with this church, wherein we promised to be subject to the government of this church during our abode here, we answer that it is very unreasonable to understand that in such an unlimited sense that we will continue in communion with [. . .]

## Northampton Church Committee on the Petition of the Minority, [c. 1751] N'ton Chh on Minority Petition

Ebenezer PomeroyNorthampton Church Committee on the Petition of the Minority

This March 5, 1752, report by the Northampton Church Committee, in the hand of Ebenezer Pomeroy, responds to the petition of the "aggrieved brethren" that explained why they absented themselves from communion. While acknowledging and apologizing for some heated statements in their Remonstrance, the Church Committee here, as in later documents such as "Reflections on the Remonstrance," defends its criticism of Edwards and his adherents.

*********

The MS (ANTS f. 1752E.7) is two folio leaves, the fourth page blank, with damage affecting the right and left margins of pages 1-2.

Here followeth some Remarks upon what those that called themselves aggrieved Brethren of the first Chh. in Northampton ---- In ye first Place they Justifie themselves, because they cant Join in Communion with those that are not santified contra=ry to ye Doctrine we were long taught by ye late[?] Stoddard, that santifying Grace was not necessary in Order to ye lawfull Attendance upon ye Lords Supper &c But this they pretty much give up, and so we shall say no more about it ----- But now ye Remonstrance itself they say contains a great Degree of unchristian Bitterness, [-] against Mr. Edwards and tho[se yt] Adhere to him, and particularly against Coll. Dwig[^007-note1] with Respect to what was said in ye Remonstrance respecting Mr. Edwards contradicting Himself; which to explain it will not bear a Dispute, that Mr. Edwards has often and again declared his Willingness to be dis=missed from this Chh & Precinct, if they continued their Aversion to Him: But how has He since his Dismission by his Preaching privately & publickly endeavoured to be resettled again over an Handfull of People? this indeed is too plain to be disputed, and altho' they go on in a long Discourse how Mr. Edwards is wrong'd in the Remonstrance which can be no Reason for 'em at all, for withdrawing Com=munion from this Chh for if Mr. Edwards be abused by this Chh. he hath his Remedy either in the Law or Gospel or both to right Himself, for these pretended aggrieved Bre=thren have no Business with it; for he is not their Pastor, neither do they belong to his Chh. for ye Matter belongs to Him and not to 'em; and wherein this Chh hath wronged Mr. Ed=wrds they stand ready to give Him any Xtian Satisfaction. They say that in ye Remonstrance, ye Chh says they are guilty of Schism. Answer. We account yt to be Schism in a Chh. when any Member of a Chh. withdraws Commu=nion from ye Chh. they belong unto, upon ye Account of any Errors in sd. Chh. which are not fundamental, that that is Schism: But indeed if yt be not Schism, tis nothing more but an Error in Judgment, which is not scandalous nor criminal

And thus they go on to say, that something came [^007-note2] from Mess. Woodbridge & Williams respecting Cold-Spring Chh. and this Chh. not well agreeing. Answer. Let 'em answer for themselves, and not this Chh for 'em. And then once and again they take Notice how Mr Edwards hath been wronged, as also Col. Dwight in our Remonstrance. In answer Unto all which we would say it seems to be ye profest Intent & Design of this Interview of ye Chh. Committee and ye aggriev'd Brethren, to make Peace, and live in U[n]ity and Brotherly Love according to the Doctrines taughht [by] our Lord Jesus Christ, and we very much question whe[=t]her that be ye Way for Peace to descend into all ye Particu=lars and send & prove: but this we would say in generall that the Remonstrance was voted by ye Chh. very sudden=ly in a Day of Temptation, and tis possible, nay and very probable some Things in it too harsh, and not well di=gested by ye Chh. and for yt we would have our Brethren pass over and forgive, according to ye Example of our blessed Lord Jesus Christ, and that is ye Way for him to shower down his Blessings upon us; and that is it we should pray for, and earnestly seek more than any outward Blessing whatsoever, and if we be at Peace among our selves that is ye Way for ye God of Peace to dwell among us: which would be such a rich Blessing yt it well becomes every true Christian to be much in Prayer to God for.

And what is said in ye Answer by ye aggrieved Bre=thren with respect to particular Persons offensive Words, particularly mention'd in Answer to yt we say yt what they alledge was said by particular Persons, and cant be Matter of Offence to 'em any more than to ye Rest of the Brethren of ye Chh. and we would refer yt Matter to ye Chh, what Reflections & Satisfactions they should give to ye Chh: for every Member of ye Chh. hath as good a Right to Satisfaction for those particular Offences as the aggriev'd Brethren have. Mind what ye great Apostle said in 2. Cor. 13. 11. Finally brethren farewell be per=fect, be of good Comfort, be of one Mind, live in Peace and the God of Love and Peace shall be with you.

And now Brethren, we are sensible tha[^007-note3] true hearted Son of Zion is seized with a Sense of [the] Judgments of God upon us, and yt they come upon us readily for our Sins, in that we have been so long as Sheep without a Sheepheerd, and now we see ye Distemper let [us be] in earnes for a Cure of ye Disease: And first would [say] negatively, tis not the Way to be healed of this Malady to be finding Fault one with another and charging the Blame [upon] others. But, secondly, let every one search his ow[n] Heart, what he hath done to provoke a holy God [^007-note4] out against us; and to be much in Prayer yt God would guide & direct us in our Duty; and we can think of no Ot=her Way than that which ye People of Nineveh took when God sent Jonah to pronounce an awfull Judgment against 'em, now the Course they took was a universal Determination of Fasting & Prayer, and to cry mightily to God, yea and to turn every one from his evil Way, and from the violence yt is in their Hands, now let us instead of saying, from ye Violence that is in our hands, say, from the Violence that is in our Words, and we then have good Reason to think yt God would be reconciled to this Chh. and Town, but most certainly tis worth while to try as the People of Nineveh did, and had Success, with only that Encouragement, Who can tell, and God heard 'em and diverted the Judgment. ---- These Reflections we have offered to our aggrieved Brethren, and are willing the Chh. should concur therein.

Northampton March 5 1752 Ebenezer Pomroy Joseph Wright Com= Jonathan Strong=mittee Noah Wright Ebenezer Hunt

[2. Edited version.]

Here followeth some remarks upon what those that called themselves aggrieved brethren of the First Church in Northampton {have offered for their vindication in absenting themselves from communion}. In the first place, they justify themselves, because they can't join in communion with those that are not santified, contrary to the doctrine we were long taught by the late[?] Stoddard, that sanctifying grace was not necessary in order to the lawful attendance upon the Lord's Supper, etc. But this they pretty much give up, and so we shall say no more about it. But now the Remonstrance itself they say contains a great degree of unchristian bitterness, against Mr. Edwards and tho[se that] adhere to him, and particularly against Col. Dwig[ht], with respect to what was said in the Remonstrance respecting Mr. Edwards contradicting himself; which to explain it will not bear a dispute, that Mr. Edwards has often and again declared his willingness to be dismissed from this church and precinct, if they continued their aversion to him: but how has he since his dismission, by his preaching privately and publicly, endeavored to be resettled again over an handful of people? This indeed is too plain to be disputed; and although they go on in a long discourse how Mr. Edwards is wronged in the Remonstrance--which can be no reason for 'em at all, for withdrawing communion from this church, for if Mr. Edwards be abused by this church, he hath his remedy either in the law or gospel, or both, to right himself--for these pretended aggrieved brethren have no business with it; for he is not their pastor, neither do they belong to his church, for the matter belongs to him and not to 'em; and wherein this church hath wronged Mr. Edwards, they stand ready to give him any Christian satisfaction.

They say that in the Remonstrance, the church says they are guilty of schism. Answer: We account that to be schism in a church, when any member of a church withdraws communion from the church they belong unto, upon the account of any errors in said church which are not fundamental, that that is schism: but indeed if that be not schism, 'tis nothing more but an error in judgment, which is not scandalous nor criminal. And thus they go on to say, that something came from Messrs. Woodbridge and Williams respecting Cold Spring church, and this church not well-agreeing. Answer: Let 'em answer for themselves, and not this church for 'em. And then once and again they take notice how Mr. Edwards hath been wronged, as also Col. Dwight, in our Remonstrance. In answer unto all which we would say, it seems to be the professed intent and design of this interview of the church committee and the aggrieved brethren, to make peace, and live in u[n]ity and brotherly love according to the doctrines taught [by] our Lord Jesus Christ; and we very much question whe[t]her that be the way for peace, to descend into all the particulars, and send and prove: but this we would say in general, that the Remonstrance was voted by the church very suddenly in a day of temptation, and 'tis possible, nay, and very probable, some things in it [are] too harsh, and not well-digested by the church; and for that we would have our brethren pass over and forgive, according to the example of our blessed Lord Jesus Christ, and that is the way for him to shower down his blessings upon us; and that is it we should pray for, and earnestly seek more than any outward blessing whatsoever; and if we be at peace among ourselves, that is the way for the God of peace to dwell among us: which would be such a rich blessing that it well becomes every true Christian to be much in prayer to God for. And what is said in the answer by the aggrieved brethren with respect to particular persons' offensive words, particularly mentioned: in answer to that, we say that what they allege was said by particular persons, and can't be matter of offense to 'em, any more than to the rest of the brethren of the church; and we would refer that matter to the church, what reflections and satisfactions they should give to the church: for every member of the church hath as good a right to satisfaction for those particular offenses as the aggrieved brethren have. Mind what the great Apostle said in II Cor. 13:11, "Finally, brethren, farewell. Be perfect, be of good comfort, be of one mind, live in peace; and the God of love and peace shall be with you."[^007-note5] And now brethren, we are sensible tha[t every] true-hearted son of Zion is seized with a sense of [the] judgments of God upon us, and that they come upon us readily for our sins, in that we have been so long as sheep without a shepherd; and now [that] we see the distemper, let [us be] in earnest for a cure of the disease. And first would [say], negatively, 'tis not the way to be healed of this malady, to be finding fault one with another and charging the blame [upon] others. But, secondly, let every one search his ow[n] heart, what he hath done to provoke a holy God [to lash] out against us; and to be much in prayer that God would guide and direct us in our duty. And we can think of no other way than that which the people of Nineveh took when God sent Jonah to pronounce an awful judgment against 'em. Now the course they took was a universal determination of fasting and prayer, and to cry mightily to God, yea, and to turn every one from his evil way, and from the violence that is in their hands; now let us, instead of saying, from the violence that is in our hands, say, from the violence that is in our words; and we then have good reason to think that God would be reconciled to this church and town. But most certainly 'tis worth our while to try as the people of Nineveh did, and had success, with only that encouragement, "Who can tell?," and God heard 'em and diverted the judgment[^007-note6]. These reflections we have offered to our aggrieved brethren, and are willing the church should concur therein. Northampton, March 5, 1752 Ebenezer Pomroy Joseph Wright Jonathan Strong Committee Noah Wright Ebenezer Hunt

[^007-note1]: **marginal.** [ht]

[^007-note2]: **marginal.** [2]

[^007-note3]: **marginal.** [t every] [3]

[^007-note4]: **marginal.** [to lash]

[^007-note5]: Possibly an intentional irony on the church committee's part: JE recited this very verse near the end of his Farewell Sermon.

[^007-note6]: See Jonah 3:9-10.

## "Narrative of Communion Controversy" (Dwight Copy) Communion Journal (Dwight copy)

Jonathan EdwardsNote to "Journal" ("Narrative of Communion Controversy")

Edwards’s original manuscript of the "Journal" or "Narrative" of the final controversy with his church does not exist, but a later copy, made by Sereno Dwight, does. Dwight’s copy, in the Andover Newton Collection (Miscellaneous Papers, f. 8), is a quarto notebook of 68 leaves, numbered pp. 1-136 by Dwight. The document was published in the Dwight ed., 1:313-99.

I have had difficulties in my mind for many [1] years[^008-note1] with regard to the admission of members into Church who made no real godliness. These gradually increased, & at length to such a degree, that I could not with an easy conscience, be active in admitting any more members in our former manner without better satis faction. In consequence of this I determined more closely to apply myself to an inquiry into the matter and search the scriptures & read & examine such books as were written to defend the admission of persons to the sacraments without a profession of saving faith. And by reading & study I found myself more strengthened in my reasons to the contrary. On which I came to this determination, that if any person should offer to come into the church without a profession Godliness, I must decline being active in his admission; which, I was sensible, would occasion much uneasiness & public noise & excitement. However I came to this conclusion that I would still continue a diligent search improving the opportunity which Divine Provi dence should give me to that end untill somebody should offer to come into the church, from time to time weighing the matter, with renewed consideration & inquiry. But withal I judged, that it would not be best wholly to conceal my difficulties untill then, lest some inconveniences should arise; and particularly I thought of this, that if some per son should offer to come into the church, whom & my principles would oblige me to reject, I should give no intimation of these my principles until then, it might be suspected that I rejected the person from personal prejudice & that my alleging scruples[^008-note2] was only to cloak my ill-will. Hence I took some opportunities [2], some years ago, freely & openly to express my opinion before several of our people; which occasioned it to be talked of among many in this town, & in other parts of the land I also designedly gave some intimations of my notions of visible christians in my work on Religious Affections; but was aware that when I came to be necessitated to act upon my principles & on this footing decline admitting any who should offer themselves to be received to the communion, this would occasion a more general noise & tumult; and therefore I determined, if I lived to have such occasion, that I would in the first, go & freely & fully declare the matter to Col. Stoddard. But it was so ordered, that no person offered to join the church for several years, & not untill after the Col’s. death.[^008-note3]

But some time the last of December (1748) a young man, was about to be married, came and offered to come into the church. I told him my opinion. He told me he hoped he could make such a profession as that I insisted on, & would take the matter into consideration. After some conversation it was agreed, that I should draw up a profession of religion that he might see when he come again. Accordingly I did so; & when he came again I showed him the profession I had drawn, but told him I should not insist upon a profession He might draw one himself in his own words; & if the [3] more essential things of true religion were contained in it I should be content. He desired time for farther consideration, & accordingly I let him have the profession I had drawn to consider of. He afterwards came again & returned the profession I had drawn, & manifested that at present he declined coming into the Church in this way, inasmuch as though he hoped he could make a profession of godliness, he did not think that he was obliged to make it in order to admission into the church. The report of this soon made great uneasiness in the town.

Some time in February 1749 I declared the matter fully to the committee of the church & proposed it to them whether they were willing that I should deliver the reasons of my opinion from the pulpit. This was strenuously opposed by several; one or two spoke in favour of my preaching on the subject; but the prevailing voice seemed to be zealously against it. Yet the necessity of the church being in some way informed of the reasons of my opinion, seemed to be allowed by all; & therefore those, who opposed my preaching proposed my printing my reasons, & doing it with all speed. And although there was no note taken, this seemed to be the general conclusion that they must be informed of my reasons from the press. Accordingly I applied myself with all diligence to prepare something for the press. After this, a young woman #[^008-note4] came to my house to join with the church, having heard of my opinion ; the town by this time being full of noise[4] talk of it & noise about it. I mentioned to her my opinion concerning the qualifications of communicants. She told me she had heard of it but hoped she could make such a profession as I required. Then upon inquiry she gave me a hopeful account of her religious experience & the operations of Divine grace upon her mind; & manifested herself ready publicly to make a profession of religion agreeably to what she had now professed in private. I then desired her to prepare for examination with respect to her doctrinal knowledge & to come to me again & I would draw up a profession agreeably to what she had expressed to me against she came again. I accordingly did so. After some time she came again & I read to her what I had drawn up. She declared herself ready to own that profession, but said [^008-note5]she was afraid by what she had heard that there would be a tumult, if she came into the church in that way & she did not desire to be the occasion of a tumult by coming into the church. I asked her if she would be willing publicly to make such a profession if the Committee of the Church would consent to it. She said she was[^008-note6].

Now I perceived so great a ferment in the town, that I was satisfied it was not best to preach upon the subject for the present and supposed it probable there would be no opportunity to be heard with any tolerable degree of calmness and attention, before what I was writing on the subject was published. I therefore prosecuted my writing with the utmost possible diligence.

About the middle of April I called the committee together [5] and informed them that as they seemed to wish at their last meeting that I should print the reasons of my opinion, so I had labored much upon the matter, & had almost prepared something for the press. And as they chose that I should print, so I now chose it also, since I had laboured so far in it & might probably say to this purpose as I am inform ed I did, that the frame of people’s minds was now such that they would be likely to hear in a great ferment if I should now preach upon the subject. But told them withal that the people ought not to proceed to vote for a separation untill they were informed of my reasons in some way or other. To this one of them replied, "No that would be unreasonable;" & nobody said any thing to the contrary; but all seemed to acquiesce in what I proposed & in waiting for my reasons from the press.

I then mentioned to them the case of the young woman aforesaid who desired to desired to come into the church, & read to them the profession of religion she had manifested herself ready to make, & asked them whether they were willing she should make such a profession publicly rather than be kept out: the case being as it was that I could not in conscience be active in admitting persons without a public profession of godliness. One or two spoke for it but others objected against it saying that for the church to consent to this was giving up the case or to that purpose. I told them that I thought that the church would

[blank][6]

nevertheless have the same advantage to insist on my re=[7] ceiving those, who could not make such a profession, & that I was then willing[^008-note7] never to make use of it as a precedent; & for their farther security, I offered them a written promise in the following words:

I the subscriber do hereby signify & declare to such as it may concern, that if my people will wait untill the book I am preparing relative to the admission of members into the church is published, I will resign my ministry over this Church, if the church desires it after they have had opportunity pretty generally to read my said book & after they have first asked advice[^008-note8] with regard to the regular steps to be taken previous to their vote: The following things also being provided, viz. That none of the brethren be admitted to vote in this affair but such as have either read my said book, or have heard from the pulpit what I have to say in defence of the Doctrine, which is the subject of it; that the Society will engage that I shall be freed from all rates; And that a regular Council do approve my thus resigning my pastoral office over this Church. Northampton, April 13th 1749. Jonathan Edwards

But still when the affair of the admission came to be put to vote there were but three out of the fifteen who voted for it.

Soon after I sent my book to the printer, urging him very much not to delay the printing. Accordingly the impression was very speedily begun[8] even before the printing of the proposals for subscription. From time to time, I renewedly urged the printer to hasten the impression, and also wrote to Mr. Foxcroft to his utmost to forward it, who accordingly did so as he informed me. Some time in the latter part of July the people grew very uneasy, supposing that the printing was needlessly delayed; & therefore they of themselves called a meeting of the members of the Church, or at least of many of them, to determine whether to wait any longer for that book. And, as I was informed, after some discourse on the matter they determined that Col. Dwight[^008-note9] who was going the next week to Boston, should make inquiries whether the book was likely to be speedily finished, & send word.

Col. Dwight, when he returned from Boston about the middle of August, brought a number of the books with him, & about twenty of them were dispersed in the town. After this there seemed to be less noise in town untill some time in October.

On the Sabbath, Oct. 15th I stayed the Church and proposed our setting apart a day for fasting & prayer; & put to the matter to vote in the following words - "That a day be set apart for solemn fasting & prayer, to pray to God that he would have mercy on this church, under its present dark & sorrowful circumstances; that he would forgive the sins of both minister & people; that he would make us to be of a right spirit, & enlighten us all that we may know what the mind & will of God is that that which is agreeable to his will & that alone [9] may be established; & that God would restore peace and prosperity to the church." This was voted by a general concurrence. Then I proposed that the services of the day should be carried on by some of the neighboring ministers as supposing that their services would be more acceptable & less liable to suspicion than mine. I particularly mentioned proposed Mr. Woodbridge of Hatfield, Mr. Williams of Hadley, and Mr. Judd of [^008-note10] they being most nearest.

There being now several persons in the town who privately made a credible profession of godliness, who were not in the church & hitherto had been kept out of it,-- The Committee of the Church having disallowed of their admission in the way of their making such a profession publicly as aforesaid; therefore I now made a proposal to the Church in the following words.

That these ministers who shall be called to assist at the fast be sought to for advice with respect to the admission of such persons as are able & willing to make a credible profession of true godliness; not that[^008-note11] minister or people should be bound by their advice to any thing contrary to their consciences; but to see if they cannot find out some way, in which these persons may be admitted consistent with a good conscience in both the Pastor and church, which may be proceeded in for the present untill our present unhappy controversies can be brought to an issue." Some objections were made against this - particularly that it was high time that the whole affair was brought to an issue with [10] regard to the admission of others as well as of those who stood ready to make a profession of godliness.
