Since this controversy has existed at Northampton, I have had occasion to converse with many gentlemen in the ministry on both sides of the question; & I find a vast difference, between those on one side & those on the other; in regard to their charity with respect to me & my conduct. Those on one side are more apt to give heed to reports, which they have heard to my disadvantage, & to be inquiring with concern into such & such parts of my conduct. They receive with hesitation & difficulty the explanations which I give, & the reasons which I offer & entertain surmises & jealousies of my design & of the motives by which I am governed. But with the ministers of the other side I find nothing of this nature.

It is very obvious that the members of this church themselves are perfectly aware of the tendency of religious opinions to bias the minds of men in this very controversy. When one of the brethren at a late church meeting, spoke in my favour [57] on one of the points now to be decided by the Council; one of the influential members, an officer in the church & one of the Church committee rose & told the Church that what that brother had said was the less to be regarded because he had manifested himself to be of my opinion with respect to the qualifications for communion. And the public acts of this people show how fully sensible they are of the strong tendency which sameness or contrariety of opinion will have[^008-note108] prejudice ministers & churches. To what other cause but such a consciousness, shall we attribute the fact, that they strive so laboriously & perseveringly to confine[^008-note109] exclusively in the ultimate decision of this controversy to judges who are now[^008-note110] on their side of the question; & that they have hired able counsel, to plead in their behalf for this very purpose. If identity or diversity of religious sentiment has no tendency to bias the mind, why all this anxiety & effort & expence & struggling to confine me to judges who differ from me & agree with themselves.

As to the neighboring ministers, I sincerely profess a very honorable esteem of them, & desire to be thankful that I have lived in peace & friendship with them; & I doubt not that they are gentlemen of too much candour to regard it as a personal reflection, when I suppose them, as well as others, liable to prejudices from this cause. I presume none of us are unwilling to own that we are the[^008-note111] subjects of the common infirmities of human nature; & doubtless we have found this the fact in so many instances that we should in some cases not think it wisdom to trust our own hearts.

This then being so evidently the case, if the decisive Council are generally of an opinion contrary to mine & the same with that of my opposers on the matter in dispute, they cannot be regarded as impartial; & of course I shall have [58] no fair chance for justice from them; & shall not, in debating & de determining the matter in controversy stand on equal ground with the other party. The point then is plain beyond all question that I ought not to be confined to such a Council.

How tender does the judgement wisdom & justice of all civilized nations teach them to be towards every one who has a deeply interesting cause depending, with regard to the impartiality of his judges. When he has any objecttions against any one proposed as a judge, how easily do they admit them, if there be the least appearance of any circumstance, tending to bias & prejudice the mind. How readily, for example are such objections admitted against any who are nominated to be of a Jury.

Local proximity, I fully admit, ought ordinarily to be regarded as a circumstance of weight in a Council who are to be judges in a religious controversy; but in no measure of equal weight with the essential qualifications of the judges themselves. And as to the qualifications of a judge, what is so essential as Impartiality ? What can be more essential in a balance, which is to determine the true weight of things, than that the scales be even ?

Thus I have given my reasons why I think the rules of equity & a regard to justice to the common rights of mankind do most evidently require that I should be allowed to go beyond the limits of this County in my choice of some of the members of the future Council. I now proceed to mention several circumstances which render it highly expedient that I should have this liberty.

This Rev. Council cannot but be sensible that it deeply concerns my reputation & my future usefulness as well as the subsistence of my family that I should have final justice done me in the Result of the future Council.[^008-note112] [59] That some of the members of that Council should come from beyond the limits of this County deeply concerns my future usefulness in another respect viz. That if I am ever employed in the work of the ministry hereafter it is not probable that it will be in this part of the Country, or any where in these western parts of N. England; and it will have a vastly greater influence as to my reputation in other parts of the country farther eastward than Hampshire, if some ministers of note who belong in those parts having had full cognizance of those affairs do recommend me.

It is the more expedient that the separation of the minister & people of Northampton, if it take place, should take place under the direction of a council having some of its members from distant places & of chief note in the country, as it will be an event of great & extensive influence on the interests of religion, & the Church of God. Northampton having been a place much heard of & extensively observed by the Church at large as to its religious concerns, & the past state of things between minister & people having been much known; the report of our separation must needs produce an extensive & great effect as great & on some accounts much greater on places than on places that are near. People at a distance have been more ignorant of our former imperfections & have been ready to look on Northampton as a kind of heaven upon earth. The result of the final Council will undoubtedly be published to the world & will be regarded with deep attention by many not only in New England but[^008-note113] the other provinces of North America as well as by some perhaps in England & Scotland. Hence surely it is best before this unhappy event of a separation shall take place that some of the wisest and ablest men in the country should have opportunity [60] to look into our affairs & give us their advice & use their wisdom if possible to prevent this calamity & that if it must take place a just report of it with its causes & circumstances may be given to the world by men whose characters are known & respected in other parts of the world. Both my reputation abroad & the interests of religion greatly require this.

It is the more reasonable that in the determination of an affair of such extensive influence I should not be confined to the limits of this particular neighborhood, because as things are constituted in this county[^008-note114] there is an[^008-note115] appeal from one Council to another, no appeal from a Presbytery of a vicinity to a Council or Synod from larger limits. But if the case goes to the vicinity that is the last resort, & they will have as much power in the case, as the general Assembly of the whole nation would have in the like case in Scotland. I beseech this Rev. Council most deliberately & impartially to consider these things & give them their due weight, as I doubt not they would govern themselves by those good rules of equity and charity, "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself," & "Do unto others, as thou wouldst that they should do unto me.[^008-note116]"

I now proceed to answer some objections. In reply to all the arguments derived from the Platform of Church Discipline, as a constitution or establishment binds[^008-note117] these churches, I would suggest the following observations

1. I know of nothing possessing the force of a rule or establishment to bind particular Churches of Christ without an express act or consent of their own, unless it be the Word of God. [61] On the principles of Protestantism, I know not by what rule, a Council, which sat ninety years ago could make a rule or establishment which could bind the present Churches without any free act of theirs, or without making it their own rule.

2. The present Church of Northampton never made that platform their rule or had the least regard to it in any one public proceeding since I have been their Pastor & I know so much of their present & past state that I may be bold to say they have never pretended to make this rule a directory in ecclesiastical matters since any one of the present members of the church was a church member. I never heard it mentioned by any of the church on any occasion until now; & I verily believe that untill a little while since the great body of the members never knew there was any such thing in existence.

3. It is inconsistent with the[^008-note118] compilers of the platform[^008-note119] itself, that it should be of the nature of a constitution or establishment obligatory on future churches, or on present Churches any farther than by their own free act[^008-note120]; for the compilers of that Platform plainly shew it to be his[^008-note121] own opinion that each particular Church has under Christ all power of discipline within itself without being bound by the determinations of other churches; & that the government of the Church is congregational & not national nor Provincial nor Classical, & therefore not subject to the decisions & constitutions of national, provincial, or classical Synods unless by their own free act.

4. The Platform itself allows expressly of departing from the vicinity when the nature of the case leads to it. [62] The words of the Platform are "There should be liberty without offence to make use of other churches as the nature of the case & the advantage of opportunity may lead thereto." I think I have plainly shown already that the nature of this case does lead to it & absolutely requires it.

As to the Vote of this Church, in the days of our fathers 35 years ago. That they would be subject to a Council of the Churches of the County until some superior Judicature were established in the province- it may sufficient here to suggest the following brief considerations

1. No persons professing Protestant principles will maintain that Christians of the present generation are bound in affairs of religion & the worship of God by the determination of their forefathers unless they have adopted the act of their forefathers & made it in some way or other their own[^008-note122] act or consent either expressed or implicit.

2. This appears still more obviously in the present case from the very different circumstances of the county in this generation & in the last. This act change in the state of things shews this act to be void unless it has been renewed since. Had their circumstances been like ours, our forefathers, we have the best reason to believe would never have formed such a determination. We have now two associations. The Churches are far more numerous & more dispersed. And as the state of things which was the ground of this act of our ancestors has ceased we must suppose the act itself to cease as to any obligation on us unless it has been renewed.[63]

3. There has been no recognition of this act by us, either explicit or implicit[^008-note123]. Certainly there has been no explicit obligation recognition. Who can point out any act or vote of us[^008-note124] by which we have recognized its binding force. Neither have we implicitly consented to it. Since I have been the pastor of this Church though we have on many occasions had the subject of Councils before us we have never in any one instance paid the least respect to this[^008-note125] act; nor do I remember that it has ever been once mentioned before the Church.

4. We have implicitly renounced it in several ways. Our proceedings have always been inconsistent with it. The vote refers to a Council of Churches; whereas we have been connected only with councils of ministers. The Vote refers to a stated Council of the Churches of the County according to Mr. Stoddards known Presbyterian principles and not to elective Councils; whereas all the Councils with which we have been connected have been elective. We have also implicitly renounced Presbyterianism, which that vote was intended to introduce & have adopted congregationalism.

5. If it had not been renounced but made our own act as much as we could make it so, it must be understood only as a general rule & could not be of force in extraordinary in which it would be contrary to reason and the rights of mankind to adhere to it; for as far as it is contrary to these it is contrary to the law of nature which is the law of God, & the law of Christ, the great head of the church.

6. In this very case the church themselves propose to renounce this act. The vote speaks of a Council of all [64] the Churches of the County, taking them as they are without election; whereas the Church have only insisted on a small part of these Churches designated by election. But[^008-note126] if all the vote is not binding, no part of it is binding. As to what I wrote 14 years ago in the controversy concerning the settlement of Mr. Breck[^008-note127] of Springfield wherein I say, - That the affairs of Religion when[^008-note128] not confined to single churches, properly belongs[^008-note129] to the neighboring churches; -- I would observe as follows

1. It would be unreasonable to understand me otherwise than I really intended viz. that this ought to be regarded as a general rule & an ordinary point of regularity. --- I suppose that the neighboring ministers think it a good general rule as well as I, & have perhaps expressed themselves to that effect, yet I have[^008-note130] reason to think there is not one of them, who thinks it a rule that will admit[^008-note131] of no dispensation. Which of the Rev. ministers here present, who knows what the state of things has been of late in Connecticut, with regard to some of the Associations there, & especially with regard to one, would think that every church & every minister within its bounds, in all ecclesiastical affairs in which they needed the help of other churches & ministers ought to be obliged in every case to make use of, & submit to the neighboring ministers & them only. Yet their Associations & Consociations have much stronger claims to be regarded as an establishment, than any thing of a like nature among us.

2. It is not merely on this occasion, when I myself am concerned & my own interest touched, that I have insisted that there ought to be exceptions in extraordinary cases from this general rule; for I insisted on this in that [65] very controversy respecting Mr. Breck. There is abundant evidence also that I expressed the same opinion long ago before the controversy between me & my people was begun. Some of the ministers here present are my witnesses, that I expressed the same opinion on occasion of the transactions of some of the associations in Connecticut. And this whole church are my witnesses to the same point: They know that I signified as much publicly in word & deed, when the Separate Society in New Haven sent to this church to assist them in their[^008-note132] Council by their pastor & a messenger. And the Church themselves did on that occasion publicly consent to a departure from this general rule without any objections made by even one individual; & accordingly a messenger was actually chosen[^008-note133] to go with me as a member of the Council at New Haven.

3. Suppose it could be[^008-note134] made to appear that in what I wrote 14 years ago in that controversy I expressed myself in universal terms - that I declared it to be my opinion that there ought to be no exception in no case whatever -- & that I could not prove that I[^008-note135] ever changed my mind untill now when it comes to be my own case; yet even in that case the question to be decided by this Council as I humbly conceive is[^008-note136] not what my opinion once was; but what is really just & right in its own nature. The judgment is the Lord’s[^008-note137], & not mine nor any other man’s. They are to decide according to the rules of reason, & the word of God, what are God’s rules; & not by what once was, or is now my opinion. If a man had a cause depending before a civil tribunal, & it could be proved that this man 14 years ago had given it as his opinion in [65a] another man’s case that such a thing was according to law; which opinion, if now adopted as a rule by the judges would operate[^008-note138]; I conceive that the judges ought not to be determined, even if he expressed himself in an unqualified manner, by what he then declared to be law in his opinion; but as they are to judge for the King & country, they must judge according to what they themselves find to be law which is the rule they are to go by.

If any shall say that it is but just that I should be paid in my own coin that I should be dealt with as I have dealt with another; I need not inform the venerable Council that the christian rule is to deal with[^008-note139] another not as he hath dealt by me or by his neighbor but as I would that he should have dealt with[^008-note140] me, & as he ought to have dealt by his neighbor. But if any should insist that this is but a proper punishment for my dealing in that manner[^008-note141] by Mr. Breck; omitting many things that might be said concerning the difference of the two cases. I would only say that my crime in that case, if there was any for which I deserved to be punished, was not doing or acting any thing whatever in opposition to Mr. Breck’s being settled by ministers from a distance. I had no hand at all in opposing those ministers, in any thing they did; for all that was done of that nature was done when I was not in New England, & when I was totally ignorant of any thing that was done untill all was over. My crime was merely defending what others had done at their request. What I wrote was at the request[^008-note142] of my honored uncle Mr. Williams of Hatfield & other ministers who had been concerned to [66] justify what they had done. So[^008-note143] if what I then wrote even supposing[^008-note144] I had expressed the opinion that there should be no exceptions to the rule, would[^008-note145] justify this Council in proceeding with me in this case on principles which otherwise would not be righteous; it must be because the Council esteem it a sufficient reason to depart from what is just & equitable in itself in their dealings with[^008-note146] to do according to an opinion which I expressed many years ago in the vindication of others. Far be it from me to entertain so low an opinion of the wisdom & justice of this Rev. Council.

Having made these observations, there is no need of my saying any thing further concerning a practical agreement of the Churches of the County to manage their ecclesiastical affairs among themselves. I would only briefly observe that what I say of this in what I wrote relative to the case of Mr. Breck, obviously had reference to the Ordination of ministers. That was a case of ordination & I do not remember that I ever knew or had heard any thing of a Council of ministers in this County on any other occasion but the ordination of ministers. But had my meaning been different, the preceding remarks are sufficient to shew that nothing could have been inferred from it which ought to govern in the present case. If there has rarely been a Council of Churches in the County in cases of controversy in particular churches; then the instances do not amount to an established rule for all cases both ordinary and extraordinary. And if the instances had been ever so numerous, yet custom can establish nothing contrary to Christ’s own rules[^008-note147] of reason & the rules of natural righteousness & equity. It is also objected that to allow ministers [67] to go out of the County for a Council, in cases like this will open a door for error which cannot be closed; & that I assert the same in what I wrote concerning the ordination at Springfield. To this objection a very brief reply will be sufficient.

1. What I assert in the communication referred to is this That to allow one party to select[^008-note148] all his own judges, will be to open a door to error -- And it is very true that when that is done there can be no security to the other party against[^008-note149] injustice or any thing else that is bad. But I never asserted that to call an impartial Council was the way in which either truth or justice could not be defended. Had I done so, I should obviously have asserted the grossest absurdity.

2. If it had so happened that half of the ministers in the county had been of my opinion on the qualifications for communion; according to this argument it would as effectually have opened the door to error to choose my part of the Council from within the County as it now will to choose it from without; & if so, it would have been in that case a good argument against my having any choice at all. And this is in effect saying that there ought to be no judges allowed in the controversy except the people themselves who constitute the parties; because, for them to have all the judges of their own choosing is one & the same thing as to be their own judges. And if it had happened that churches in the county had been almost all of my opinion, as now they are of theirs; then the argument would be just as strong for their going out of the county as now for confining me to it. Can this venerable Council lend their sanction to a rule which works such mani=[68] fest injustice?

3. This rule will no more defend truth than expose it. If a particular church is in the right & the rest of the county happens to be in the wrong, then that church in summonning a Council receives by adhering to this rule, just as much disadvantage, as they would receive advantage if the reverse were the fact. And we all know that there are as many churches, & counties, & countries which are erroneous, as orthodox -- nay many more.

4. The objectors in making this objection implicitly admit, that those who are on one side in a particular[^008-note150] controversy are not likely to be impartial & therefore not likely to do justice to the other side. Otherwise how would the cause of truth be exposed by a Council though they should all be on my side, much less by one half of them being so. And if their being all on my side will naturally bias them in favour of me & my conduct; then, by parity of reason, their being all on my side will naturally bias them in favour of them & their conduct. If this be so; it proves the necessity of a balance in the Council to bring the whole taken together to an equilibrium.

I will[^008-note151] take notice of one objection which may possibly arise in the minds of some of the Council, viz. That if I am allowed to go out of the County, on the ground that this is an extraordinary case, it will be a bad precedent & others will insist on the like liberty, & will on some pretence or other claim that their case is also extraordinary; & thus it will be difficult to maintain any rule or keep any bounds in any case. To this I would reply

1. If this be a case of such a character that righteousness[^008-note152] requires that I have this liberty, the fear of others claiming the [69] same without reason ought not to prevent justice from being now done to me. It is a very common thing because exceptions & peculiar liberties are granted in extraordinary cases to demand them in others where they ought not to be granted; but surely this is no good reason for not granting them in a case where it would plainly be the grossest injustice to refuse them. But not to enlarge on this point I observe.

2. That in this case, as it now stands, there can be no possible danger of a bad precedent in allowing me to go out of the county on the ground that justice imperiously requires it; because I have first submitted the decision of the matter to a Council of the neighboring churches, who are to determine whether this be a case which requires this liberty to be granted or not. So instead of its being a dangerous precedent, it is a precedent which will tend rather to scren the churches from all the inconveniences feared; because it is not an instance of going abroad for a Council in neglect & contempt of the churches of the neighborhood, but the churches of the neighborhood are first applied to & they themselves are made the judges whether the case is thus[^008-note153] extraordinary or not. If we could actually form as strict & firm an establishment with regard to a limitation of Councils to a neighborhood as any of us could desire; I should think no man could desire a greater strictness than this -- that no minister or church should depart from such a strict consistory even in extraordinary cases, but with the approbation & by the allowance of that consistory. If we wished to contrive a method which should effectually prevent the mischief of extraordinary cases being made precedents [70] for ordinary ones, we could not contrive one more effectual than this - That the churches of the neighborhood themselves should be the judges of those extraordinary cases. In this way the neighborhood has all the opportunity for self preservation which it can possibly desire.

I ask the pardon of the Rev. Council for being thus particular in my argument on this subject: the case being one as may easily be seen of vast consequence to me & my family. I hope notwithstanding, that every thing which has been said will have its due weight with the Council, & that since I have submitted this question so deeply interesting to myself, to judges who are all of a contrary opinion from me, in the main controversy between the two parties between whom you are to judge that I shall experience the happy effects of your steady & unshaken integrity in your righteous determination of this important point.

[blank page]

When the agents for the Church had said what they thought [71] proper in reply to the preceding remarks, I[^008-note154] presented to the Council the following considerations on the question - Whether the state of things is now[^008-note155] ripe for a Council being called to judge, whether I shall be dismissed from my pastoral office here, or not[^008-note156]?

I suppose the state of things not to be[^008-note157] ripe for the calling of a Council to decide on the question of dismission for the following reasons.

1. It is very apparent that my people have never[^008-note158] given me a proper hearing on the great question, which is the que origin of all our present debates & difficulties, & which must be the ground of[^008-note159] rejecting me from being their pastor, if ever this is[^008-note160] done in the issue of the present controversy. I say the ground of their rejecting me - for if I am removed from being their pastor, they must reject me; fir they must first vote for my being removed. Though this alone will not dissolve the relation between me & them, yet as a precedent step it is indispensably requisite. But if any rules of reason or religion are to be regarded, this cannot be done untill they have first given me a fair hearing on that point which is the cause of their demanding such a dissolution. In the solemn transaction of my ordination & settlement as their pastor I on my part took the charge of their souls; & they, as in the presence of God, solemnly committed the care of their souls to me; & thus the relation which now subsists was established between me & them by mutual covenant. Hence if ever they reject me from being their pastor, & are active in withdrawing themselves from under[^008-note161] my ministerial care it must be on one of these two accounts; either[^008-note162] because they suppose me obviously unfit to be a minister or 2. secondly Because they suppose that I fail of performing some of the essential duties of a minister;[72] such as are made essential by Christ’s appointment. The former of these reasons has not been insisted on. If they suppose that I fail in the latter respect, & that I neglect to perform some of the essential duties of a minister; & I insist on my own justification & plead that what I do is agreeable to the word & institutions of Christ, & therefore my duty as a minister of Christ; they are bound to give me a fair & full hearing before I can be rejected by them, or they released from their sacred obligations to me as my people. Especially is this true if the point on which they insist was never to be[^008-note163] settled in the Church of Christ, as to be regarded indisputable, & still more if a very great proportion of those who have been universally esteemed orthodox christians & divines have without dispute been on my side. In such a case as this what pretence can a people have for bursting the sacred bonds of their covenant with their pastor without hearing him.

If the determination of no such important public act of theirs depended on their judgment of the matter, but I merely offered to deliver the doctrine for which I insist as a part of the counsel of God; on this account alone my people would have been[^008-note164] obliged, on a disputable point to give me an impartial hearing. A minister by his office is to be the guide & instructor of his people. To this end he is to study and search the scriptures & to teach the people, not the opinions of men, of other divines or of their ancestors, but the mind of Christ. As he is set to enlighten them so it is a part of his duty is to rectify their mistakes &, if he sees them out of the way of truth or duty, to be a voice behind them saying This is the way, walk ye in it." Hence, if what he offers to exhibit to them as the mind of Christ be different from their previous apprehensions, unless it be on some point which is established in the church of God as fundamental, surely [73] they are obliged to hear him. If not, there is an end at once to all the use & benefit of teachers in the Church in these respects as the means of increasing its light & knowledge, & of reclaiming it from mistakes & errors. This would be in effect to establish not the word of Christ, but the opinion of the last generation in each town & church, as an immutable rule to all future generations to the end of the world. Thus it would evidently be the duty of a people to hear[^008-note165] their pastor in such a case, if no such important act of theirs with respect to him as their rejecting him from being their pastor depended upon it. But when they are proposing to withdraw themselves wholly from him, to cast off all the bonds of their covenant with him, to withhold his maintenance casting him & his family on the wide world, & to renounce all the obligations & duties which they owe him as their pastor; & their doing this depends on their judgment of the doctrine which he offers to preach to them as the mind of Christ; for them resolutely & finally to refuse so much as to give him a hearing, is one of the most flagrant instances of injustice in a people towards their pastor which perhaps has been heard of in these parts of the world.

Surely the state of things among a people cannot be regarded as ripe for such important proceedings as these till they are in a capacity to act in them understandingly and as knowing what they do. But it is obvious that this cannot be untill they have given their pastor a fair hearing; nor can they ever be regarded as having prepared themselves for thus rejecting their pastor as having no farther concern with him in a pastoral relation, until they have first discharged the debt[^008-note166] due to him as their pastor; & this cannot be done untill they have heard him, untill they have heard what he offers to teach them as the [74] mind & will of Christ, have heard what he has to say in for himself in this matter wherein they are offended with him as supposing that he fails to perform the duty of a minister of Christ towards them.

This Church in its first establishment held the very principles for which I now contend, but Mr. Stoddard was was of a contrary opinion & from the pulpit he freely & abundantly delivered what he supposed to be the mind & will of God. And was it ever doubted whether the people were obliged to give him a hearing? In the days of Mr. Mather the Church was congregational in its principles & practice. Mr. Stoddard was a Presbyterian & abundantly preached his presbyterian principles; & did any body doubt of their obligation to hear him. Yea it never entered into the hearts of the people that any proceeding of theirs so important as their rejecting him from being their minister depended on their judgment of his doctrine.

That my people have never given me a fair hearing on the point of controversy between us is exceedingly apparent.

They have never generally read the work which I have published on the subject. This is publicly confessed. Only twenty copies have been procured. Many of those who might have read even these have shewed an utter aversion to reading it. One of my most strenuous opposers declares that the people are in no way to be informed of the reasons of my opinion for two years to come; while others have asserted that they are never likely to be generally informed. Numerous witnesses declare that they had already[^008-note167] refused to read it & that others have said that they would not even let the book come into their houses. If the Council are at a loss on this point & any one should call it in question [75] I suppose there are numbers present who can say enough with regard to it to satisfy any reasonable person. From the narrative which has been given the Council also see how stiffly & inflexibly the people Committee[^008-note168] have ever declined hearing the reasons of my opinion from the pulpit, when proposed from time to time before the printing of my book & since; & how they have been so much engaged in the matter that they have repeatedly refused to have the question whether it was reasonable & best that I should preach upon the subject[^008-note169] to the neighboring ministers or to any Council whatsoever. Before the publication of my pamphlet they would not consent that I should preach on the ground that it was best I should publish; & now I have published they will not read.

Finally though I have often desired it they have never given me an opportunity to state the reasons of my opinion even in private conversation.

Thus the great part of my people have refused to give me any hearing at all, or to suffer themselves to be informed in any way whatsoever of the reasons of my opinion. Nay, they have industriously guarded against it, as what they have so much dreaded, that they would by no means allow of any advice, or any thing which they thought might open a door for it; but have been engaged to have me hastily thrust out before there should be any farther opportunity for the people to be better[^008-note170] informed least it should make proselytes[^008-note171] to my sentiments & thus divide the people & make parties among them. If this be not a violent proceeding for a christian Church toward their pastor I never expect to know what is. Herein they have not only violated the rules of christian charity & gentleness & acted inconsistently with moral righteousness, but [76] very inconsistently with themselves. They themselves from the beginning have implicitly acknowledged that I had a right to be heard by them, & that[^008-note172] the people should in some way or other be informed of the reasons of my opinion. If there was no need of this, then what need of their desiring me to print my reasons, & what need of their waiting some time for the impression[^008-note173]; & what need of their meeting together in July to see if they should wait any longer for my book; and what need if their desiring Col. Dwight to enquire when the books were likely to be done & to send them word & what need of their determining if the books were likely to come speedily that they would wait for them & what need after the books came of their forbearing to act for about two months.

It may be said that although the people in general have not read my pamphlet, yet most of the leading men in the church have read it. To this I answer; If I have a right to be heard by some I have a right to be heard by the body of the people. What need of any being informed if there be no need of the people in general being informed. If one can be justified in refusing to hear or read why not another; & why may not every one be justified in refusing to hear or read a word. And thus why might not all[^008-note174] the people have thrust me out immediately as soon as they knew my opinion without giving me any opportunity to print or speak any thing for myself. The controversy on this subject is between me & the Church & not between me & the leading men of the Church; & if I have any right to be heard at all it is by them with whom I have this controversy. It is not merely the leading men but all the brethren who are to have a hand in the act of [77] the Church which must make way for a dissolution of my pastoral relation with them if it be dissolved. They have lately expressly & deliberately refused to leave the government of the Church to the leading men in a public formal consideration of the matter; but would have it in the hands of the whole society. If others beside the leading men are not set aside as cyphers in acting with regard to my being turned away, they ought not to be aside as cyphers in learning & judging. The grand reason continually urged & insisted on why my reasons should not be heard from the pulpit has been, that there was danger of its making parties in the town. Now I beseech the Rev. Council to consider for a moment what sort of an objection this is -- The very reason why it is thought just that a person, in a cause in which he is liable to suffer, should be allowed to plead his own cause is that he may have a fair opportunity before he suffers to convince others that his cause is good. What a strange reason then is it, why a man in such a case should not be allowed to plead his own cause & why his plea should not be heard - lest some of those to whom he offers his plea should be convinced that his cause is good. So unreasonable is this opinion that the very end of a man’s pleading his cause & the very thing which is the sole ground & reason why it is accounted fair & just that a man should be allowed to plead his own cause viz. a fair opportunity to convince others that his cause is good - is by the people made the main objections why I should not plead my own cause - viz. because if I do, there will be an opportunity, & so a possibil=[78] ity, of convincing some that my cause is good.

The case would be the same if it were a cause on the issue of which my life depended[^008-note175]. This discussion might occasion parties & unhappy contentions. This is not only possible but often has actually been the case with regard to the execution of persons in a public capacity. How often have cities & nations been set into a ferment on such occasions. Must we therefore say that the suspected person shall have have no hearing because the people -- the judges on whose voice[^008-note176] his life depends are united in condemning him;[^008-note177] there is danger of their being divided if he is allowed to speak for himself.

And besides my people in the very making of this objection are condemned out of their own mouths. The objection in the very terms of it, is an implicit acknowledgement, that there has been as yet no sufficient trial what the minds of the people would be on a fair & full hearing of what I have to say for myself. Yea a confession that they suspect & that very strongly, that the opinion of many if I should have a full hearing would be far otherwise than now. For, if not, how would the people after hearing me be divided into parties any more than they are now ? But if as they thus confess there has been sufficient trial what the minds of the people would be after a full hearing, certainly there ought to be a sufficient trial & they ought not to strive to hinder it; for in striving to hinder it they do directly & avowedly strive to have me condemned & turned out of the ministry & with my family deprived of maintenance without a fair & proper[^008-note178] trial;[79] which is certainly the most barefaced injustice. But it may be asked - Why didn’t you preach,[^008-note179] why did you not use your right, without waiting for the consent of the people ? -- To this question I have several answers.

1. When for the sake of peace, I have repeatedly proposed to the people that, with their consent, I would preach upon the subject, they have, as I just observed uniformly refused it; & have also from time to time even to submit the point whether it was reasonable I should preach upon it to the neighboring ministers. After such refusals the question should scarcely be asked[^008-note180].

2. The state of the people has been most obviously such that if I had taken any opportunity on the Sabbath without their previous consent, it would have been the occasion of tumult on that holy day to the extreme dishonor of Christ & wounding the interests of religion. Noise & uproar have risen to such a height already, that I cannot think it was my duty or that it would have become the prudence & moderation of a minister of the Gospel. It would have had the appearance the of great strenuousness; & I thought it better that the people should first be prepared by the advice of a Council which would greatly tend to prevent the mischievous effects.

3. If I had appointed lecture there is[^008-note181] not the least probability that the people would have attended them. Of this the Rev. Council will be satisfied by considering the account they have had of their conduct.

On the whole I thought it the most prudent course to wait for a more favorable opportunity: It may be said that the people are the more to be [80] justified in rejecting me & turning me out from my office, without hearing me because I was settled on the contrary principles.

I answer that this objection can be of no force unless they mean by it that I settled on Mr. Stoddard’s judgement as my rule. If I did I did not settle as a minister of Christ, but as a minister of Mr. Stoddard. Even if it had been so, that I had settled in this manner on Mr. Stoddard’s principles this was one which I found among his principles which he expresses in one of his works in these words -- "He who believes principles because our forefathers affirm makes idols of them; & it would be no humility but baseness of spirit, for us to judge ourselves incapable of examining principles which have been handed down to us. If we are any wise fit to open the mysteries of the Gospel we are capable of judging in these matters."---

It was implied in my ordination vows that I would study the Scriptures; that I would make the word of God, & not the word of any man, my rule in teaching my people, & that I would do my utmost to know what was the counsel of God, & to declare it. This was implied in my covenant with God & the people at my settlement; & it was implied in their covenant with me God & with me that in my so doing they would diligently & impartially hear & examine what I should offer to them as the counsel of God.

It is said that Mr. Stoddard would never have consented to my settling here, if he had foreseen that I should so differ from him in my principles. To this it is sufficient to reply, that he[81] doubtless would have been as much against it if he had foreseen in me any such departure from his principles as has actually taken place in the church since his death with regard to church discipline. I had as much reason given me by the Church at[^008-note182] my settlement, to depend upon it that they would allow me the same power in Church government which they[^008-note183] yielded to Mr. Stoddard; as they had to depend upon it that I would allow them the same open door to the Lord’s table. The Church allowed Mr. Stoddard a negative; & never so far as I have heard of, disputed it, at least never in the present[^008-note184] generation. Now they greatly find fault with me for claiming it, and have departed to the length of Brownism. They have as properly departed from the principles on which they settled me, as I have departed from those on which I accepted a settlement.

If the objection should arise in the minds of any of the members of the Rev. Council,--That if I should be allowed to preach my doctrine to my people, there might be some danger of infecting neighboring churches I hope I need not say much in answer to such an objection. Plain justice must not be hindered & suppressed for fear of some imagined accidental inconveniences. The wiser Heathen could say "Fiat justitia, ruat coelum."-- The neighboring ministers have as much liberty to preach & defend their principles among their people as I desire to have among mine & can do it with far greater advantages than I expect to enjoy. Doubtless they would take this liberty & would take it ill if any one should attempt to restrain them. And I trust they are very willing to do to others as they wish others to do to them.

II. That the state of things is not ripe for calling the propos=[82] ed Council is apparent from the frame & temper of mind which my people have hitherto been in, & especially of late. I am sensible that an Ecclesiastical Council in their advice are not to proceed by any uncertain conjectures concerning the secrets of mens hearts. But yet in adapting their advice to the state of a people they doubtless are to have[^008-note185] regard to those things which are visible & notorious. What the temper of the minds of this people, at least of the governing part of them, has been hitherto & especially of late, has been as manifest as any thing concerning the state of a people can be. It cannot be hid. It must needs be visible to all around us. It is manifest not only from the customary conversation of the people in private houses but from the whole tenor of their public proceedings -- from the methods which have been taken, from the measures adopted, from the proceedings of Church meetings, & Precinct meetings, & their Committee, from the speeches which have been publicly made & the acts which have been publicly done. It would occupy a great deal of time to set forth all the particulars. But this is needless; as the Council has heard the Narrative of our proceedings.[^008-note186]

The temper which the people have manifested, I humbly conceive, ought to be the more observed by the Rev. Council & to have the greater influence on their determination because I have never offered this people any provocation, unless yielding & condescending, & taking the utmost care to avoid offending them has a provocation. I have sought peace & pursued it & have striven to the[^008-note187] utmost to avoid occasions of strife. I have clogged them in any reasonable proceeding in this affair though against myself. [83] I told them long ago, even at the very first interview with the Church that if they insisted on calling a council immediately, who should have power to finish our whole controversy, I would not oppose or hinder it, though I could not advise to it. I have yielded to them from time to time in every thing wherein I could do it with a clear conscience. That after examining the subject by the aid of the sacred scriptures in the best manner I am able, I have adopted & still hold the sentiments which I have publicly professed, with regard to the qualifications for full communion in the Visible Church;-- & that too with the fullest expectation of being driven from my ministerrial office & stripped of a maintenance for my numerous family -- I admit.-- Whether in all this I have acted in the fear of God, with a good conscience & in the integrity of my heart the[^008-note188] Rev. Council may judge. This one thing excepted, if it be an exception I have given my people no sort of occasion in any respect whatsoever for any violent proceeding or the least vehemence; unless yielding & submitting for peace sake be just warrant for[^008-note189] insulting me the more. For evidence of all this I appeal to the Narrative of our proceedings which has been read here publicly in the hearing of you all.

Now I think the temper & frame of mind which my people discover in the way of taking the important step of dissolving the relation between me & them for the present; & that on two accounts:

1. Such a temper & frame is manifestly utterly inconsistent with a proper & just hearing & considering the reasons which I have to offer for myself in that thing which is the grand controversy between me & them. So that if it could [84] be proved that they had all read my book through, which it is apparent they are far from having done, yet merely in this they do not discharge themselves. They ought to give a fair hearing at least with some degree of calmness, & candor & coolness of consideration; but from facts, which are open & public it is evident that they have been notoriously far from it. Hearing in a high degree of fermentation of mind manifested by continued outward manifestation irregularity & precipitation of proceeding, is no fair hearing, & ought not to stand for any thing, or to be regarded as any hearing at all by wise & just judges. Hence it is most plain that my people are now bound to give me a fair hearing, before they can justly demand a dissolution of my pastoral relation; & with equal clearness is it manifest that means must first be used with them to bring them to another temper of mind any such demand can be heard or accepted.

2. It is[^008-note190] itself utterly unfit and unbecoming in a christian church to proceed to an affair so deeply affecting their spiritual welfare as the dismission of their pastor in such a temper of mind: especially of one who has been so long their pastor, & between whom & them such a state of things has subsisted as between me & this people. The Apostle says to the Church of Corinth, "Let all things be done with charity"; -- & surely it is exceedingly[^008-note191] unbecoming Churches of the Lamb of God to manage their religious affairs of the greatest importance in a ferment and tumult; which ought to be managed with great solemnity, deep humiliation, submission to the awful frowns of Heaven & humble dependence on God, & with [85] fervent prayer & supplication. But for a church to undertake such an affair in such a manner as this, will be most unbecoming the Gospel, greatly to the dishonor of God & religion & eminently calculated to prevent the divine blessing.

The Rev. Council will also perceive that the consequences of my being driven away in this manner will be in many respects exceedingly pernicious.

1. It will[^008-note192] be a great & most extensive injury to the credit and interest of religion. For the story to be circulated that the people of Northampton, a people heretofore so often & remarkably distinguished by the divine favour, drove away their minister in the midst of so much heat & contention, I need not say how it will wound religion abroad.

2. It will be a great wrong to this; not only as they will thereby bring guilt upon[^008-note193] themselves; but will exceedingly wound their own reputation & interest in the country, & render difficult the future settlement of the ordinances of the Gospel among them.

3. It will be a great injury to me.

4. If the people are countenanced in these measures by a Council so far as to advise to such an issue which the people seek with such a temper & in such a tumultuous manner; it will in its consequences be a great injury to other churches and ministers as it will directly encourage similar proceedings in case of differences between minister & people. This case is likely to be very famous, the eyes of the whole country x[^008-note194] are greatly drawn upon it, to observe the issue & management of it. It will be likely to be long remembered, & will there fore be so much the more likely to be of extensive & lasting influence as a precedent.

Hence I humbly conceive that there is no ripeness in the present state of[86] things for any immediate measures in order to bring about this event, unless violence of spirit & of conduct be regarded as[^008-note195] ripeness of a christian church for managing their religious concerns of the most solemn nature & of the greatest importance. Indeed this seems to be the notion which many of the Church have had of such a ripeness from their earnestly driving the matter at the last Precinct meeting to have me voted out of town before this Council assembled i.e. to have it voted that the people desired that I should be gone, so that the people Council might see that they were ripe. But I suppose the[^008-note196] Rev. Council have greater wisdom than to entertain the same notion of a ripeness for such a proceeding; & I humbly conceive that they will see it to be indispensably necessary that first the utmost endeavours be used to bring the people to juster views & a better temper; before they advise to any steps in order to an immediate separation. And I beseech the Council to use their utmost & most prudent endeavours that if finally we are separated, we may part with one another fairly & peaceably.

I also request of the[^008-note197] Rev. Council that they will[^008-note198] do me the justice in their Result,--not merely to advise the people disjunctively, either to read my book or to hear my reasons from the pulpit; but to give it as their opinion that I have a right to preach, & that if I do preach, the people are obliged to hear me. I humbly conceive that this will be no more than the case requires for three reasons

1. if such disjunctive advice be left, it will not tend so much to ripen in any measure to ripen our affairs for an issue; for then indeed they will neither hear me [87] preach, nor be likely to read my book. It will be said that the number of copies is small. The disinclination is great. It will be supposed that the Council do not wish a very strict scrutiny whether they have read it or not. The whole matter will be left at very[^008-note199] loose ends & in great uncertainty. Thus it will tend greatly to embarrass our affairs & lengthen out our controversy.

2. My people complain, many of them, that on this subject they cannot understand me. Now if unawares I have spoken to them a language they cannot understand, let me have an opportunity to explain myself. Surely it will not be proceeding with christian moderation & charity, - to say at once, "You have been mistaken in your manner of pleading your cause you have spoken to us inadvertently, so that we have wholly misapprehended your meaning; but since you have thus failed of making us understand you, your mouth shall now be stopped, & we will give you no farther opportunity to speak for yourself."

3. I ought, before I leave this people, to whom I have so long stood in such a relation to have an opportunity given me to leave with them a testimony for myself in that matter which proves so great an offence to them not only with those who are in the church but with others both male & female to whom I have stood in such a relation to have an oppor= tunity of whom it can never be expected that they should generally read my pamphlet. The laws of nature & the laws of Christ require me to love this people to whom I have been so related & to value their charity & esteem. I have reason also to think that there are many of my spiritual children,[^008-note200] in this congregation who now entertain hard thoughts on account of my opinion. Now I ought not to be driven from hence without opportunity to exhibit a testimony for [88] myself before them & so with the people at large. When I have done so, I demand nothing of them but an impartial hearing. I desire not to lord it over their consciences. They have a right to judge for themselves & may use what means[^008-note201] to see the strength of the arguments on the other side, they please by reading books or conversing with ministers who differ with[^008-note202] me in judgment.

I humbly trust therefore, that this Rev. Council will not fail to leave behind, in their Result, a direct & full expression of their judgment on this important point. After the Agents for the Church had replied to these remarks, the Council adjourned. The next morning I delivered in to the Council the following writing

I the subscriber do make the following decalration & offer: -- That if my people, being so advised by the Council of churches now sitting, will hear me deliver the reasons of my opinion from the pulpit & consider further of the matter in controversy between me & them until the spring when it shall be comfortable traveling, laying aside all public agitation until then, & then desire a Council of Churches in order to bring our controversy to a final issue, & will consent being also so advised by this Council, that I shall have an equal hand in the choice of the Council with them, & that I should go out of the County into the other parts of N. England for my choice; & this Council on a full hearing & thorough consideration of our case can find out no way for a composition or accommodation, either [89] by satisfying my conscience in yielding some points to the people, or by making them easy in some things in a compliance with me, or any other way which the council in their wisdom may devise; but the people shall after all declare their unwillingness that I should be their pastor, I will declare it before the Council as my desire that the people should be left entirely at their liberty as to my continuing their pastor; & will move it to them to gratify the people’s desire in dissolving my pastoral relation with this church, provided the Precinct will first engage to free me from rates,- & will, the Council so advising, resign my pastoral office. -- This[^008-note203] is that, to which I humbly propose & desire this Rev. Council to advise this people to consent; withal strongly advising that in the mean time quietness & peace be maintained, & jangling agitations & public proceedings, tending to enkindle or uphold strife be laid aside; & that the Lord’s supper be restored, if the people can find it in their hearts freely to consent to it, on the advice of the Council; & that this Council also endeavour to find out a way, that those who are able & willing to make a profession of Godliness, may be admitted into the church in a way consistent with a good conscience in both pastor & people & that all parts of the public service of God be quietly, steadily & regularly upheld & attended. Jonathan Edwards" Northampton, Dec. 27th 1749"

The committee or agents of the Church were allowed some time to consider of this proposal, & were after=[90] wards heard in their objections against it. The next day, Dec. 28th, the Council drew up & declared the following Result.

[The rest of this page is blank.][^008-note204]

Then the Council adjourned themselves to the first Wednesday in February [91] 1750.

The next Sabbath, Dec. 31, I publicly read the Result of the Council to the whole congregation & declared a readiness on my part to comply with the Result of Council[^008-note205]; & desired the Church to take the subject of the restoration of the sacrament of the Lord’s Supper into their consideration until next Sabbath, when I proposed to put it to vote in the Church, Whether the Lord’s Supper shall be administered the Sabbath following.

The next Sabbath Jan. 1. 1750 the Precinct met again according to adjournment, & having understood that the Rev. Peter Clark of Salem Village had undertaken to write an Answer to my Book on Qualifications for Communion, they determined to write to him desiring him to expedite what he had undertaken. They also chose a committee to converse with me pursuant as they supposed to that clause of the Result of Council wherein they advise that Pastor & people should converse freely together about the point in controversy.

The Committee chosen were Major[^008-note206] Pomroy, Lieut. Noah Wright, Dr. Mather, Capt. Roger Clap, Increase Clark, Deac. Cook, and Ebenezer Hunt. The Precinct meeting then[^008-note207] adjourned themselves to Monday, Feb. 12.

Deac. Cook came to me that evening, & informed me of the appointment of this Committee, & their business[^008-note208] & of their design of coming to converse with me the Wednesday following. I objected against it as a public proceeding & so plainly contrary to the advice of the Council; but told him that I would nevertheless take the matter into consideration untill the next evening when I would send him my thoughts & determination on the affair in writing. Accordingly the next evening I sent him the following Letter:

To Deacon Noah Cook in Northampton" On mature consideration I am confirmed in the same mind which I expressed the last night concerning the Committee chosen to confer [92] with me. It appears to me altogether of the nature of a public proceeding with respect to the present controversy. The appointment & choice of the committee was a public proceeding. The Committee are the representatives of a public society. And if you come & confer with me as a Committee of the Precinct you then[^008-note209] act in a public capacity in the name & behalf of the Precinct; & all from beginning to end will be a public public proceeding & so plainly contrary to the advice of the Council. The appointed interview of the Committee with me cannot be understood otherwise than as a meeting appointed for a sort of[^008-note210] public dispute; for[^008-note211] the whole Parish will not be actually present, by their representatives & it is to be a debate or discourse managed in behalf of the whole. The Committee are to hear my arguments in some sort as the ear of the Society, that the whole may be influenced by it otherwise I do not see how they can in hearing act in behalf of the Precinct; & if they do not act in behalf of the Precinct how do they act as a committee for the Precinct. This I think is not a reasonable way of proceeding for the information of the whole Parish not tending to light and peace but the contrary, & contrary to the express words of the Council’s advice, & disagreeable to the plain design of it- tending to supersede & set aside the thing at which they aimed. Therefore I must decline conferring with such a body of men together chosen as a committee of the Precinct; but stand ready at any time to confer with freedom & friendliness with each of these brethren, or any others coming in a private capacity, & in their own name only. I am your friend & servant for Jesus’ sake, Jan. 2, 1750 " Jonathan Edwards "

On Wednesday Feb. 7, 1750 the Council met again, & the subject of the Pastor’s going out of the County, in the choice of his part of the Decisive Council, was again very largely de=[93] bated before them, by the Pastor, & the Committee of the Church, & also by some private members of the Church. My own remarks upon the subject were as follows.[^008-note212]

If I should attempt to prove that a vicinity of churches have no jurisdiction over particular churches within their bounds established by a universal unalterable rule which ought never to be dispensed with, in any case whatever; I presume the Rev. Council would regard the attempt as wholly impertinent a needless burdening them with proofs of what nobody would ever dispute. I shall therefore take it for granted unless it should[^008-note213] be questioned that the rule of confining Councils to a vicinity is only a general rule from which exceptions are to be made in cases especially requiring it. Hence the only question is whether that be the case or not.[^008-note214]

In order to determine this question with clearness & certainty we must, as I observed at the session of the Council in December, previously ascertain what will be the business of the proposed Future Council. The business obviously must determine the qualifications; & if on a strict comparison of business & qualifications, it be found that a different Council is really requisite from that which may be constituted of churches of the vicinity; then it will follow that a different Council must be allowed & cannot be denied; & that, whatever may be said of any customs of churches, or of any parallel customs with regard to civil tribunals; the nature of this particular case must be looked into, & that, & that alone must determine the matter. What the nature of the case requires, that the law of reason requires & justice requires, & that the Law of God requires.

If I may be allowed to recapitulate very briefly some of the remarks then made in order to refresh the memories of [94] the Council I observe that the business of the future Council will be not to decide, Whether my opinion, on the qualifications for communion is right or not - because we know the opinion and practice of every man who will be chosen, before he comes. Nor is it[^008-note215] whether I shall remain the minister of Northampton if, after all proper steps are taken to effect an accommodation the people still desire my dismission -- because, when that shall have been done I will trouble no Council any farther than barely to give me leave to relinquish my Pastoral office. But their business will be to decide: 1. Whether they ought not to make some attempts to effect an accommodation of our difficulties; & if this decided in the negative, 2. Whether all has been done by all[^008-note216] parties, which justice requires to be done, previous to a separation; & if this be decided in the affirmative to state in their Result. 3. What are the grounds of my dismission, how far I am innocent, & how far I may be recommended as deserving future employment in the ministry. If this Rev. Council will closely consider the matter they will easily see that these things must constitute their business.

And if this be so, the question, What qualifications does this business require ? amounts to simply this -- Is impartiality, as to the two contending parties, a qualification absolutely necessary in those who are to judge between them? -- To determine this, let each of the points which must be submitted to the future Council, be viewed. If means of accommodation are to be used, ought not those who are to act as mediators, to be in the middle between the parties to be reconciled. If they are to judge with regard to the mutual rights & claims of the two parties, & to decide whether each has done all that the other may fairly require previous to a separation; [95] does not settling points of equity, between two parties at variance, require even balances. And if they are to pronounce before the world, on the conduct of the pastor in this controversy as well as on his general character; may he not justly demand that the tribunal which is to do this shall be impartial?

If these things are plain, & I cannot but think that every person of sober thought will own them to be self- evident; then the only things to be determined are these two 1. What constitutes an essential defect of impartiality as to the two parties in this case ?

2. Whether the defect can be supposed to belong to a Council constituted of the Churches in this vicinity ? As to the first point, it must be obviously be admitted to be a radical defect in the impartiality of the Council, if the members of it are all on one side, or are all known to side with one of the parties against the other, on the main point in controversy. What can be more plain[^008-note217] that a balance cannot be even, & therefore cannot be fit to adjust matters of equity between two parties, if all the previous weight is in one scale. And is it not equally evident that a Council, who are all on one side in the controversy, are not in the middle between the two parties, as mediators to reconcile them. And since a Council cannot in this case be obtained that is impartial as to individuals, because all the members are chosen, & chosen with reference to their opinions; it is plain that the consistory ought to be so constituted, that one part may balance the other. If we cannot find a balance which has no previous weight in either of the scales, yet surely we should seek one which has not all the weights in one scale [96] but equal weights in both, that the balance may be even. I need not remark again that mankind[^008-note218] both the wise & unwise, are liable to the strongest prejudices against the persons & conduct of those around them, who differ from them in matters of religion; that this is as true with regard to points not fundamental, as with regard to those which are; that it is especially true, when the controversy is at the height of agitation; that it is preeminently true with regard to those who are the movers & managers of the controversy; & that nothing from age to age has been found to excite prejudices equally strong with this. These points are too clear to admit of denial or doubt. Hence if the future Council be all one side, as to the main controversy between me & my people, it is an apparent & essential[^008-note219] defect of impartiality; & of course it is most unrighteous to confine the other party to such a Council & oblige him to be judged by them & no other.

As to the other question, Will a Council taken wholly from the County have this defect; it is a fact perfectly known that the ministers & Churches of this County are almost universally against me on the point, which now divides me & my people & makes us two parties. Perhaps there may be one or two ministers who are partly of my mind; but then their churches are all of a contrary mind & on the same side with my people. I suppose that there is not more than one minister fully of my mind with respect to the Sacrament of the Lord’s Supper & not one Church of my mind with regard to either of the sacraments. Of course a Council cannot be obtained wholly from within the County, without the forementioned essential defect.

It having been[^008-note220] established as I humbly conceive, that the future [97] Council cannot be qualified for the business which will be laid before it, unless it is strictly impartial; that an impartial Council cannot be chosen, unless I am permitted to select from beyond the limits of the County of Hampshire; & that to deny me this liberty therefore would be direct & palpable injustice; I shall proceed to remark on some of the objections which are brought against this measure.

It was urged at the former sitting of the Council- That if there actually is a jurisdiction over particular churches & ministers established in a vicinity, the reason alleged cannot be a sufficient reason for an exception; & that if there is no such jurisdiction actually established by agreement, yet if there ought to be it alters not the case. For if it ought to be established we may fairly look upon it as really established in the law of reason; &, if so, this case has nothing in it to require an exception from such a jurisdiction any more than a case growing out of any other error fundamental or nonfundamental. If a Church should complain of its minister for turning Arminian, & all the ministers & Churches were Calvinists, it is said he ought not therefore to[^008-note221] go from the vicinity, to get half of the Council to be[^008-note222] of his opinion, in order that it might be impartial. -- In answer to this objection I would observe,

1. That in order to judge of its force the business of the future Council must be kept in mind. And I hope it will be remembered that the business of the Council will not be to try me with regard to my opinion respecting the qualifications for communion, to find me guilty or not[^008-note223] or to justify or condemn that opinion. The fact that a differ=[98] ence of opinion subsists between the pastor & the Church, will indeed be taken into consideration, as well as the question, whether on the whole it is best[^008-note224] we should be separated; but doubtless they would regard it as useless & impertinent, to try me on the question, whether this opinion is heresy or not In such a case they would be called to try a minister as a delinquent, to examine the fact & openly to censure the doctrine.

If this were the business of the Council it might not[^008-note225] make a great difference as to the manner in which it ought to be constituted for the case of such a minister would be like the case of a person accused of some crime for which he was to be tried. In such a case the impartiality requisite is impartiality as to the fact, but not as to the nature of the crime. Thus, if a man were accused of drunkenness, the judges ought to be strictly impartial as to the question,[^008-note226] Whether drunkenness be a crime. The crime they ought to abhor, and such abhorrence renders them not the worse but the better judges. Virtue of course prejudices men against vice; & the more virtuous judges are,[^008-note227] the more zealously opposed to vice, the fitter are they to be judges of vicious persons! Hence in such a case it would be ridiculous in[^008-note228] the accused to insist that half his judges should be men who approved drunkenness & that, whether there were any established jurisdiction or not. And it would not alter the case whether it were proposed that his judges should be of the vicinity or brought from some other continent. From whatever places collected they ought in[^008-note229] strict justice to be men, who had an entire abhorrence of the crime of drunkenness.

The seeming force of this objection arises from a confusion of thought in those [99] who were[?] it in losing sight of the real point in question in forgetting the proper business of the future Council, & inadvertently supposing it to be like that of judges who are called to try a criminal. The question of fact whether I hold a given opinion will not come up before them: It is admitted beforehand. Neither will the question whether that opinion be heresy: no man pretends it. That opinion will not be presented to them as a crime or fault to be judged & punished; but merely as the ground of an alleged difference of opinion between pastor & people. It is not on the merits of the cause i.e. of my opinion on which[^008-note230] they will decide but on the case of difficulty growing out of a difference of opinions[^008-note231]; & as was observed before, they will be called to act as mediators between the disagreeing parties, to settle matters of equity between them, & to judge of the character & conduct of the pastor with regard to the controversy. And with regard to all these points it has been shown that to be on either side has a most obvious[^008-note232] tendency to bias the mind against the other.

That the seeming force of this objection in the minds of those who urge it arises from losing sight of the true state of the case, & the proper business of the Council is obvious from the very example adduced by way of illustration, viz. That in a civil action it is no valid objection against the justices of the vicinity, who have an established jurisdiction, that their opinion on a given point of law is already known: - because in the present case the business of the future Council will not be, to try the merits of the cause, or to judge whether my opinion be agreeable to the Law, i.e.[^008-note233] the word of God or not. And with respect to this example I would further observe the following things.

1. If it were really so that the proposed Council were to judge the very merits of my cause i.e.[^008-note234] the soundness of my opinion, the [100] instance adduced would not be at all parallel or of any force in the present argument. Let the case be put thus: Suppose a man has done something towards a given individual which many regard as a breach of Law exposing him to be disenfranchised & the question turns on a point of Law which has long been matter of warm controversy between[^008-note235] judges & jurists; & suppose there is no stated tribunal but it is the custom of the Country in cases of controversy for each party to choose half of the judges; & it is known that there are as many jurists on one side in the controverted point of Law as on the other, & there is no appeal from the tribunal chosen but their judgment will be final; would it not be reasonable in this case, if one party chose half of the judges favorable to his side of the question that the other should choose the other half favorable to his side; & that, although all the jurists of the immediate vicinity were opposed to him. If one party had actually chosen his half of the judges who were all on his side, would it not be mere mockery to tell the other party that he also might have the liberty of choosing half of the judges, as well as his adversary, but only he must choose all of them from the side which were opposed to him. Now this is precisely my case. Councils are elective & to be appointed by joint or mutual choice according to uniform practice. This has been the practice of both parts of this County. It was pursued in the lower part of the County in the case of Mr. Allis; & in the upper part of the County in the case of Mr. Rawson, & in the Council of May 3, 1737, of which I was the scribe & have the original papers now by me, as well as in the subsequent Council convened at the same place. My people[^008-note236] do not pretend that any stated consis=[101] tory exists or that the Council is not to be elective they offer me the choice of one half of the Council, but only would confine me to Churches & ministers of their opinion.

2. In civil affairs appeals are allowed from the justices of the County to others who come from a distance appointed without any regard to vicinity; & the determination of those more remote judges supersedes & sets aside that of the judges of[^008-note237] the vicinity. Indeed many important are carried directly to these more distant judges without suffering the judges from the vicinity to meddle with them any further than to refer them to the judgment of the distant judges; & that too on account of the great importance of the case. And so it would here in our ecclesiastical affairs if we had regular inferior & superior tribunals like those of Scotland.

3. Difference of opinion on a mere point of law has very little tendency to prejudice the mind against the persons & conduct of others, compared with difference of opinion in matters of religion. No one will dispute the fact that the latter has in all ages excited the deepest prejudices in mankind against each other. But who ever heard of such prejudices, & alienations in individuals, in parties, & in nations, merely because they differed in opinion on point of law.

4. Civil tribunals are not appointed to act the part of mediators between contending parties; except in cases of arbitration in which each party has equal liberty of choice. Their office is to see that the laws be executed; & there is not therefore the same necessity that there should be some of the judges on each side as in the present case.

But to return to the objection itself.
