It is asked, if a minister [102] should be complained of by his people for embracing Arianism or any other heresy or for turning to another denomination for example to the Church of England & a Council should be required to adjust the difficulty why might he not in such a case as well as in the present insist on the liberty of going out of the County to get half of the Council who embraced the same heresy, or who belonged to the Church of England, that they might be impartial? To this I answer,

1. [^008-note238]The Council would not come together to consider the question whether the individual, if he had embraced the alleged heresy or had changed to another denomination might be lawfully continued as the minister of a Congregational Church. This point is settled beforehand. They would come simply to find whether the charge against him was true or false. Hence I suppose that the following will be found on the most careful inquiry to be the reasons & the only reasons why he could not claim to have some of the judges of his own side.

(1.) Because in the case of acknowledged heresy, those who are on his side are not fit to be members of the Christian Church. Fundamental errors are scandalous; & the Church cannot therefore consistently with their own profession call such as constituent members of a christian Council & leave their ecclesiastical affairs with those who embrace them for those[^008-note239] who are not duly qualified to be members of a Christian Church cannot be fit members of a christian Council, to direct & manage the affairs of the christian Church. Or, (2.) because in the case of turning to a different denomination, that of the Episcopalians or the Anabaptists the individual is now statedly of a different denomination [103] communion. For although christians of these denominations may occasionally & transiently join with Presbyterians & Congregationalists in some parts of worship; yet as to what is stated, there is a division openly established. It is a point perfectly settled that, as to their stated[^008-note240] & their ecclesiastical proceedings, they must act apart. And there would be an obvious inconsistency in a church employing those who are already of a distinct sect, & have no ecclesiastical connexion with them to order & settle their ecclesiastical affairs.

These I suppose to be the only reasons, why it is not proper that a minister, who embraces heresy, other or joins another denomination, cannot choose from his own side half of the Council, which is convened cannot choose from his own side half of the Council not to judge of his doctrines, or to mediate, or to[^008-note241] do justice between the parties, but to investigate an alleged fact & on finding it to vacate the office; unless in such a case as that of embracing Popery, there might be this additional reason against Papists being allowed to sit on the Council. That Papists are bound to injure, persecute & destroy the Protestant Church as much as in them lies & we cannot be bound to entrust our affairs to those whose avowed design it is beforehand to injure & destroy us.

2. The reason why a minister in such a case may not go out of the vicinity to get half of the council of his own side is not the existence of any established jurisdiction or of[^008-note242] established rule respecting the vicinity or the County but of a totally different nature, as will appear from a slight examination. Suppose a minister settled over an orthodox church to turn Arian, & all the churches & ministers[^008-note243] to be professed Arians, it is admitted not only [104] that the church are not bound to the vicinity but that the minister has no right to choose from it, because the vicinity are all unfit to be of any Council. So if he should turn Episcopalian & all the vicinity are Episcopalians it is a point conceded that the Church is not limited to the vicinity & that the minister cannot choose a single member of the Council from the vicinity. Vicinity therefore has no alteration in the cases; which demonstrates the objection to be wholly without force for it plainly shows that it is not any established right in the vicinity, which is regarded in the cases alleged. On the contrary it is the nature of the case in itself considered, & that alone which governs in this matter, & sets aside all considerations of vicinity; which is the very point which[^008-note244] I have asserted & urged. For I have insisted all along that the nature of this case as it is in itself, must be considered & what that demands must be allowed as paramount to the bare circumstance of neighborhood. So that this objection carefully examined, oversets the position it is brought to prove & establishes & demonstrates the grand point on which I insist.

3. It is the natural right of the people, & their liberty of conscience, & not vicinity which governs in the instantces alleged. But in the present case as it is not an established jurisdiction in the vicinity, so it is not the rights of conscience which should confine me to the County; & therefore nothing at all. It cannot be the rights of conscience which confines[^008-note245] me, for it is as much against the rights of conscience for me to choose ministers of my own opinion in the County as out of it. The thing which invades the rights of conscience, if they are invaded [105] at all is not the circumstance of nearness or remoteness, but my having a part of the Council of my opinion whether brought from the vicinity or elsewhere. But it has never been disputed nor[^008-note246] questioned, that I have a right to choose half of the ministers & churches of my opinion, if I can find so many within the County. And my people have urged that some of the ministers in the County are of my opinion & that I may choose them as far as they go though I must not go out of it the County. Now let us turn the tables & suppose that all the churches & ministers in the County were of my opinion would I not have liberty to choose my half of the Council from the vicinity, any rights of conscience in the people to the contrary notwithstanding ? Certainly all who would allow me any liberty of choice at all must admit this. So that it is in effect granted that it is neither any right of jurisdiction in the vicinity nor any rights of conscience in my people which ought to confine me to the limits of the County in my choice. And what then I ask, besides these two things can be conceived of which ought thus to confine me ? - I am not aware of any fallacy in this reasoning, & I wish it to be examined to the bottom.

4. If there be any such thing as an established jurisdiction in the churches of the vicinity, either by argument or the word of God, or the nature of things; then it will follow that all our ecclesiastical judicatories must be stated & not elective. For if there be a settled power or of jurisdiction then the subject of this power or the subject [^008-note247]in which it inheres, is also settled. If it be settled at all, it is settled somewhere or in some subject; [106] & that subject of course is not left at loose ends, to be determined from time to time by the choice of the parties concerned. But such an established jurisdiction as this, has not been pretended or[^008-note248] acted upon - either by this church, or by any of the neighboring churches or ministers; but the churches & ministers[^008-note249] have proceeded on a contrary principle, very lately in various instances; for they have acted in Councils convoked by election without an objection or even an intimation that any established judicatory of the neighborhood ought to have been convoked. Why then should any such establishment be first mentioned & insisted on in my case; where, as we have seen it works the grossest injustice? That usually it is convenient & proper that Councils should be of the neighborhood, I freely acknowledge; but that there has been any such uniform usage as establishes the right now for the first time pretended, I utterly deny; & from those who assert it I unhesitatingly demand the evidence. No such established jurisdiction[^008-note250] either by agreement or custom or the law of God, or the law of reason - was ever maintained either in our days or the days of our forefathers. In the days of our predecessors in the ministry in this County, nothing like it was known; for Mr. Stoddard & Mr. Williams of Hatfield formerly went, when invited to a Council at Norwich in Connecticut, & if I mistake not to another Council at Lebanon; which surely they would not have done if they had thought the law of God & nature settled such an establishment in vicinities. And if any such thing be now insisted on in order to limit me to the vicinity, it will in fact be a new rule[^008-note251] invented for my [---][107] case to deprive me of my plain rights - in a case which has much more that is peculiar to require an exception from such a rule, than other cases in which it has never been pretended.

If it should be insisted that the Council ought not to be elective at all; but that we should take the churches as they come, or that we should take the whole county -- though this is a point not referred to this Council -- I observe that it will be the first instance of[^008-note252] a minister certainly during the present generation, &, as I suppose, since the county was formed. In the case of Mr. Allis the Council was by just[^008-note253] choice, as was that in the case of Mr. Rawson. In the latter of which I was the scribe the question was whether Mr. R. was qualified for the work of the ministry - on account[^008-note254] of his learning, his orthodoxy & his morals; the Council was called by mutual election; Mr. Williams of Hatfield & Mr. Chauncey were members; & no person thought of objecting against the mode of convocation as irregular &[^008-note255] improper. In the second Council in the case of Mr. Rawson, the churches of the Association were indeed convoked; yet it was by a free mutual agreement on prudential considerations merely, & not with reference to any supposed rule binding them to it; as I know from a particular & full enquiry. Elective Councils & not stated ones have hitherto been made use of in all parts of the County both in settling & in unsettling ministers. Very lately, Mr. Webster was settled in the upper part of the County, by an elective Council, & the election was not confined to the County for only two or three were taken from this County to join others from abroad; although it was known beforehand that it was a matter of dispute whether he ought to be settled. Hence most evidently if it be now insisted[^008-note256] as a [108] rule, that the members of a Council must be of the County it will be a new rule introduced for my sake.

And if my Church should now insist upon it, which they have never yet done, that we should convoke a Council of all the churches of the County without distinction it would be peculiarly injurious in my case, not only because they embrace the opposite opinion[^008-note257] in the present controversy but because it is well known that numbers of them have manifested a particular dislike of me - some, on account of[^008-note258] supposed rigidness of my Calvinism; some, a dislike more directly personal in consequence of my disapproving of their settlement; & some in consequence of the late religious disputes which have arisen respecting the late religious excitement throughout New England. These dislikes I do not wish to revive & establish by being obliged to object against them by name as improper members of a Council in my case. Still such things may most reasonably to be considered in the present affair.

With regard to the objection that this Church 35 years ago voted to be subject to a Council of the churches of the County, -- if it has any seeming force, I desire that it may be brought to the test of an exact inquiry in order to determine whether it is now obligatory on the church of the present generation or whether it is null & void. It is certainly one or the other. It is either in force for the existing pastor & members or it is not. It is either alive or dead. If it be alive & in force, then the whole of it is alive; but if it be not alive then it is dead as to every part of it; & we are obliged to observe none of its provisions, except as the laws of God & nature independently of that vote bind us to observe them. Now the question whether that vote is dead or alive ? is [109] easily resolved & depends on the resolution of another question viz. Whether, on Protestant principles, the determination of Ancestors as to matters of religion & the worship of God binds future generations, without their consent either express or implied ? The present members of this Church at least nine out of ten are a new generation. As to any express confirmation of that vote it is notorious that there has been no such thing. As to any implied confirmation of it, if there has been any, it must have been by conforming to it in practice. But this to my knowledge has not been done by the present church of Northampton. Since I have been their pastor they have uniformly in their practice implicitly rejected & annulled it & that not merely in circumstantials, but in the substance & main scope of it. The thing mentioned in the vote is a Council of Churches & not of ministers. The first association of ministers in this county was formed[^008-note259] after my settlement. Yet this church never convoked a Council of Churches in this generation untill now. The main design of that vote too was to have a stated judicatory & not an elective council according to the presbyterian principles of Mr. Stoddard; yet this main design has never been regarded but wholly counteracted. So that it is evident to a demonstration that this church now has no such constitution, & that the ancient Church vote is fact dead. The neighboring ministers & churches also have customarily neglected & counteracted that old argument of some of our forefathers. - If it is now held to bind this church it will be the first time during the present generation. It will be revived out of the dust purely for my sake, after it has been long dead & buried; which will be tantamount to making a new[^008-note260] constitution to meet my individual case. If that vote [110] be really no establishment for us, then I am bound by no part of it; &, if so, no argument ought to be drawn from it with regard to any thing either less or more pertaining to the present case.

It is objected that the present Council can have no supposed[^008-note261] reference to any supposed business of the future Council except that mentioned in the votes of the church to judge whether pastor & people shall be separated; & that they are simply to determine what sort of Council is proper to do that business. To this I answer,

1. The business expressly mentioned in the Vote will not depend on judging the correctness of my opinion respecting[^008-note262] the qualifications for communion, but on a judgment of the rights of the people as to liberty of conscience, & of the rights of the pastor as to what he can fairly demand of the people & so will consist in settling points of justice between the parties.

2. The business expressly mentioned in the vote, does certainly imply what is necessarily connected with it, considering the state of the case; & so must therefore be considered by this Council as implied in that business. Considered thus it includes all the three things which were first mentioned. If a surgeon is sent for to judge whether a limb shall be amputated, the message implies that he is to judge whether amputation may not be dispensed with; if not whether it shall take place immediately or be postponed; & if so in what manner it is to be done with the least injury. In the same manner the expression in the vote of the Church cannot be understood to limit the business of the decisive Council, any further than[^008-note263] their doing what is proper for a Council to do which is called on the business expressed in the vote.[111] It is objected that if diversity of religious sentiment, on account of the prejudices which it usually occasions in the case of those who differ disqualifies men from being judges in an ecclesiastical controversy in the case of those who differ from them, then it also disqualifies them from being judges in a civil controversy; & of course that the judges of our Courts in New England being chiefly congregational its would be improper judges in any cases where either parties was an episcopalian or anabaptist, or quaker & that the justices in England being all of the Church of England would be improper judges of dissenters.

Answer. If there were any stated judicatories in the nation but merely justices commissioned to be convoked for each cause by the election of the parties, & there were as many lawful justices on one side as on the other; it would obviously be most just for one party to be allowed to chose as many on his side as the other on his - especially if the controversy were of a religious nature, & the conduct to be judged of, had reference to that very question in which the judges differed from each other. For example, suppose the Test-Act were taken off in England & half of the justices were dissenters, & in all controversies the common law of the nation gave each party the right of choosing one half of his judges; & suppose that a controversy arises between an Episcopalian & a Dissenter concerning a Dissenter[^008-note264] & writing in defense of Dissenters & endeavouring to propagate Dissent & that the point to be judged of is the conduct of the two parties in the management of this controversy & the Episcopalian has chosen half of the judges who are of his side; would it not be [112] equitable that the Dissenter should choose the other half of his side: especially if a part of the business of the judges who are of his side was to act the part of mediators --. I readily admit that in deciding religious controversies, the judges actually employed have in fact point of fact been usually all on one side & the well known effect has been the grossest unrighteousness & the most violent persecution. This has been true in Romish & Lutheran Countries as well as in England & Scotland & almost every where as every one acknowledges.

Two or three of the objections touched upon at the former sitting of the Council deserve some additional remarks[^008-note265].

It was objected that, on this plan there can be no defense against error because some a minister can find some who will justify his opinions; as I am said to have asserted in my Remarks on the Springfield controversy.

To this I reply

1. In this objection the actual state of the present case is wholly forgotten; for, as has been often observed the Council will not meet either to justify, or condemn my opinion, whether it be truth or error.

2. What I assert in my remarks on that Controversy is merely this - that, if one party is allowed to choose all his own judges there can be no defence against error. And I say the same now; & it is very true, that if one party has all the judges on his side, there can be no defense for the other party, against error, or injustice or any thing else that is bad because such a Council must of course be partial. What I there assert therefore, instead of being inconsistent with [113] what I now urge, establishes the[^008-note266] grand point & overthrows the very position it is brought to prove viz. that all the council ought to be on one side.

3. All the force in the objection, if it has any, lies - not against my going out of the county for a choice, but against my having any choice at all, that is, against any elective Councils whatever. For if a minister has any advantage in his choice to defend himself in error it arises from his having liberty to choose half his judges & not from the place where they are chosen whether in or out of the County. The rule of confining him & the church to the County where they live will no more defend the truth than expose it. It will do one or the other just as the county is. If the churches of this county had happened to be of my side, this rule would have defended me in my error & would have disabled the Church from defending themselves just as much as going out of the County will now. Indeed the rule would more generally expose orthodox churches than defend them for there are more erroneous vicinities in the world world than orthodox ones.

It may be objected that by thus insisting on the liberty of going out of the county, I cast a reflexion on the neighboring ministers as though they had not honesty enough to do me justice. To this I answer that, if any individual is in circumstances, which powerfully tend to prejudice his mind in favor of, or against either of the parties in a given case, it is no reflexion on him to object against as improper to be employed as a judge in that case. Thus, if a man is nearly related to one of the parties; or where a town is a party & he is an inhabitant of that town; he cannot sit as judge or juror in such a case. But there is far more reason to object against all the judges being wholly on [114] one side to judge of the conduct of two parties in a religious controversy than to object against near relations; as will appear if we consider two things:

1. Men are as prone, in religious differences to favor their own party as they are[^008-note267] their near relations. This is true all the world over, in every age with both learned & unlearned, that men are friends to their own party, & commend their persons and justify their conduct. How widely different are the opinions of two opposite parties of the same points of conduct. Thus, how different are the opinions of Presbyterians from those of the Episcopalians concerning the conduct of the Presbyterians in the time of the Long Parliament; & so I might adduce innumerable other instances.

2. Difference in religious sentiments excites one kind of prejudice which nearness of relation does not; for though the latter prejudices us in favor of those who are related to us yet it does not prejudice us against those who are not related to us; whereas we are not only prejudiced in favor of those who agree with us in sentiment, but we are strongly prejudiced against those who differ from us. And the strongest prejudices ever known in the world have arisen from this cause. Hence the question with regard to the neighboring ministers & churches is not whether they are just & upright men, but whether these circumstances naturally tend to bias them in this controversy. If they do, then it is obviously against the plain rights of mankind to oblige me to be judged only by them. It was objected that to allow me to go out of the county will be a bad precedent, & greatly expose the peace & good order of the county in its ecclesiastical affairs. I desire this objection to be thoroughly examined; & we shall be able to determine whether it has any force by resolving these two questions[115]

1. Whether the rule to confine Councils to a vicinity, be so universal & unalterable, that it is absolutely without exceptions & ought never to be departed from in any case, ordinary or extraordinary; & if not; then, 2. Whether this case be so far an extraordinary as to require an exception from the general rule.

As to the first of these questions, I should affront this Rev. Council by suggesting that any one of them would affirm it; for it would contradict their own practice & that of all our churches; as well as the sentiments which they have advanced respecting the Consociations in Connecticut.

The only remaining question is Whether this case be so far extraordinary as to require an exception from the general rule. And if the Council will reflect on what has been said it will I think be plain to a demonstration that the most absolute[^008-note268] rules of justice do absolutely require it. We are two controverting parties, & we want a council because we want judges to judge between us; - & for what unless to do justice between us; - We want a balance to weigh both parties & is it not essential that the scales be even.[^008-note269] Why then oblige me to be weighed against the other party when their scale is so much the heaviest before we are put into the balance. It is the law of God, Thou shalt have a just balance; & his strict injunction, That which is altogether just shalt thou follow. The prudence & justice of this Council teaches them in other respects not to give one side an advantage above the other - particularly, not to hear one side in the absence of the other; —— particularly, not to hear the one side in the absence of the other, to have the same rules respecting evidence for both & to give each equal opportunity to plead his own cause. Now if justice requires that such an exact equality be maintained in circumstantials, how much more does it require that in a point so essential as the choice of the tribunal [116] which is to decide the main controversy, there be a perfect equality, & that neither party be allowed to have all the judges on his own side. How highly my people estimate the advantage of having all the judges on their own side is abundantly evident from their strenuous & persevering efforts to secure it; & if it be so great an advantage, why should we not be placed on the same footing. My people have ever pretended that I ought not to choose half of the Council; nor have we submitted the question to this Council whether this our case shall be referred to the whole County without any choice in the case. I say we have never[^008-note270] submitted this question because it was never once mentioned, & I suppose never entered into our hearts. I am sure it did not into mine. This point was never controverted between us; & it is absurd to suppose, that we summoned a Council of Churches[^008-note271] which we had never even once disputed, but had always been agreed in. That I should have a choice in the Council was fully admitted by the Church & the point debated between us was, whether I should go out of the County in my choice. But if I be allowed a choice as well as they, why should I not be allowed choose those who are on my side as well as the Church those who are on their side. To deny me this, is utterly inconsistent with[^008-note272] allowing me a choice, for the pretence is an equality of advantage or an equal chance for justice. To allow me an equal choice is an implicit acknowledgement that I am in justice entitled to an equal advantage; but to tie me up to judges who are of their opinion is grossly inconsistent with this acknowledgement. To tell me with a great show of fairness, you shall stand as good a chance for justice as we, you shall choose one half of the Council, & we will choose [117] the other; --- but then we will choose those who are on our side, & you also shall chose those who are on our side, & opposed to yourself on the main point of controversy is only only mocking and insulting me. This Council in their former result intimate, that a part of the business of the future council will be[^008-note273] to reconcile us; &, in the very nature the Council which comes with power to separate must have power to decide whether the separation shall be immediate or whether it is not their duty to act as mediators & attempt a reconciliation. And what is more obvious to common sense[^008-note274] that a mediator should be impartial one in whom both parties confide & have an equal interest. If it be lawful to compare little things with great ones, did not the most High himself, when he was pleased to appoint a mediator between God & man, take care that he should partake of both natures to qualify him for that office.

And besides the grounds already mentioned, requiring an exemption from the ordinary rules of proceeding this case is in other respects very[^008-note275] extraordinary, not merely in some of its circumstances but in its very nature; so much so that no such case I apprehend has occurred in New England or even in the christian church. The proposed council will convene to decide on a new question - a question which I suppose was never before submitted to an ecclesiastical tribunal. And certainly, in the decision of such a case, it cannot but be allowed, that a number of the senior ministers of the Country should be engaged.

The circumstances of this Country ought also to be considered [118] we having no appeal from one Council to another. Such a state of things makes a great alteration, as to what is reasonable in many particular cases, & requires some things to be allowed which need not be allowed, if here, as in Scotland, we could appeal from the Churches of the vicinity to those of the Province & ultimately to the assembly of the whole nation. It is a strange way of arguing that, because there ought to be a regular establishment in the country, we ought therefore to bind ministers & churches to the same measures as if there were one; & yet not allow them the same[^008-note276] privileges which an establishment secures, & which alone can render those measures tolerable. If we had a regular establishment, there would be here as in Scotland no elective Councils. There would have been none of late in Hatfield nor in this town in the case of Mr. Hawley. Yet, as we have no establishment, every one allows the necessity of such Councils in many cases; & the same point[^008-note277] involves us in the necessity of going out of the vicinity because we have not the right of appeal.

On the whole admitting it to be a good general rule that Councils should be selected from the neighborhood no reason can be assigned why this case should not be exempted, unless we take the broad ground that no exemptions from general rules shall ever be allowed, however just expedient & necessary in themselves for fear of a bad precedent. But was this broad ground ever taken, nor can it ever be observed in human society. No such uniform observance of general rules was ever known in our ecclesiastical concerns. It is a general rule that the alleged delinquency of a private brother ought first to be tried[^008-note278]; yet [119] the Church may refer it directly to a council as was lately done in Hatfield with the approbation of the churches & with no fear of a bad precedent. And with regard to this very point, to go from the vicinity in the choice of Councils, so far from being a new thing, has been customary, where the peculiar circumstances of the case required it, & not only in this province but even in Connecticut. The aggrieved party at Goshen, in their controversy with Mr. Heaton, passed by the consociation to which Goshen belonged[^008-note279] & summoned a council from the remote parts of Connecticut by the advice of Col. Williams of Wethersfield & of the Rev. Mr. Williams of Lebanon. Mr. Searl also neglecting the consociation with which Sharon is connected recommended by the advice of the best judges summoned a Council to settle him at that place. But what I desire is not to go from the vicinity in a neglect of churches of the vicinity, but to select half the Council from abroad first asking the leave & approbation of these churches.

As to the danger of this case being pleaded as a precedent for the same liberty in ordinary cases; the danger is less than perhaps in any other case because all the world regard this case as wholly new & extraordinary.

And it is a great mistake, that an exemption from a general rule, in a singular case which imperiously demands it, tends to weaken that general rule. The very contrary is true that to grant the exemption strengthens, & to refuse it weakens the rule; for the injustice which men suffer by an over exact observance of the rule is ascribed to[^008-note280] the rule itself & thus we are inclined to renounce it. The ecclesiastical constitution of Connecticut has of late been evidently weakened by thus overstraining the general rules & to refuse reasonable exemptions where plain justice requires them, will tend to deter the [120] country from ever coming into a regular establishment. If then the whole matter be duly weighed by the Rev.[^008-note281] Council, I cannot but think they will judge that no ill consequences will follow from granting me this equal liberty of choice, which I claim as a matter of obvious justice; and that there will be many good consequences as there will be opportunity to obtain a just & impartial Council by bringing the whole of the consistory to a proper balance, as we shall be able to employ a number of the senior ministers & gentlemen of chief note in the Country in determining this new & extraordinary affair, as there will be a proper consistory to attempt a reconciliation between the parties, & as the proceedings will be better stated abroad[^008-note282] more to the satisfaction of the world. I beseech the Rev.[^008-note283] Council not suddenly to pass over these things, but to weigh them with thorough deliberation. I trust to their justice that they will use great care & diligence that there will be no unequal & hard dealing as to the terms I am bound to, in the hearing & determining this affair of such vast importance.

[rest of page blank]

After the preceding remarks had been submitted by the [121] Pastor, the Committee of the Church & some of the private members of the church addressed the Council on the same subject; when after long consideration, the question being put, the members of the Council were equally divided upon it, & the following writing was drawn up by them &[^008-note284] sent to the Pastor & Church Committee[^008-note285]

Several of the members of the Council did then as christian friends advise that Pastor & People should in this affair condescend to each other, each departing in some degree from what they had insisted on; that the Pastor should no longer insist on going out of the County for half of the Council, & that the people should not insist on[^008-note286] his being confined wholly to the council County, but that they should consent that he should go out for a minor part, the major part of the Council being of the County. This seemed to be the concurring sentiment of the members of the Council, & this reason was given why it was not voted in Council that they did not look upon finding out[^008-note287] such an expedient for an accommodation. I then

I then manifested before the members of the Council my disappointment, in that the Council had never given their judgment Whether it was the duty of my people to hear the reasons of my opinion from the pulpit, although that matter had been particularly debated before them. Upon which one of them viz. Mr. Partridge replied that although the Council had said nothing expressly about that matter in their R yet there was something in their [122] Result, which was drawn up at the first sitting of the Council, which was supposed to be a sufficient intimation of the mind of the Council that my people ought to hear me viz. that passage wherein[^008-note288] the Church Committee opposing my delivering my principles from the pulpit, as one probable occasion of the great uneasiness and dissatisfaction which had arisen between the Pastor & people[^008-note289]. I then made a declaration before the members of the Council & also in the presence of the Committee of the Church to the following purpose

"I judge that there is a great prospect of our controversy issuing in a separation between Pastor & people; & on long & mature deliberation[^008-note290] I have determined that I cannot leave this people without first making trial whether my people will hear me give the reasons of my opinion from the pulpit, unless I am advised to the contrary by a Council; being fully persuaded on the best inquiry I can make that a very great part of the people have never read my book, nor have by any means been informed of my reasons, & are not likely to be informed in any other way than from the pulpit. However I am willing to refer this matter to a[^008-note291] Council as a case of conscience to be resolved by them whether I have a right to deliver my reasons from the Pulpit;[^008-note292] & if they determine that I have not, I can be easy to forbear."

On hearing this the Council at first talked of desiring to be by themselves to consider of this matter. But it was presently said by some of them that it was a matter which was indisputable that I had a right & a right which I ought not to put out of my hands, & ought not to leave the matter to any Council; & so they put by [123] the thought of entering into any any particular consideration of the subject. Upon which I declared that I judged that I had a right to preach on the subject on the Sabbath; but that I might do it in the manner[^008-note293] which would least offend. I would first make trial whether my people would hear me in Lectures appointed to that end, & that I proposed to have my next[^008-note294] Lecture the next Thursday, Feb. 15, at 2 oclock P.M.; & if I found that my people would not hear me on Lecture days, I would reserve liberty to myself to do it on the Sabbath. None of the members of the Council said any thing[^008-note295] against any part of this thing[^008-note296] which I had thus declared. The next Sabbath at the conclusion of the afternoon exercise, I informed the Congregation of this which passed before the members of the Council & the Committee of the Church viz. of this declaration which I then made & renewed my declaration of preaching Lectures & appointted the first Lecture to be the next Thursday.

The next[^008-note297] Monday, Feb. 12 the Precinct met according to adjournment and a vote was passed by a small majority[^008-note298] to come to me & desire me not to preach Lectures on the subject in controversy according to my declaration & appointment. They proceeded to choose Deacon Cooke, Deacon Pomeroy & Captn. John Lyman for this purpose & then adjourned themselves to the 1st Monday in March.

The Committee that was chosen came to me the same day at evening on their appointed errand. But after considerable discourse with them I informed them that I could not think that[^008-note299] I should be in the way of my duty without preaching my appointed Lectures to such as were willing to hear me.

Accordingly the next Thursday, Feb. 15, I preached my first [124] Lecture, which was thinly attended by my own people; but there were present a very great number of strangers - I suppose much than half the Congregation - which was partly owing to the fact that the County Court was then sitting. And I would observe by the way that the justices of the Court adjourned themselves to attend my Lecture; which greatly provoked the Clerk of the Court & occasioned his uttering himself very openly & publicly in some very harsh expression wherein he called me a tyrant one who Lorded it over God’s heritage, &c. The next saturday, Feb. 17th a little before night, Dea. Cook & Dea. Pomeroy came to me[^008-note300] & told me that it was the desire[^008-note301] of the brethren of the Church that I would stay the Church the next day to see if the Church would not call the ministers of this Association to advise them what course to take under their present difficulties circumstances. I objected that it was but a little more than a week since we had had a Council who were called on this very business to direct us how we should conduct ourselves for the present; & that there was nothing new or remarkable[^008-note302] in our circumstances, nothing but what was visible to the Council before they went away. And I told them that I was fixed in it, to have no hand in calling any more Councils in our affairs, unless I might have a choice with the people, & might choose some out of the County, & might have some in the Council favoring my own opinion, so that there might be somewhat of a balance in the Council: on which they went away.

On Monday, Feb. 19, Dea. Cook & some others went[^008-note303] to get subscriptions to a paper drawn up & directed to the Ministers of this Association setting forth to this purpose That I had been applied to for a Church meeting, to see if the Church would not call the Association together for their advice &c. &c. & that I refused, & that I refused &[^008-note304] at the same [125] time declared that I never would have any hand in calling any council in our affairs unless I might choose one half out of the County, as I had heretofore insisted & therefore desiring them to come together & give them advice. To this number they obtained a considerable number of subscribers, so far as I can learn about 50; & then carried it to the Rev. Mr. Williams of Hadley who undertook to send to the members of the Association, & accordingly did send to most of them. I saw two of the letters which he sent which were very much to the same purpose. The following is a copy of one of them: --

The Northampton people are desirous of having some minister preach on the opposite side of the controversy to Mr. Edwards. They have brought an application to the Association for advice & lodged it with & at their desire & at the advice of some of our brethren in the ministry I have written to the ministers to desire them to meet at my house on Thursday, early in the morning. I hope you will come for you cannot easily think what posture they[^008-note305] are in. And unless we do concert some measures we are in danger of being overrun; & Northampton will proceed to extreme measures being conducted by some gentlemen not over tender of ministers or churches; which may prove of pernicious consequence to us & all our churches. I have the promise of some gentlemen to come & hope you will not fail. From your brother, &c. Chester Williams Hadley Feb. 20 1750 Six of the ministers came together on this notification, viz. Mr. Partridge of East Hadley, Mr. Billings of Cold Spring, Mr. A. of Sunderland, Mr. Ashley of Deerfield, Mr. Woodbridge of [126] Hatfield, & Mr. Williams of Hadley. That day, being Thursday Feb. 22, I had my second lecture which was also thinly attended[^008-note306] by my own people, but by a great number of strangers as it appeared to me more than half the congregation being strangers. -

In the evening three of the forementioned ministers came to my house viz. Mr. Partridge, Mr. Billings, & Mr. Woodbridge; & showed me the paper which had been lodged with Mr. Williams directed to the Association subscribed by a number of the members of this church; & also showed me a vote which they had passed among themselves -- that they would proceed to give some advice to the people of Northampton as they desired. I informed them that there was a great misrepresentation in the paper sent to them representing me to have declared That I utterly refused to have any hand in calling a Council, unless I might choose half the members out of the County - whereas all that I said was - That I would have no hand in calling any Council unless I might choose some of the members out of the County, -- & that I had no thought of insisting on half when the Deacons were here; but all I had thoughts of was only choosing a minor part agreeably to the private advice of the members of the late Council. After much more conversation with them on our affairs, the next morning these gentlemen returned to their brethren at Hadley; & after long consideration they broke up & did nothing.

March 5, The Precinct met again according to adjournment. This being also the day of the town meeting for choosing town officers, the Precinct meeting was adjourned to the next day. The next day they met, & adjourned themselves untill[^008-note307] half past 4oclock P.M. That was the day of my 4th lecture on the on the subject in controversy, which being the last I intended [127] but one, & the public Fast being appointed on the thursday following, which would put by my last Lecture a week longer I sent the people word at this meeting that if they insisted on my calling a church meeting in order to my calling a decisive Council, before my last lecture was past, I would warn one the next Monday; though I chose if they were willing, that it should be deferred untill my last Lecture. Accordingly they consented that it should be so deferred; untill after my last Lecture & appointed Deacon Cook to come to me & desire me to call a Church meeting the week after my last Lecture, & then adjourned themselves to March 22 at half after[^008-note308] 4oclock the day of my last Lecture when they met & adjourned themselves to April 2 1750.[^008-note309]

On[^008-note310] the next sabbath March 26, the Church met, & at the request of some of the brethren, I desired that those who were of the same principles on which the Church had proceeded in their former practice in the admission of members to full Communion in the Church would manifest by holding up their hands; & it appeared that there was a great majority still of those principles.

We next proceeded to consider the business on which a Council should be called & I proposed the following draught[^008-note311] of a vote for consideration - "That a Council be called to give us their best advice for a remedy[^008-note312] arising from the present unbroken[^008-note313] state of the Church, by reason of the controversy here subsisting concerning the qualifications for full communion in the church. And if, upon the whole of what they see & find in our circumstances, they [128] judge it best that Pastor & people be immediately separated that they proceed to dissolve the relation between them."

There was much discourse concerning this draught[^008-note314]. It was read publicly & distinctly, three or four times, & it was desired that each particular passage of it might be considered & scanned.

It was offered to such as desired to view & examine it & handed from one to another. Some amendments were proposed but the amendments did not seemed to be liked as well as the first draught[^008-note315]. It was then put to vote & it was questioned whether it was a vote. Then it was desired that all might sit down & hold up their hands for some considerable time, & then it appeared plainly to be a vote, & was generally acknowledged to be so by such as had appeared most to oppose it & was not questioned by more than one or two. And one of them viz. Major Pomeroy said it was generally allowed to be a vote & therefore intimated it to be his mind that it should pass as such & that we should[^008-note316] proceed to other business.

Then I declared to the Church what I should insist on with respect to the Council that should be chosen viz. To choose half of the members of the Council; & to choose three ministers or churches without the bounds of the County, & should not insist on more than three unless the whole Council consisted of more than 14. fourteen. I also told them that I should insist on a provisional choice of some others out of the County to come in case any of the three first pitched upon should fail so that I might indeed have three from other parts of the land.

It was then strongly insisted on by the Church that I that I ought not to allowed to choose any out of the County; [129] so that the consideration of the number to be chosen out of the County was for present laid aside. And the matter debated was, Whether I should be allowed to bring any at all from without the bounds of the County. After much talk it was put to vote in the following words: Whether or not you do consent that in choosing the Council now under consideration, I, in my choice should go out of this County for any part of the Council? Let those who do consent, manifest it by holding up their hands." --- It was not voted. -- Upon which I told the Church that the business of the meeting was come to an end; as it was apparent that Pastor & people could act in nothing together in calling a Council. After some discourse I told them that I stood ready to yield to have but two of the Council from abroad unless the Council consisted of more than ten; but should insist on three if the whole was more than ten & on more if the Council was above fourteen. It being moved by some of the brethren that the meeting should be adjourned[^008-note317] for further consideration; It was accordingly adjourned to the next day at one oclock.

Accordingly the next day we met again, & the last subject of consideration concerning my going out of the County for any part of the Council was proposed again; &, supposing they[^008-note318] now had sufficient consideration of the matter I was about to put it to vote again. But then it was desired by some that there might be a reconsideration of of the first vote which[^008-note319] had passed the day before concerning the business of the Council, & suggested that if some alteration were made in that vote there was a probability that the latter vote concerning my going [130] out of the County would pass without difficulty. Then Major Pomeroy declared that a number of brethren had met together & drawn up something determining the business of the Council: which draught he read which was to this purpose viz. —— That the Council should come & propose terms of accommodation between Pastor & People; to which, if the people did not consent, the Council should proceed immediately to dissolve the relation between Pastor & people." I objected much against setting aside what had been already voted to make way for any new projections as only tending to open a door for new contests & difficulties & greatly to entangle & lengthen out our affairs & also because there was nothing in what had been already voted but what was essential, what the nature of things & the state of our affairs did necessarily require, & therefore what I must insist upon. And I particularly[^008-note320] to what Major Pomeroy proposed as limiting the Council without referring any thing to their judgment or advice, & not so much a suffering them to act their own judgment in determining whether it was best immediately to separate between Pastor & People. The same things were strongly objected by some others; & on the whole I manifested that I should wholly decline putting it to vote; on which, after much earnest talk I put the other matter to vote viz. whether I should be allowed to go out of the County for any members of the Council -- which again passed in the negative. Upon this I informed the church that I was not against their having farther time for consideration if they desired it, & farther conference with them or with [131] a committee they might choose; but it would not be worth the while to make any farther attempt to act in concert if they were fixed & resolved in these two things --- Not to leave it to the discretion of the Council whether to separate; & not to allow me to choose any members of the Council out of the County. - Some of them declared they were fixed in these things. Upon which I asked, if any had any thing to object against my dissolving the meeting seeing we were come at present to a stop as to acting in any thing together; & told them withal that, if afterwards on further consideration a church meeting was desired to reconsider these matters, I would not refuse warning one. On this occasion there was much earnest talk about the power of the Church to act without me & to call a Council themselves.

Finally some of the brethren thought that I had best dissolve the meeting & accordingly I dissolved it; & immediately after which, as the people began to move in order to go out of the meeting house, some individuals called out very earnestly for them to stay, & proceed to act without me & see if they could not have Maj. Pomeroy’s draught (aforementioned) put to vote. I then came away; & the people that stayed behind differing among themselves gradually dispersed & did nothing.

The next Friday, March 30 1750, I sent to Deacon Cook the following declaration.[^008-note321]

The Monday following, being April 2, the Precinct met according to adjournment. It was a very thin meeting consisting of about 44 persons. It was proposed by some that the Precinct should send a messenger to the Association [132] of the lower part of the County who were to sit Springfield Mountains, for their advice. Whether they had best to consent to going out of the County or not ? & it was put to vote whether they should send a messenger, but they were not able to determine whether it was a vote or not untill they divided when it appeared to be a vote[^008-note322] by a majority of 26 against 18. Accordingly Major Pomeroy, Senior & Mr. Joseph Hawley were appointted a Committee to write to the Association in behalf of the Precinct; & Josiah Pomeroy was appointed the Messenger to carry the letter & bring the return. The meeting was then adjourned untill Tuesday April 10, Accordingly this Committee wrote & sent to the same[^008-note323] Association. On this occasion, the day before the Association met, I sent the following letter to Mr. Hopkins of Springfield.[^008-note324]

The Association on[^008-note325] account of the message sent to them & my letter to Mr. Hopkins, sent a letter to me & another to the Committee which are as follows.[^008-note326]

Tuesday April 10th, the Precinct met again according to adjournment. It was a thin meeting of about 41 members. The moderator read my declaration aforementioned sent to Deacon Cook & also the letters of the Lower Association to me & to the Committee; & after some discourse it was voted to desire me to call a church meeting the next Monday, in order to some farther attempts for an agreement between Pastor & people on some issues for bringing these affairs to an issue, or that purpose. Then the meeting was [133] adjourned to the next Tuesday, April 17th.

Accordingly the next sabbath, April 15th, I warned a meeting of the church for the next day at 3 oclock P.M. Agreeably to this warning the church met on Monday April 16. After the meeting was opened by prayer Major Pomeroy read the letter which the committee of the Precinct had received from the Lower Association of this County - After this ensued some new disputes concerning the reasonableness of my being allowed to go out of the Committee[^008-note327] for some of the Council which should have power to issue the our controversy. - I also read the forementioned Declaration which I had sent to Deacon of March 30th. —— I then told the Church, if they had any thing to propose concerning another previous Council, agreeably to the advice they had received, I stood ready to to consider of it, & told them, if there appeared a disposition to call another previous Council to determine -- Whether it was not reasonable that I should be allowed to go out of the County in the choice of a Decisive Council; -- I would take the matter into consideration & would ask the advice of my friends. But from what was said there did not appear to be any prevailing inclination to it, but the contrary. Then it was put to vote again -- Whether they were willing that I should go out of the County for any members of the Council which should have power to issue our controversy: -- And the church dividing upon it there were about 109 against it & 56 for it.

After this I put it vote whether the Church were willing I should go out of the County that another previous Council should be called, in some way mutually chosen in order to determine whether I might be allowed to go out of the County for some members of a decisive Council, & and whether in that case they did consent that I should [134] take time to consider of this matter & ask advice of my friends ? -- & I saw but 3 or 4 hands up[^008-note328] for it. So the meeting was dismissed.

The next April 17th I went a journey down the country. The same day after I was gone the Precinct met again according to adjournment when was read the following Letter from the Rev. Mr. Clark of Salem Village, directed to Major Pomeroy to be communicated to the Precinct.[^008-note329] The former vote respecting the business of the Council, which passed at the Church meeting March 26 was read. Then I proposed that a vote should be put again respecting my going out of the County for two ministers or churches of the Council; & it was insisted that there should be a saving clause added intimating that it was not proposed that this vote should be used as a precedent for the future. Then I put the vote to the brethren thus, Do you consent that in choosing the Council to transact the forementioned business I in my choice should choose two ministers or churches without the bounds of this County not intending that this shall hereafter have the force of a precedent It passed in the Affirmative -- Then I desired that I might make a provisional choice of two more who might be applied to in case those I might first choose should fail, or could not be obtained. This also was voted.

Then it was proposed whether the Council should be a Council of Ministers or Churches; & it was determined by a vote that it should be a Council of Churches. And then with respect to the number of Churches of which the Council should consist; it was voted that it should consist of ten churches.

Then we proceeded to a nomination & choice of particular ministers & churches of which the Council should consist, I [135] first proposed Mr. Billings & the church of Cold Spring which were voted.

Then it was moved that a committee of the brethren should be chosen to go aside to consider whom to nominate to the Church to be chosen on their part. Accordingly a Committee was chosen viz. Major Pomeroy, Mr. Joseph Hawley, Lieut. Pomeroy Wright, Dea. Pomeroy, & Dea. Cook; & after they returned we went on with the choice. On the whole, of those whom I nominated were chosen the following Ministers with their Churches:

Within the County Rev. Mr. Reynolds of Enfield Rev. Mr. Billings of Cold Spring with their churches; Rev. Mr. Abercrombie of Pelham and provisionally in either case either of these should fail, the Rev. Mr. Woodbridge of South Hadley and his church

Without the County Rev. Mr. Foxcroft of Boston Rev. Mr. Parkman of Westborough and provisionally, in case of failure of these, Rev. Mr. Wigglesworth of Ipswich Hamlet Rev. Mr. Hobby of Reading with their Churches By the nomination of the Committee, were chosen on the part of the church Rev. Mr. Woodbridge of Hatfield, Rev. Mr. Breck of Springfield, Rev. Mr. Hubbard of Sheffield, with their churches; Rev. Mr. Williams of Hadley, Rev. Mr. Ashley of Sunderland, And for a reserve in case of failure of either of these Rev. Mr. Williams of Long Meadow, with their churches;[136] Rev. Mr. Leavitt of Somers, Then it was voted that the day for the opening of the Council should be the 19th of June next. Then the brethren proceeded to choose a Committee to be their agents to represent them & manage their cause before the Council; & the persons chosen were Maj. Pomeroy, Lieut. Wright, & Mr. Joseph Hawley. Then the church meeting was dismissed, & the Precinct meeting was opened, who determined to defray the charge of entertaining the Council; & desired the Committee of the church to procure some person, either a minister or a layman to act as an advocate for the brethren & plead their cause before the Council.

[^008-note112]: DPV inserts (p. 349): "and of course that that Council should be an impartial Council. The removal of a minister from his people ordinarily lays him under great disadvantages, and commonly hurts his reputation though indeed he be not to blame. There is left on the minds of the world some suspicion, whether something or other blame-worthy or unhappy in him, his temper, or conduct, was not the cause. People therefore are generally not so willing to employ such removed ministers. There is commonly a great deal said against them; and how much of it is true and just, and how much unjust and false, the world do not know, and do not think themselves obliged to be at the trouble of enquiring; but rather think it their province to have nothing to do with them. Hence, as I think I have been innocent, and conscientious, and fair and faithful, with my people in this affair, according to the best light which I enjoy; so it concerns me greatly, that I should have full justice done me, in the result of the Final Council."

I have sought in vain for a copy of the Result of this Advisory Council; and have been able to ascertain, only, that they recommended, That there should be a restoration of the Sacrament of the Lord’s Supper; That Pastor and People should converse freely together, about the point in controversy; That there should be no public proceedings of any kind whatever, relative to the point in controversy; and that they also expressed the opinion, That the Church Committee opposing Mr. Edwards’ delivering his principles from the pulpit, was one probable occasion of the great uneasiness, and dissatisfaction, which had arisen between the pastor and the people." [N.B. The Result is in the Beinecke Coll.]

## "Preface to Farewell Sermon" (Draft Fragments) Farewell Serm. Pref. frags

Jonathan EdwardsTwo fragments of Preface to *Farewell Sermon*, 1750. BRBL, f. 1244.

[^009-note1]

The Council which sat here in June 1750. having heard that I had made certain draughts of the Covenant or Forms of a publick Profession of Religion which I stood ready to accept of from the Candidates for Church Communion They for their further Information sent for Them Accordingly I sent them four distinct draughts or Forms which I had drawn up about a twelve-month before as what I Stood ready to accept of (any one of Them) rather than contend and break with my People.

The two shortest of these Forms are here inserted for the Satisfaction of the Reader They are as follows "I hope I do truly find a Heart to give up my self wholly to God according to the Tenour of that Covenant of Grace which was Seal'd in my Baptism and to walk in a way of that Obedience to all the Commandments of God which the Covenant of Grace requires as long as I live"

Another

"I hope I truly find in my Heart a willingness to comply with all the Commandments of God which require me to give up my self wholly to Him & serve Him with my Body & my spirit and do accordingly now promise to walk in a way of Obedience to all the Commandments of God as long as I live"

such Kind of Professions as these I stood ready to accept rather than contend and break with my People not but that I think it much more convenient that ordinarily the publick Profession that is made by Christians should be much fuller & more particular and that (as I hinted in my Letter to Mr. Clark) I should not chuse to be tied up to any certain Form of words but to have Liberty to vary the Expressions of a publick Profession the more exactly to suit the sentiments & Experience rience of the Professours that it might be a meas more [^009-note2] just & free Expression of what each one finds in his Heart

And moreover it must be noted that I ever insisted on it that it belonged to me as a Pastor before a Profession was accepted to have full Liberty to instruct the Candidate in the Meaning of the Terms of it and in the Nature of the Things proposed to be professed And to enquire into his doctrinal understanding of these Things according to my best discretion and to Caution the Person as I should think needful against Rashness in making such a Profession or doing meerly mainly for the Credit of Himself or his Family or from any secular views whatsoever and to put Him on serious self-examination and searching his own Heart and Prayer to God to search and enlighten Him that He mayn't be hypocritical & deceived in the Profession He makes withal pointing forth to Him the many ways in which Professours are liable to be deceived

Nor do I think it improper for a Minister in such a Case to enquire and know of the Candidate what can be remembred of the Circumstances of his Christian Experience as this may tend much to illustrate his Profession and give a Minister great Advantage for proper Instructions Tho' a particular Knowledge and Remembrance of the Time & method of his first Conversion to God is not to be made the Test of a Persons sincerity nor insisted on as necessary in order to his being received into full Charity Not that I think it at all improper or unprofitable that in some special Cases a declaration of the Particular Circumstances of a Persons first Awakening and the Manner of his Convictions Illuminations & Comforts should be publickly lickly exhibited before the whole Congregation on [^009-note3] occasion of his Admission into the Church tho' this be not demanded as necessary to Admission. I ever declared against insisting on a Relation of Experiences in this sense (viz a Relation of the particular Time and steps of the operation of the spirit in first Conversion) as the Terms of Communion Yet if by a Relation of Experiences be meant a declaration of Experience of the great Things wrought wherein true Grace and the essential Acts & Habits of Holiness consist in this sense I think an Account of Persons Experiences necessary in order to his Admission into full Communion in the Church But that in whatever Enquiries are made and whatever Account is given neither minister nor Church are to set up themselves as searchers of Hearts but are to accept the serious solemn Profession of the well-instructed Professours of a good Life as best able to determine what He finds in his own Heart.

[^009-note4]

## "Notes on the Remonstrance of a Committee of the Northampton Church against the Proceedings of the Council, May 1751" Notes for 1751 Remonstrance

Jonathan EdwardsNotes on the Remonstrance of a Committee of the Northampton Church against the Proceedings of the Council, May, 1751

This are a portion of the notes Edwards used to draft "Vindication from the Church's Accusations" (BRBL, f. 1265), relating to the effort by a Northampton minority to have Edwards settled over a second church. A council took place on May 16, 1751, at which Edwards rejected the idea, but the meetings nonetheless generated a series of acrimonious "accusations" from the church. Edwards's references in the notes (e.g. "7. bottom," "8. top") are probably to locations in the church's remonstrance, no longer extant.

*********

MS fragment at Firestone Library, Princeton University, AM14458. A flawed transcription is in Howard C. Rice, Jr., "Jonathan Edwards at Princeton," *Princeton University Library Chronicle* 15-16 (1953-55), 78-79.

[^010-note1]

[^010-note3]

7. bottom. [I] declared in my farewell that I had not the pastoral care. Providence had discharged me.

8. top. I must resign 'em.

8. top. not only *de jure* but *de facto* pastor. This deny.

I might find it out afterward. Essential defect. Might not admit to the irregularity.

8. top. Insinuated that I don't seek the peace. That I would be settled over a few to the destruction of the whole.

8. middle. To procure a council in order to install me.

8. Readiness to settle in the place. I should have gone if it had not been for this.

8. About so long before as he knew. I knew nothing about it. I had no respect to it.

8. bottom. As if I had already foregone the opportunity of settling at Stockbridge and had determined to settle here.

9. I declare still I have no inclination, they retaining their settled aversion. It [was] not such a council as I insisted on. Not but that [they are] greatly to blame in having such a settled aversion. See their duty to dismiss aversion and return.

9. Militated *totis viribus*.[^010-note4] It was in defense of others and at their desire.

9-10. Saying nothing against a separation. I was persuaded it would turn to my reproach. Continued complaints of the separation. I manifested before the method which I thought ought to be taken.

I never manifested any inclination on any inclination but on the view of the settled aversion. But might think great injustice in the manner of it.

10. Engaged to stay at Northampton as a minister.

## "Vindication from the Church's Accusation" Vind. from Chh's Accusation

Jonathan Edwards"Vindication from the Church's Accusation," 1751

Following the council of May 16, 1751, the Northampton church leaders accused Edwards of deliberately trying to upset their efforts to secure a new pastor, and cast reproach upon his character and motives for calling the council. Edwards drew up this document, never published, in response. For a draft, see "Notes on the Remonstrance of a Committee of the Northampton Church against the Proceedings of the Council, May, 1751" (Princeton). On the circumstances surrounding this composition, see Dwight, 1:453.

*****

BRBL, f. 1265. Three folio leaves plus two fragments. The first fragment is composed of a scrap measuring 2" high by 3" wide, on which Edwards wrote the long title of the draft, pinned to another fragment of 7" high by 3" wide; the second fragment measures 5 3/4" high by 3" wide. Together, these two fragments constitute the first of 10 columns or sections, so numbered by Edwards. The three folio leaves each measure 12" high by 7" wide, are divided front and back into two columns with red lines, and are salvaged from a bound manuscript apparently dating from the 17th century and containing scattered hand-written entries by an unknown author on various topics, possibly scripture commentary. Edwards turned these pages upside-down and wrote in the blank spaces. The only writing by Edwards on the verso of the last sheet is the title, "Vindication from the Chh's Accusation."

1
